Theory of beam echoes
Tanaji Sen, FNAL, Batavia, IL 60510

Abstract
We develop the theory of beam echoes in circular accelerators under several different conditions. We derive detailed expressions for the echo amplitude and pulse width with nonlinear quadrupole and dipole kicks, first without and then with momentum spread. We use the theory with the linearized dipole and quadrupole kicks to solve the diffusion equation for different dependencies of the diffusion coefficient on the action. We then consider the use of multiple quadrupole kicks to increase the maximum echo amplitude. We have extended these calculations partially to the 2D case and we also have partial results for longitudinal echoes.



1
Introduction

The concept of beam echoes was introduced by Stupakov
[
1
]
and was first measured in the
longitudinal plane at the Fermilab Accumulator
[
3
]
. Since then it has been observed at other
accelerators. We list references on echoes from [5] - [12] in reverse chronological order.

The lecture notes by Alex Chao
[
4
]
inspired me to write these notes in the same spirit.
These notes are written mainly for the benefits of researchers and students. The other purpose is to serve as
reference material for journal articles.

The contents in this paper are arranged in roughly in the order in which the topics were worked on.
A word of caution: be aware of typographical errors especially in the equations, of which there may be quite a few.

2
Influence of a dipole kick

2.1
Dipole moment

Here we consider the general case where the dipole kicker is at a phase advance
\Delta\mu
from the BPM location where the centroid is
located. The changes
(\Delta x,\Delta x^{\prime})
in position and slope at the BPM are related to the changes
(0,\theta)
at the kicker
via the transfer matrix between the two locations

\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\Delta x\\
\Delta x^{\prime}\end{array}\right]=\left(\begin{array}[]{c c}M_{11}&M_{12}\\
M_{21}&M_{22}\end{array}\right)\left[\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
\theta\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}M_{12}\theta\\
\ M_{22}\theta\end{array}\right]

(2.1)

The transfer matrix elements from the kicker to the BPM are

M_{12}=\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\sin\Delta\mu,\;\;\;M_{22}=\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{K}}{\beta}}(\cos\Delta\mu-\alpha\sin\Delta\mu)

where
\beta_{K}
is the beta function at the kicker and
(\beta,\alpha)
are the values at the BPM. In terms of the conjugate variables
x,p

defined by
p=\beta x^{\prime}+\alpha x
, the changes at the BPM location are

\displaystyle\Delta x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\sin\Delta\mu

\displaystyle\Delta p

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\Delta x^{\prime}+\alpha\Delta x=\beta\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{K}}{\beta}}(\cos\Delta\mu-\alpha\sin\Delta\mu)\theta+\alpha\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\sin\Delta\mu\theta=\theta\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\cos\Delta\mu

In terms of these variables, the dipole kick, the action
J=(x^{2}+p^{2})/(2\beta)
changes to

J_{1}=\frac{1}{2\beta}[(x-\Delta x)^{2}+(p-\Delta p)^{2}]=J-\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{K}}{\beta}}\theta(\sin\Delta\mu x+\cos\Delta\mu p)+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}

If the density distribution before the kick was
\psi_{0}(J)
, after the kick it is
\psi_{1}(J_{1})
.
We use the transformation to action angle variables
J,\phi

x=\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi,\;\;\;\;p=-\sqrt{2\beta J}\sin\phi

to obtain immediately after the kick

\psi_{1}(J,\phi)=\psi_{0}(J+\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J}\theta\sin(\phi-\Delta\mu)+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2})

At time
t
after the kick, the distribution evolves to

\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)=\psi_{1}(J,\phi-\omega(J)t)

The dipole moment at time
t
is

\langle x\rangle(t)=\int dJ\int d\phi\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)

Taking the initial distribution to be

\psi_{0}(J)=\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}]

(2.2)

where
J_{0}=\epsilon
is equal to the initial emittance.
Hence

\langle x\rangle(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}]\int dJ\sqrt{2\beta J}\int d\phi\exp[-\frac{1}{J_{0}}(J+\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J}\theta\sin(\phi-\omega(J)t-\Delta\mu))]

(2.3)

The
\phi
integration is done using

\int d\phi\cos\phi\exp[-a\sin(\phi-b)]=2\pi I_{1}(a)\sin b

To to the
J
integration we assume a linear dependence on
J

\omega(J)=\omega_{0}+\omega^{\prime}J

where
\omega^{\prime}
is a constant. Hence

\langle x\rangle(t)=\frac{1}{J_{0}}\exp[\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}]\int dJ\sqrt{2\beta J}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}]I_{1}(\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J}\theta)\sin(\omega_{0}t+\omega^{\prime}Jt+\Delta\mu))]

Changing variable to
a=\sqrt{2\beta J}
, we have

\langle x\rangle(t)=\frac{1}{\beta J_{0}}\exp[\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}]\int da\;a^{2}I_{1}(\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{K}}{\beta}}\frac{\theta}{J_{0}}a)\exp[-\frac{a^{2}}{2\beta J_{0}}]{\rm Im}[\exp[i(\omega_{0}t+\Delta\mu)]\exp[i\frac{\omega^{\prime}t}{2\beta}a^{2}]]

Using

\int_{0}^{\infty}da\;a^{2}\exp[-Aa^{2}]I_{1}(Ba)=\frac{B}{4A^{2}}\exp[\frac{B^{2}}{4A}]

Substituting

A=\frac{1}{2\beta J_{0}}(1-i\omega^{\prime}J_{0}t)=\frac{1}{2\beta J_{0}}(1-i\Theta),B=\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{K}}{\beta}}\frac{\theta}{J_{0}},\;\;\Theta=\omega^{\prime}J_{0}t

we have

\frac{B}{4A^{2}}=\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{K}}{\beta}}\frac{\beta^{2}\theta J_{0}}{(1-i\Theta)^{2}},\;\;\;\frac{B^{2}}{4A}=\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}\frac{i\Theta}{(1-i\Theta)}

Hence

\langle x\rangle(t)=\theta\sqrt{\beta_{K}\beta}{\rm Im}\left[\frac{e^{i(\omega_{0}t+\Delta\mu)}}{(1-i\Theta)^{2}}\exp[\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}\frac{i\Theta}{(1-i\Theta)}]\right]

Let

\eta=\omega_{0}t+\Delta\mu+\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}\frac{\Theta}{(1+\Theta^{2})}

(2.4)

We have to evaluate

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\left[\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}\frac{\Theta^{2}}{1+\Theta^{2}}\frac{\exp[i\eta]}{(1-i\Theta)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}\frac{\Theta^{2}}{1+\Theta^{2}}]\frac{1}{(1+\Theta^{2})^{2}}{\rm Im}\left[(1+2i\Theta-\Theta^{2})e^{i\eta}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}\frac{\Theta^{2}}{1+\Theta^{2}}]\frac{1}{(1+\Theta^{2})^{2}}\left[(1-\Theta^{2})\sin\eta+2\Theta\cos\eta\right]

Let

\sin\nu-\frac{2\Theta}{(1+\Theta^{2})},\;\;\Rightarrow\cos\nu=\sqrt{1-\sin^{2}\nu}=\frac{1-\Theta^{2}}{1+\Theta^{2}}

Hence

\left[\frac{(1-\Theta^{2})}{(1+\Theta^{2})}\sin\eta+\frac{2\Theta}{(1+\Theta^{2})}\cos\eta\right]=\sin(\eta+\nu)

where

\tan\nu=\frac{2\Theta}{1-\Theta^{2}}

Hence

\langle x\rangle(t)=\frac{\theta\sqrt{\beta_{K}\beta}}{(1+\Theta^{2})}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}\frac{\Theta^{2}}{1+\Theta^{2}}]\sin(\eta+\nu)

(2.5)

The amplitude of the dipole kick is

\langle x\rangle^{amp}(t)=\frac{\theta\sqrt{\beta_{K}\beta}}{(1+\Theta^{2})}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}\frac{\Theta^{2}}{1+\Theta^{2}}]

(2.6)

This is independent of the phase advance
\Delta\mu
from the kicker to the BPM.

If the dipole kicker and BPM are at the same location,
\beta_{K}=\beta
and
\Delta\mu=0
.

2.2
Emittance Growth

Here we consider the time evolution of the second order moments.

At time
t
following the dipole kick, the distribution is

\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)=\psi_{0}(J+\theta\sqrt{2\beta J}\sin(\phi-\omega(J)t)+\frac{1}{2}\beta\theta^{2})

(2.7)

Hence

\langle x^{2}\rangle=\int_{0}^{\infty}dJ\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi 2\beta J\cos^{2}\phi\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)

(2.8)

With
\psi_{0}=(1/2\pi J_{0})\exp[-J/J_{0}]
, introducing
z=J/J_{0}
, then

\psi_{2}(z,\phi,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-z]\exp[-\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{k}J_{0}z}\sin(\phi-\omega(z)t)]\exp[-\frac{\beta\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]

and

\langle x^{2}\rangle=\frac{\beta J_{0}}{2\pi}\exp[-\frac{\beta\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]\int dzd\phi\;z\exp[-(z+\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{k}J_{0}z}\sin(\phi-\omega(z)t))](1+\cos 2\phi)

(2.9)

There are two integrations over
\phi
for which we use the integration results

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\phi}d\phi\;\exp[-a\sin(\phi+b)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi I_{0}(a)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\phi}d\phi\;\exp[-a\sin(\phi+b)]\cos 2\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\pi I_{2}(a)\cos 2b

Hence

\langle x^{2}\rangle=\beta J_{0}\exp[-\frac{\beta\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]\int dz\;z\exp[-z]\left(I_{0}(\theta\sqrt{\frac{2\beta_{K}z}{2J_{0}}})-I_{2}(\theta\sqrt{\frac{2\beta_{K}z}{2J_{0}}})\cos 2\omega(z)t\right)

(2.10)

Writing
\cos 2\omega(z)t=\cos 2[\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J_{0}z]t={\rm Re}[e^{i2\omega_{\beta}t}e^{i2\omega^{\prime}J_{0}tz}]
, we use these results to do the
integration over
z
,

\displaystyle\int dz\;\;z\exp[-z]I_{0}(a\sqrt{z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\frac{1}{4}a^{2})\exp[\frac{a^{2}}{4}]

\displaystyle\int dz\;\;z\exp[-bz]I_{2}(a\sqrt{z})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a^{2}}{4b^{3}}\exp[\frac{a^{2}}{4b}]

Here
a=\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}/J_{0}}
and
b=1-i2\omega^{\prime}J_{0}t
. Define the dimensionless variables

\Theta_{2}=2\omega^{\prime}J_{0}t,\;\;\;a_{K}=\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}},\;\;\;\Psi_{2}=2\omega_{\beta}t+\frac{a_{K}}{1+\Theta_{2}^{2}}

(2.11)

We have

\langle x^{2}\rangle=\beta J_{0}\exp[-a_{K}]\left[(1+a_{K})\exp[a_{K}]-a_{K}{\rm Re}\left(\frac{e^{i\omega_{\beta}t}}{(1-i\Theta_{2})^{3}}\exp[\frac{a_{K}}{1-i\Theta_{2}}]\right)\right]

We evaluate the second term separately

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left(\frac{e^{i\omega_{\beta}t}}{(1-i\Theta_{2})^{3}}\exp[\frac{a_{K}}{1-i\Theta_{2}}]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}}{\rm Re}\left[(1+i\Theta_{2})^{3}\exp[i(\omega_{\beta}t+\frac{a_{K}(1+i\Theta_{2})}{1+\Theta_{2}^{2}})]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}}\exp[\frac{a_{K}}{1+\Theta_{2}^{2}}]{\rm Re}\left[(1+i\Theta_{2})^{3}\exp[i\Psi_{2}]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}}\exp[\frac{a_{K}}{1+\Theta_{2}^{2}}]\left[(1-3\Theta_{2}^{2})\cos\Psi_{2}-(3\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{2}^{3})\sin\Psi_{2}\right]

Using
(1-3\Theta_{2}^{2})^{2}+(3\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{2}^{3})^{2}=(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}
, we cam write

(1-3\Theta_{2}^{2})\cos\Psi_{2}-(3\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{2}^{3})\sin\Psi_{2}=(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3/2}\cos(\Psi_{2}+\nu)

where

\cos\nu=\frac{(1-3\Theta_{2}^{2})}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3/2}},\;\;\;\sin\nu=\frac{3\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{2}^{3}}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3/2}}\;\;\;\tan\nu=\frac{3\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{2}^{3}}{1-3\Theta_{2}^{2}}

Let
\alpha=\tan\Theta_{2}
, then

\tan\nu=\frac{3\tan\alpha-\tan^{3}\alpha}{1-3\tan^{2}\alpha}=\tan 3\alpha,\;\;\;\nu=3\alpha=3\tan^{-1}\Theta_{2}

Hence

\displaystyle\langle x^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta J_{0}\exp[-a_{K}]\left[(1+a_{K})\exp[a_{K}]-a_{K}\frac{1}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3/2}}\exp[\frac{a_{K}}{1+\Theta_{2}^{2}}]\cos(\Psi_{2}+3\tan^{-1}\Theta_{2})\right]

(2.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta J_{0}\left[(1+a_{K})-\frac{a_{K}}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3/2}}\exp[-\frac{a_{K}\Theta_{2}^{2}}{1+\Theta_{2}^{2}}]\cos(\Psi_{2}+3\tan^{-1}\Theta_{2})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta J_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\beta\beta_{K}\theta^{2}-\frac{\beta\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3/2}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}\Theta_{2}^{2}}{2J_{0}(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})}]\cos(\Psi_{2}+3\tan^{-1}\Theta_{2})

Define

\displaystyle\Sigma_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta J_{0},\;\;\;\Delta\Sigma=\frac{1}{2}\beta\beta_{K}\theta^{2},

(2.13)

\displaystyle A_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3/2}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}\Theta_{2}^{2}}{2J_{0}(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})}]

(2.14)

Then

\langle x^{2}\rangle=\Sigma_{0}+\Delta\Sigma-A_{2}\cos(\Psi_{2}+3\tan^{-1}\Theta_{2})

(2.15)

The first term corresponds to the initial emittance emittance, the remaining terms represent the change. At long times, the last term will decay exponentially, so asymptotically at long times, the change is

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\Delta\langle x^{2}\rangle=\frac{1}{2}\beta\beta_{K}\theta^{2}

The rms emittance is found from

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\langle x^{2}\rangle\langle(x^{\prime})^{2}\rangle-(\langle xx^{\prime}\rangle)^{2}]^{1/2}

(2.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}[\langle x^{2}\rangle\langle(p-\alpha x)^{2}\rangle-(\langle x(p-\alpha x)\rangle)^{2}]^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\beta}[\langle x^{2}\rangle\langle p^{2}\rangle-(\langle xp\rangle)^{2}]^{1/2}

We have

\displaystyle\langle p^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\beta\int_{0}^{\infty}dJ\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi J\sin^{2}\phi\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)

(2.17)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Sigma_{0}+\Delta\Sigma+A_{2}\cos(\Psi_{2}+3\tan^{-1}\Theta_{2})

while

\displaystyle\langle xp\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\beta\int_{0}^{\infty}dJ\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\phi J\sin\phi\cos\phi\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)

(2.18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{2}\sin(\Psi_{2}+3\tan^{-1}\Theta_{2})

To arrive at this result, we use the integration

\int_{0}^{2\phi}d\phi\;\exp[-a\sin(\phi+b)]\sin 2\phi=2\pi I_{2}(a)\sin 2b

Hence

\langle xp\rangle=\beta J_{0}a_{K}{\rm IP}\left(\frac{e^{i\omega_{\beta}t}}{(1-i\Theta_{2})^{3}}\exp[\frac{a_{K}}{1-i\Theta_{2}}]\right)

Since we now take the imaginary part, the cosine function in
\langle x^{2}\rangle
is replaced by the sine function.

Hence the time dependent rms emittance is

\epsilon=\frac{1}{\beta}[(\Sigma_{0}+\Delta\Sigma)^{2}-A_{2}(t)^{2}]^{1/2}

(2.19)

At large times we expect
A_{2}\rightarrow 0
, hence

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\epsilon=\frac{1}{\beta}(\Sigma_{0}+\Delta\Sigma)=J_{0}+\frac{1}{2}\beta\theta^{2}

(2.20)

3
Evolution of the tune spread after dipole kick

The rms tune spread
\langle\sigma(\nu)\rangle
is given by

\sigma(\nu)=[\langle(\Delta\nu)^{2}\rangle-(\langle\Delta\nu\rangle)^{2}]^{1/2}

(3.1)

Assuming as above that the tune spread as a function of
J
is
\Delta\nu(J)=\nu^{\prime}J
, the mean tune spread is

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\nu\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dJ\int d\phi\Delta\nu(J)\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)=\nu^{\prime}\int dJ\int d\phi J\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)

(3.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\nu^{\prime}}{2\pi\epsilon_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}]\int dJ\;Je^{-J/\epsilon_{0}}\int d\phi\exp[-\frac{1}{\epsilon_{0}}\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J}\theta\sin(\phi-\omega(J)t)]

To integrate over
\phi
, we use

\int d\phi\exp[-a\sin(\phi+b)]=2\pi I_{0}(a)

(3.3)

Hence

\langle\Delta\nu\rangle=\frac{\nu^{\prime}}{\epsilon_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}]\int dJ\;Je^{-J/\epsilon_{0}}I_{0}(\frac{\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J}\theta}{\epsilon_{0}})

(3.4)

This is independent of time. Introducing
z=J/\epsilon_{0}
and using

\int dzz\exp[-z]I_{0}(a\sqrt{z})=(1+\frac{a^{2}}{4})\exp[\frac{a^{2}}{4}]

Here with
a=\sqrt{2\beta_{K}/\epsilon_{0}}\theta
, we have

\langle\Delta\nu\rangle=\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}\exp[\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}](1+\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}})\exp[\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}]=\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}(1+\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}})

(3.5)

And

\langle(\Delta\nu)^{2}\rangle=\frac{(\nu^{\prime})^{2}}{\epsilon_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}]\int dJ\;J^{2}e^{-J/\epsilon_{0}}I_{0}(\frac{\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J}\theta}{\epsilon_{0}})

(3.6)

Using

\int dzz^{2}\exp[-z]I_{0}(a\sqrt{z})=(2+a^{2}+(\frac{a^{2}}{4})^{2})\exp[\frac{a^{2}}{4}]

Hence

\langle(\Delta\nu)^{2}\rangle=(\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0})^{2}[2+2\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{\epsilon_{0}}+(\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}})^{2}]

(3.7)

Hence, the rms tune spread in the presence of the dipole kick is

\displaystyle\sigma(\nu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}[2+2\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{\epsilon_{0}}+(\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}})^{2}-(1+\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}})^{2}]^{1/2}

(3.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}[1+\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{\epsilon_{0}}]^{1/2}

4
Higher order echoes following a dipole and quad kicks

The
n
th order moment following these kicks is

\displaystyle\langle x^{n}(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dJ\int d\phi(2\beta J)^{n/2}\cos^{n}\phi\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t)

(4.1)

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\omega^{\prime}(J)\tau J^{3/2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sin[2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))]\cos[\phi-\omega(J)t]

(4.2)

Second order moment echo

\langle x^{2}(t)\rangle=\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\tau 2\beta\int dJ\;\omega^{\prime}(J)JJ^{3/2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\int d\phi\cos^{2}\phi\sin[2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))]\cos[\phi-\omega(J)t]

(4.3)

The integration over
\phi
is

I=\int d\phi\cos^{2}\phi\sin[2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))]\cos[\phi-\omega(J)t]\equiv 0

because the integral decomposes into a sum of integrals of the form
\int d\phi\sin m\phi
,

m\neq 0
; all of which vanish. Note that any integration over a product such as

[\sin,\cos](m_{1}\phi+a_{1})[\sin,\cos](m_{2}\phi+a_{2})...
etc where the trig function can be
either sine or cosine always vanishes if the sum
m_{1}+m_{2}+...={\rm odd}
.

Hence there is no echo in the second order moment.

Echo in the third order moment

\langle x^{3}(t)\rangle=\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\tau(2\beta)^{3/2}\int dJ\;\omega^{\prime}(J)J^{3/2}J^{3/2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\int d\phi\cos^{3}\phi\sin[2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))]\cos[\phi-\omega(J)t]

(4.4)

The
\phi
integration gives

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos^{3}\phi\sin[2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))]\cos[\phi-\omega(J)t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\pi}{8}[3\sin\omega(J)(t-2\tau)+\sin\omega(J)(3t-2\tau)]

Let
\omega(J)=\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J
, then

\langle x^{3}(t)\rangle=-\frac{\pi}{2}\beta_{k}\beta\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau\int dJ\;J^{3}\psi_{0}^{\prime}[3\sin\omega(J)(t-2\tau)+\sin\omega(J)(3t-2\tau)]

Define the phase variables

\Phi=\omega_{\beta}(t-2\tau),\;\;\;\xi_{1}=\omega^{\prime}(t-2\tau)J_{0},\;\;\;\Phi_{3}=\omega_{\beta}(3t-2\tau),\;\;\;\xi_{3}=\omega^{\prime}(3t-2\tau)J_{0}

(4.5)

Substituting
\psi_{0}^{\prime}=-(1/2\pi J_{0}^{2})\exp[-J/J_{0}]
, we can write

\langle x^{3}(t)\rangle=\frac{1}{4J_{0}^{2}}\beta_{k}\beta\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau\int dJ\;J^{3}{\rm Im}\left(3\exp[i\Phi]\exp[i\xi_{1}J/J_{0}]+\exp[i\Phi_{3}]\exp[i\xi_{3}J/J_{0}]\right)

Introducing the integration variable
z=J/J_{0}
, we have two integrals of the form

\int dz\;z^{3}\exp[-(1-ib)z]=\frac{6}{(1-ib)^{4}}

Hence

\langle x^{3}(t)\rangle=\frac{3}{2}\beta_{k}\beta\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}^{2}{\rm Im}\left(3\frac{\exp[i\Phi]}{(1-i\xi_{1})^{4}}+\frac{\exp[i\Phi_{3}]}{(1-i\xi_{3})^{4}}\right)

(4.6)

Let

\displaystyle 1+i\xi_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\xi_{1}^{2})^{1/2}\exp[i\Theta_{1}],\;\;\;\Theta_{1}={\rm Arctan}[\xi_{1}]

\displaystyle 1+i\xi_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\xi_{3}^{2})^{1/2}\exp[i\Theta_{3}],\;\;\;\Theta_{1}={\rm Arctan}[\xi_{3}]

Then

\frac{1}{(1-i\xi_{1})^{4}}=\frac{(1+i\xi_{1})^{4}}{(1+\xi_{1}^{2})^{4}}=\frac{1}{(1+\xi_{1}^{2})^{2}}\exp[4i\Theta_{1}],\;\;\;\;\frac{1}{(1-i\xi_{3})^{4}}=\frac{1}{(1+\xi_{3}^{2})^{2}}\exp[4i\Theta_{3}]

Hence

\langle x^{3}(t)\rangle=\frac{3}{2}\beta_{k}\beta\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}^{2}\left(\frac{3}{(1+\xi_{1}^{2})^{2}}\sin(\Phi+4\Theta_{1})+\frac{}{(1+\xi_{3}^{2})^{2}}\sin(\Phi_{3}+4\Theta_{3})\right)

(4.7)

The first term has a maximum at
\xi_{1}=0
or at
t2\tau
, the same time as the echo in

\langle x\rangle
. The amplitude of this echo at
t=2\tau
is (ignoring the contribution from the
second term) is

\langle x^{3}(t=2\tau)\rangle^{amp}=\frac{9}{2}\beta_{k}\beta\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}^{2}

(4.8)

while the second term has a maximum at
\xi_{3}=0
or at the earlier time
t=2\tau/3
, its
amplitude being (again ignoring the contribution of the 1st term)

\langle x^{3}(t=\frac{2}{3}\tau)\rangle^{amp}=\frac{3}{2}\beta_{k}\beta\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}^{2}

(4.9)

This echo at the earlier time has an amplitude one-third of the amplitude at the later time

t=2\tau
.

Since the echo in
\langle x\rangle
at
t=2\tau
has an amplitude

\langle x(2\tau)\rangle^{amp}=\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}
, we can write

\langle x^{3}(t=2\tau)\rangle^{amp}=\frac{9}{2}\beta J_{0}\langle x(2\tau)\rangle^{amp}=\frac{9}{2}\sigma_{x}^{2}\langle x(2\tau)\rangle^{amp}

(4.10)

where
\sigma_{x}
is the initial beam size at the BPM.

Conjecture 1
: With an odd moment echo
\langle x^{n}\rangle
,
n
is odd, there is an echo
at this time

T_{n}=\frac{2}{n}\tau

Conjecture 2
: With an odd moment echo, there will be
n-2
echoes if
n>1
. For
example, with
n=5
, there will be 3 echoes.

Above conjectures are wrong
: This follows from the integrations

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos^{5}\phi\sin[2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))]\cos[\phi-\omega(J)t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{5\pi}{2^{5}}[2\sin\omega(J)(t-2\tau)+\sin\omega(J)(3t-2\tau)]

\displaystyle 2\int d\phi\cos^{7}\phi\sin[2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))]\cos[\phi-\omega(J)t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{7\pi}{2^{7}}[5\sin\omega(J)(t-2\tau)+3\sin\omega(J)(3t-2\tau)]

Hence the higher order echoes are only at times
t=(2/3)\tau,2\tau
.

5
Decoherence functional, tune spread and all that

In
[
14
]
, the rms tune spread is related to a decoherence functional.

First, using the density distribution function
\rho(J)
is used to define a tune distribution function

f(\Delta\nu)
such that
f(\Delta\nu)\Delta\nu
is the probability for a particle to have the tune shift
\Delta\nu

within the range
d\Delta\nu
. This is defined via the tune shift function
\Delta\nu(J)
as

f(\Delta\nu)=\int dJ\rho(J)\delta(\Delta\nu-\Delta\nu(J))

(5.1)

This distribution function can be used to calculate mean values as

\langle\Delta\nu\rangle\equiv\int f(\Delta\nu)\Delta\nu\;d\Delta\nu=\int dJ\rho(J)\Delta\nu(J)

(5.2)

The decoherence functional is defined as the normalized time dependent centroid as

K(t)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{K}\beta}\theta}\langle x(t)\rangle

(5.3)

where
\theta
is the dipole kick. This can be written in units of turns
N
with
t=NT_{rev}
.

In the limit of an infinitesimally small dipole kick
, it was shown in
[
15
]
that the decoherence
functional can be written in the form

K(N)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sin[2\pi N(\nu_{0}+\Delta\nu)]f(\Delta\nu)d\nu={\rm Im}\left[e^{2\pi iN\nu_{0}}\int e^{2\pi iN\Delta\nu}f(\Delta\nu)d\Delta\nu\right]

(5.4)

This has the property that
K(N=0)=1
(because
f(\Delta\nu)
is normalized to unity). This is not a property of
the definition of
K(N)
in Eq.(
5.3
) above. More on this discrepancy below.

The amplitude of the centroid oscillations is then given by the modulus of the complex integral

K_{amp}(N)=|\int e^{2\pi iN\Delta\nu}f(\Delta\nu)d\Delta\nu|

(5.5)

With this form of
K_{amp}(N)
, it follows that the tune spread
\sigma(\nu)
defined in Eq. (
3.1
)
can be found from

\sigma(\nu)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{-\frac{d^{2}}{DNA^{2}}K_{amp}(N)|_{N=0}}

(5.6)

Comment: This is not obvious; if it is true, it must use the fact that
K_{amp}
is the absolute value.

K_{amp}(N)=[(\int\cos(2\pi N\Delta\nu)f(\Delta\nu)d\Delta\nu)^{2}+(\int\sin(2\pi N\Delta\nu)f(\Delta\nu)d\Delta\nu)^{2}]^{1/2}

(5.7)

Writing
\rho(J)=\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon_{0}}\exp[-J/\epsilon_{0}]
, and
\Delta\nu(J)=\nu^{\prime}J
, we have

f(\Delta\nu)=\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon_{0}}\int dJ\exp[-J/ESP_{0}]\delta(\Delta\nu-\nu^{\prime}J)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int dz\exp[-z]\delta(\Delta\nu-\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}z)

(5.8)

and therefore

\displaystyle K_{amp}(N)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\int dz\;\exp[-(1-2\pi iN\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0})z]|=|\frac{1}{1-2\pi iN\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}(1-\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\exp[-z]\exp[2\pi iN\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}z])|

(5.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+(2\pi N\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0})^{2})}|1+2\pi iN\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}|=\frac{1}{(1+(2\pi N\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0})^{2})^{1/2}}

This form for the centroid is
wrong
, as can be seen by comparing this with the exact form

[
4
]
, Eq. (14)

\displaystyle\frac{\langle x(N)\rangle_{amp}}{\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{K}}{\beta}}\frac{1}{1+(2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}N)^{2}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}\frac{(2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}N)^{2}}{1+(2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}N)^{2}}]

(5.10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{K}}{\beta}}\frac{1}{1+(2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}N)^{2}}+O(\theta)

(5.11)

Even dropping terms of
O(\theta)
in the RHS and putting
\beta_{K}=\beta
, this does not reduce to the form of

K_{amp}(N)
.
Instead, the correct relation appears to be

\frac{\langle x(N)\rangle_{amp}}{\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta}=|K_{amp}(N)|^{2}+O(\theta)

(5.12)

The correct expression above for the centroid comes from

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta\epsilon_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}]{\rm Im}\left\{e^{i\omega_{\beta}t}\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-\{1-i\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}t\}z]I_{1}(\frac{\sqrt{2}\beta_{K}\theta}{\sqrt{\beta\epsilon_{0}}}\sqrt{z})\right\}

(5.13)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta{\rm Im}\left\{e^{i\omega_{\beta}t}\int dz\;z\exp[-\{1-i\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}t\}z]\right\}+O(\theta^{2})

(5.14)

In the second line, we approximated
I_{1}(x)\approx x/2+O(x^{3})

The problem is in the definition of
K(N)
. Comparing the last equation above with Eq.(
5.9
), we see
that
K_{amp}
and hence
K
have a missing factor of
z
in the integrand. This is clear from the definition
of
K(N)
in Eq.(
5.4
) which has no information about the transverse amplitude of particles.
Therefore even for infinitesimally small kicks the following holds

\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta_{K}\beta}\theta}\langle x(N)\rangle\neq\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sin[2\pi N(\nu_{0}+\Delta\nu)]f(\Delta\nu)d\nu

(5.15)

The RHS is only an average over the phase but without averaging over the amplitude.

While we can use the definitions of
K(N)
in Eq.(
5.4
) and
K_{amp}
in Eq.(
5.9
),
it is not clear how they are related to the decoherence of the centroid.

Definitions of the decoherence time
N_{decoh}
in
[
14
]

•

At time
N_{decoh}
,
K_{amp}N_{decoh}=0.5K_{amp}(0)=0.5
. From this definition and
Eq.(
5.9
), it follows that

N_{decoh,1}=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}=\frac{0.276}{\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}

(5.16)

•

Definition from feedback theory for emittance growth: The behavior of
K(N)
is relevant in this case,
since a strong feedback system must damp beam oscillations quicker than the decoherence time. This
implies that only the initial stage of the decoherence process leads to residual emittance growth. The claim is

Because
K_{amp}(N)
has a quadratic dependence in the limit
N\rightarrow 0

an adequate definition is

N_{decoh}\equiv\frac{1}{\sqrt{-K_{amp}^{\prime\prime}|_{N=0}}}=\frac{1}{2\pi\sigma(\nu)}

(5.17)

Reason behind the claim: It may be true on general grounds that
K(N)
starts with maximum value of 1 at
N=0

and decreases thereafter. So
K_{amp}^{\prime}(N=0)=0
and
K_{amp}(N>0)<K_{amp}(0)
. Why is this true?

In this case

K_{amp}(N)=1-\frac{1}{2}(\frac{N}{N_{decoh}})^{2},\;\;\;\;N\ll N_{decoh}

Using Eq.(
5.9
) again, it follows that

K_{amp}^{\prime}(N)=-\frac{(2\pi\nu^{\prime})^{2}N}{[1+(2\pi\nu^{\prime}N)^{2}]},\;\;\;K_{amp}^{\prime}(0)=0,\;K_{amp}^{\prime}(N)<0

and from the 2nd derivative,

N_{decoh,2}=\frac{1}{2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}=\frac{0.159}{\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}

(5.18)

•

Definition from noise: Another definition comes from the effects of noise without feedback. Assume
that at each turn, the beam receives uncorrelated kicks of amplitude
a_{m}
at turn
m
. Then the average
displacement after
N
turns is

\Delta x_{c}=\sum_{m=0}^{N}K(N-m)a_{m}

(5.19)

Question: How does this follow from the definition of
K(m)
? Note that this uses the oscillating decoherence
function
K(m)
and not just the amplitude of this function.

The averaged (over noise) squared displacement is (assuming uncorrelated kicks so that

\langle a_{m}a_{n}\rangle=\langle a^{2}\rangle\delta_{mn}
)

\langle(\Delta x_{c})^{2}\rangle=\sum_{n=0}^{N}\sum_{m=0}^{N}K(N-m)K(N-n)\langle a_{n}a_{m}\rangle=\langle a^{2}\rangle\sum_{m=0}^{N}K^{2}(N-m)=\langle a^{2}\rangle\sum_{m=0}^{N}K^{2}(m)

(5.20)

Definition of decoherence time
N_{decoh}
:

\langle(\Delta x_{c})^{2}\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{2}N_{decoh}\langle a^{2}\rangle

(5.21)

Comment: This definition does not make sense. The average squared displacement grows with time due to
white noise, but the expression above assumes it becomes constant. Inconsistent definition.

Continuing with the above, we have

N_{decoh,3}=2\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}K^{2}(m)\approx\int_{0}^{\infty}K_{amp}^{2}(m)dm

(5.22)

where in the last approximation it was assumed that
K(n)
oscillates rapidly at the betatron frequency, so
that its squared average is half the squared average of the amplitude.
Again using Eq.(
5.9
), we have

N_{decoh,3}=\frac{1}{4\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}=\frac{0.25}{\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}

(5.23)

•

Hierarchy of decoherence times

N_{decoh,1}>N_{decoh,3}>N_{decoh,2}

Question: How would the definition of
K(N)
be generalized for arbitrary kick amplitudes?

The basic definition of
K(N)
in Eq.(
5.4
) is a Sine transform of the tune distribution function.
The amplitude function
K_{amp}(N)
is the same as the amplitude of the Fourier transform.

5.1
Decoherence time from the centroid evolution

From the exact expression for the amplitude of the centroid

\langle x(N)\rangle_{amp}=\beta_{K}\theta\frac{1}{1+(2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}N)^{2}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}\frac{(2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}N)^{2}}{1+(2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}N)^{2}}]

(5.24)

we can find approximately the time at which the centroid amplitude falls to
1/e
of its initial value.
Define the parameter

b^{2}=\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{\beta}{\beta_{K}}a^{2},\;\;\;a=\frac{\beta_{K}\theta}{\sigma_{0}},\;\;\;\sigma_{0}=\sqrt{\beta\epsilon_{0}}

(5.25)

Here
a
is the dipole kick amplitude relative to the rms beam size
\sigma_{0}
. Setting the amplitude of the
exponential factor in Eq.(
5.24
) to -1 yields the approximate decoherence turn number

\displaystyle N_{decoh}^{(1)}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{b^{2}-1}}\frac{1}{2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}

(5.26)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}-1}}

This form can be written in terms of the rms tune spread after the dipole kick, given in Eq.(
3.8
).

b^{2}=\frac{1}{2}(\frac{\sigma(\nu)}{\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}-1)

Hence, we also have

N_{decoh}^{(1)}\approx\frac{1}{2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\frac{\sigma(\nu)}{\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}-3}}

(5.27)

At this time, the ratio of the centroid amplitude to its initial value is

\frac{\langle x(N_{decoh})\rangle_{amp}}{\langle x(0)\rangle_{amp}}=(1-\frac{1}{b^{2}})e^{-1}

(5.28)

The RHS approaches
1/e
for
b\gg 1
. This analysis above assumes that
b>1
,

The completely general equation to be solved is

\frac{1}{1+\Theta^{2}}\exp[-b^{2}\frac{\Theta^{2}}{1+\Theta^{2}}]=\exp[-1]

where we defined
\Theta=2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}N
. This equation can be rewritten as

\ln(1+\Theta^{2})+b^{2}\frac{\Theta^{2}}{1+\Theta^{2}}=1

(5.29)

When
b^{2}\gg 1
, we have the solution in Eq.(
5.26
).

In the opposite limit when
b^{2}\ll 1
, i.e. for very weak kicks, if we drop the 2nd term, then in this limit
we have

N_{decoh}^{(2)}\approx\sqrt{e-1}\frac{1}{2\pi\nu^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}},\;\;\;\;b^{2}\ll 1

(5.30)

Since we have

N_{decoh}^{(2)}>N_{decoh}^{(1)}

this analysis shows that the decoherence time decreases as the dipole kick increases.

The intermediate case when
b^{2}\sim O{1}
needs more work.

6
Decoherence and echoes in 1D with chromatic tune spread

Now the betatron tune depends on the synchrotron motion via the chromaticity
\chi

\omega(J,\delta)=\omega_{\beta}[1+\chi\delta(t)]+\omega^{\prime}J=\omega_{\beta}[1+\chi\hat{\delta}\cos(\omega_{s}t+\phi_{s0})]+\omega^{\prime}J

(6.1)

where
\hat{\delta}
is the amplitude of the relative energy deviation for the particle and
\phi_{s0}
is
the initial synchrotron phase of the particle. Note that the chromaticity
\chi
is defined here as

\chi\delta=\frac{\omega(J=0,\delta)-\omega_{\beta}}{\omega_{\beta}}=\frac{\nu(J=0,\delta)-\nu_{\beta}}{\nu_{\beta}}

i.e. the chromaticity is scaled by the nominal tune
\nu_{\beta}
. Another definition of the linear chromaticity is to
define it as the 1st term in a Taylor series expansion of
\nu(\delta)
as

\nu(\delta)=\nu_{\beta}+\xi\delta+O(\delta^{2})

Hence
\chi=\xi/\nu_{\beta}
.

Alternatively, it may be better to consider the complete longitudinal phase space
(\hat{z},\hat{\delta})
. In this
case, assuming linear motion

\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{z}(t)\\
\hat{\delta}(t)\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\cos\omega_{s}t&\sin\omega_{s}t\\
-\sin\omega_{s}t&\cos\omega_{s}t\end{array}\right]\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\hat{z}_{0}\\
\hat{\delta}_{0}\end{array}\right]

(6.2)

For this to be valid,
\hat{z},\hat{\delta}
must have the same dimensions.

One way would be to scale the physical quantities
(z,\delta)
by their rms values as

\hat{z}=\frac{z}{\sigma_{z}},\;\;\;\hat{\delta}=\frac{\delta}{\sigma_{\delta}}

So the betatron tune changes with time as

\omega(J,t)=\omega_{\beta}[1+\chi\sigma_{\delta}\{\hat{\delta}_{0}\cos\omega_{s}t-\hat{z}_{0}\sin\omega_{s}t\}]+\omega^{\prime}J

(6.3)

In this case, the betatron phase advances with time as

\Delta\phi(t)=\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\;\omega(J,t^{\prime})=(\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J)t+\chi\sigma_{\delta}\frac{\omega_{\beta}}{\omega_{s}}[\hat{\delta}_{0}\sin\omega_{s}t-\hat{z}_{0}(1-\cos\omega_{s}t)]\equiv\Delta\phi_{\beta}(t)+\Delta\phi_{s}(t)

(6.4)

where
(\Delta\phi_{\beta}(t),\Delta\phi_{s}(t)
denote the betatron and synchrotron motion contributions to the phase
change, and
\Delta\phi(0)=0
.

If the initial transverse distribution is
\psi_{0}(J)
, after the dipole kick, the distribution is

\psi_{1}(J,\phi)=\psi_{0}(x,p-\beta_{K}\theta)=\psi_{0}(J+\theta\sqrt{2J/\beta}\sin\phi+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2})

(6.5)

We assume that the change in the betatron motion does not affect the longitudinal motion, so that there are
no changes to the longitudinal distributions.

At time
t
after the dipole kick, the distribution changes to

\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)=\psi_{1}(J,\phi-\Delta\phi(t))=\psi_{0}(J+\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{2J/\beta}\sin\phi_{-t}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}),\;\;\;\phi_{-t}=\phi-\Delta\phi(t)

(6.6)

The centroid can be found by the usual procedure, except that the average must be done over initial coordinates
in longitudinal phase space

\langle x(t)\rangle=\int dJ\int d\phi\int d\hat{z}_{0}\int d\hat{\delta}_{0}\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)\psi_{s}(\hat{z}_{0},\hat{\delta}_{0})\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi

(6.7)

where we assumed that the transverse and longitudinal distributions are uncoupled.

Assume that the longitudinal distributions are Gaussian, so that

\psi_{s}(\hat{z}_{0},\hat{\delta}_{0})=\frac{1}{2\pi}\exp[-\frac{\hat{z}_{0}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\hat{\delta}_{0}^{2}}{2}]

(6.8)

Substituting for
\psi_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dJ\int d\phi\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi\int d\hat{z}_{0}\int d\hat{\delta}_{0}\psi_{0}(J+\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{2J/\beta}\sin\phi_{-t}+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2})\psi_{s}(\hat{z}_{0},\hat{\delta}_{0})

(6.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon_{0}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{2\beta}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}\int dJ\int d\phi\sqrt{J}e^{-J/\epsilon_{0}}\cos\phi

\displaystyle\times\int dz_{0}\int d\hat{\delta}_{0}\exp[-\frac{\hat{z}_{0}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\hat{\delta}_{0}^{2}}{2}]\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{2J/\beta}}{\epsilon_{0}}\sin(\phi-\Delta\phi_{\beta}(t)-\Delta\phi_{s}(t))

The
\hat{z}_{0},\hat{\delta}_{0}
integrations are of the form

I_{s}(a,b,c)=\int d\hat{z}_{0}\int d\hat{\delta}_{0}\exp[-\frac{\hat{z}_{0}^{2}}{2}-\frac{\hat{\delta}_{0}^{2}}{2}]\exp[-a\sin(\phi_{-,\beta}+b\hat{z}_{0}-c\hat{\delta}_{0})]

(6.10)

where

a=\frac{\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{2J/\beta}}{\epsilon_{0}},\;\;\;b=\chi\sigma_{\delta}\frac{\omega_{\beta}}{\omega_{s}}(1-\cos\omega_{s}t),\;\;\;c=\chi\sigma_{\delta}\frac{\omega_{\beta}}{\omega_{s}}\sin\omega_{s}t,\;\;\;\phi_{-,\beta}=\phi-\Delta\phi_{\beta}(t)

We use the expansion

e^{-a\sin\theta}=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}i^{n}I_{n}(a)e^{in\theta}

We obtain

\displaystyle I_{s}(a,b,c)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}i^{n}I_{n}(a)e^{in\phi_{-,\beta}}\int d\hat{z}_{0}e^{-inb\hat{z}_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\hat{z}_{0}^{2}}{2}]\int d\hat{\delta}_{0}e^{-inc\hat{\delta}_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\hat{\delta}_{0}^{2}}{2}]

(6.11)

We use the integrations

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\;\exp[-ax^{2}]\cos[bx]=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{a}}\exp[-\frac{b^{2}}{4a}],\;\;\;\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\;\exp[-ax^{2}]\sin[bx]=0

Hence

I_{s}(a,b,c)=2\pi\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}i^{n}I_{n}(a)e^{in\phi_{-,\beta}}\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{2}[(nb)^{2}+(nc)^{2}]\right\}

(6.12)

We have

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon_{0}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\sqrt{2\beta}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}\int dJ\int d\phi\sqrt{J}e^{-J/\epsilon_{0}}\cos\phi I_{s}(a,b,c)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2\beta}}{2\pi\epsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}\int dJ\int d\phi\sqrt{J}e^{-J/\epsilon_{0}}\cos\phi\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}i^{n}I_{n}(a)e^{in(\phi-\Delta\phi_{\beta})}e^{-[(nb)^{2}+(nc)^{2}]/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2\beta}}{2\pi\epsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}\int dJ\;\sqrt{J}e^{-J/\epsilon_{0}}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}i^{n}I_{n}(a)e^{-in\Delta\phi_{\beta})}e^{-[(nb)^{2}+(nc)^{2}]/2}

\displaystyle\times{\rm Re}\left[\int d\phi\exp\{i(n+1)\phi\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon_{0}}\sqrt{2\beta}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}e^{-[b^{2}+c^{2}]/2}{\rm Re}\left[\int dJ\;\sqrt{J}e^{-J/\epsilon_{0}}i^{-1}I_{-1}(a)e^{i\Delta\phi_{\beta}}\right]

Using

{\rm Re}[-if(z)]={\rm Im}[f(z)],\;\;\;I_{-m}(z)=I_{m}(z)

and introducing the scaled variable

u=J/\epsilon_{0},\;\;\;a=\sqrt{2}\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{u}{\beta\epsilon_{0}}}=b_{2}\sqrt{u};\;\;\;b_{2}=\sqrt{2}\frac{\beta_{K}\theta}{\sigma_{0}}

we have

\Delta\omega_{\beta}=\omega_{\beta}t+\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}ut

Hence

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta\epsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}[(b)^{2}+(c)^{2}]}{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\omega_{\beta}t}\int du\;\sqrt{u}I_{1}(b_{2}\sqrt{u})e^{-(1-i\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}t)u}\right]

(6.13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta e^{-\frac{1}{2}[(b)^{2}+(c)^{2}]}{\rm Im}\left[\frac{e^{i\omega_{\beta}t}}{(1-i\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}t)^{2}}\exp[\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}\frac{i\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}t}{(1-i\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}t)}]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta_{K}\theta}{1+\Theta^{2}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}[(b)^{2}+(c)^{2}]}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}\frac{\Theta^{2}}{(1+\Theta^{2})}]

\displaystyle\times\sin[\omega_{\beta}t+\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}\frac{\Theta}{(1+\Theta^{2})}+2{\rm Arctan}\Theta]

where as before

\Theta(t)=\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}t

Substituting for
b
and
c
,

\displaystyle b^{2}+c^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\chi\sigma_{\delta}\frac{\omega_{\beta}}{\omega_{s}})^{2}[(1-\cos\omega_{s}t)^{2}+\sin^{2}\omega_{s}t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(\chi\sigma_{\delta}\frac{\omega_{\beta}}{\omega_{s}})^{2}[1-\cos\omega_{s}t]=[2\chi\sigma_{\delta}\frac{\omega_{\beta}}{\omega_{s}}\sin(\omega_{s}t/2)]^{2}

Hence the amplitude of the decoherence is in the presence of chromaticity

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle_{amp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta_{K}\theta}{1+\Theta^{2}}\exp\{-2(\frac{\chi\sigma_{\delta}\omega_{\beta}}{\omega_{s}}\sin(\omega_{s}t/2))^{2}\}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}\frac{\Theta^{2}}{(1+\Theta^{2})}]

(6.14)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\exp[-\frac{1}{2}\alpha^{2}]\langle x(t)\rangle_{amp}(\chi=0)

(6.15)

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\chi\sigma_{\delta}\frac{\omega_{\beta}}{\omega_{s}}\sin(\omega_{s}t/2)=\sqrt{b^{2}+c^{2}}

(6.16)

We see that chromaticity results in a multiplicative exponential factor which oscillates with the synchrotron
frequency.

Note: The factor of
\alpha
defined above is the same as the
\alpha
defined in Eq.(7a) of
[
12
]
, once
we take into account the difference in definition of chromaticities. Here we have defined the chromaticity

\chi
via the change of tune amplitude

|\Delta\nu_{\beta}|=\nu_{\beta}\chi\delta

(6.17)

while Meller et al define it via
|\Delta\nu_{\beta}|=\chi\delta
.

6.1
Emittance growth with chromatic tune spread

The rms emittance is given by

\epsilon=\left[\langle x^{2}\rangle\langle p^{2}\rangle-(\langle xp\rangle)^{2}\right]^{1/2}

(6.18)

We have

\langle x^{2}\rangle=\int dJ\int d\phi\int d\hat{z}_{0}\int d\hat{\delta}_{0}\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)\psi_{s}(\hat{z}_{0},\hat{\delta}_{0})2\beta J\cos^{2}\phi

(6.19)

The integrations over the longitudinal variables are unchanged, leaving us with

\displaystyle\langle x^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}\epsilon_{0}}2\beta e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}/\epsilon_{0}}\int dJ\int d\phi\sqrt{J}e^{-J/\epsilon_{0}}\cos^{2}\phi\;I_{s}(a,b,c)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{2\pi\epsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}/\epsilon_{0}}\int dJ\;Je^{-J/\epsilon_{0}}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}i^{n}I_{n}(a)e^{-in\Delta\phi_{\beta})}e^{-[(nb)^{2}+(nc)^{2}]/2}

\displaystyle\times\left[\int d\phi\exp\{in\phi\}[1+\frac{1}{2}(e^{2i\phi}+e^{-2i\phi})]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta}{\epsilon_{0}}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}/\epsilon_{0}}\int dJ\;Je^{-J/\epsilon_{0}}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}i^{n}I_{n}(a)e^{-in\Delta\phi_{\beta})}e^{-[(nb)^{2}+(nc)^{2}]/2}

\displaystyle\times\left[\delta_{n,0}+\frac{1}{2}(\delta_{n,-2}+\delta_{n,2})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\epsilon_{0}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}/\epsilon_{0}}\int du\;ue^{u}\left[I_{0}(a)-I_{2}(a)e^{-2[b^{2}+c^{2}]}\cos 2\Delta\phi_{\beta}\right]

For the 1st integral we use (
a=b_{2}\sqrt{u}
)

\int du\;u\exp[-u]I_{0}(b_{2}\sqrt{u})=(1+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4})\exp[\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4}]

For the 2nd integral, we write it as

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int du\;ue^{u}I_{2}(b_{2}\sqrt{u}){\rm Re}[e^{2i(\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}u)t}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left[e^{2i\omega_{\beta}t}\int du\;uI_{2}(b_{2}\sqrt{u})\exp[-(1-2i\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0})u]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left[e^{2i\omega_{\beta}t}\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4(1-2i\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0})^{3}}\exp[\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4(1-2i\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0})}]\right]

Let as before

\Theta=\omega_{\beta}t,\;\;\;\Theta_{2}=2\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}t,\;\;\;\Psi_{2}=2\Theta+\frac{b_{2}^{2}\Theta_{2}}{4(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})}

(6.20)

Evaluating the second term above separately,

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left[\frac{e^{2i\Theta}}{(1-i\Theta_{2})^{3}}\exp[\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4(1-i\Theta_{2})}]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}}{\rm Re}\left[(1+i\Theta_{2})^{3}\exp[2i\Theta+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4(1+\Theta_{2})^{2}}(1+i\Theta_{2})]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}}\exp[\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})}]{\rm Re}\left[(1+i\Theta_{2})^{3}\exp[i\{2\Theta+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})}\Theta_{2}\}]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}}\exp[\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})}]{\rm Re}\left[(1+i\Theta_{2})^{3}\exp[i\Psi_{2}]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}}\exp[\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})}]\left[(1-3\Theta_{2}^{2})\cos\Psi_{2}-(3\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{2}^{3})\sin\Psi_{2}\right]

Using

(1-3\Theta_{2}^{2})^{2}+(3\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{2}^{3})^{2}=(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}

we can write

(1-3\Theta_{2}^{2})\cos\Psi_{2}-(3\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{2}^{3})\sin\Psi_{2}=(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3/2}\cos(\Psi_{2}+\Delta\Psi)

where

\tan\Delta\Psi=\frac{3\Theta_{2}-\Theta_{2}^{3}}{1-3\Theta_{2}^{2}},\Rightarrow\Delta\Psi=3{\rm Arctan}[\Theta_{2}]

Combining the two terms from the integration (after using
b^{2}+c^{2}=\alpha^{2}
)

\langle x^{2}\rangle=\beta\epsilon_{0}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}/\epsilon_{0}}\left[(1+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4})\exp[\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4}]-e^{-2\alpha^{2}}\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}}\exp[\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})}]\cos(\Psi_{2}+\Delta\Psi)\right]

(6.21)

Since
b_{2}^{2}/4=(\beta_{K}\theta)^{2}/(2\beta\epsilon_{0})=\frac{1}{2}(\beta_{K}^{2}/\beta)\theta^{2}/\epsilon_{0}
reduces to

\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}/\epsilon_{0}
when
\beta_{K}\equiv\beta
, I should replace
\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta^{2}/\epsilon_{0}
by

b_{2}^{2}/4
. Hence

\displaystyle\langle x(t)^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\epsilon_{0}\left[1+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4}-\frac{e^{-2\alpha^{2}}}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}}\exp[-\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4}(1-\frac{1}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})})]\cos(\Psi_{2}+\Delta\Psi)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\epsilon_{0}\left[1+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4}-\frac{e^{-2\alpha^{2}}b_{2}^{2}}{4(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}}\exp[-\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4}\frac{\Theta_{2}^{2}}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})}]\cos(\Psi_{2}+\Delta\Psi)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\epsilon_{0}\left[1+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4}-e^{-2\alpha^{2}}A_{2}\cos(\Psi_{2}+\Delta\Psi)\right]

where

\displaystyle A_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}}\exp[-\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4}\frac{\Theta_{2}^{2}}{(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\beta_{K}\theta)^{2}}{2\beta\epsilon_{0}(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})^{3}}\exp[-\frac{(\beta_{K}\theta)^{2}\Theta_{2}^{2}}{2\beta\epsilon_{0}(1+\Theta_{2}^{2})}

At
t=0
,

\alpha(0)=0,\;\;\;\Theta_{2}(0)=0,\;\;\;A_{2}(0)=\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4},\;\;\;\Psi_{2}(0)=0=\Delta\Psi(0)

hence we have
\langle x(0)^{2}\rangle=\beta\epsilon_{0}
as desired, In the opposite limit of
long times
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}A_{2}(t)=0
, hence we have

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\langle x(t)^{2}\rangle=\beta\epsilon_{0}\left[1+\frac{(\beta_{K}\theta)^{2}}{2\beta\epsilon_{0}}\right]

(6.22)

The chromaticity has no impact on the asymptotic value of
\langle x(t)^{2}\rangle
.

The remaining rms values are

\displaystyle\langle p^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dJ\int d\phi\int d\hat{z}_{0}\int d\hat{\delta}_{0}\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)\psi_{s}(\hat{z}_{0},\hat{\delta}_{0})2\beta J\sin^{2}\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\epsilon_{0}\left[1+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4}+e^{-2\alpha^{2}}A_{2}\cos(\Psi_{2}+\Delta\Psi)\right]

At
t=0
, we have

\langle p(0)^{2}\rangle=\beta\epsilon_{0}[1+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{2}]=\beta\epsilon_{0}+(\beta_{K}\theta)^{2}

Hence

\langle x^{2}\rangle\langle p^{2}\rangle=(\beta\epsilon_{0}\left[(1+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4})^{2}-e^{-4\alpha^{2}}A_{2}^{2}\cos^{2}(\Psi_{2}+\Delta\Psi)\right]

Similarly,

\displaystyle\langle xp\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dJ\int d\phi\int d\hat{z}_{0}\int d\hat{\delta}_{0}\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)\psi_{s}(\hat{z}_{0},\hat{\delta}_{0})2\beta J\cos\phi\sin\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\epsilon_{0}e^{-2\alpha^{2}}\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4}{\rm Im}\left(\frac{e^{2i\Theta}}{(1-i\Theta_{2})^{3}}\exp[\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4(1-i\Theta_{2})}]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\epsilon_{0}e^{-2\alpha^{2}}A_{2}\sin(\Psi_{2}+\Delta\Psi)

It follows that the rms emittance is

\displaystyle\epsilon(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}\left[(1+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4})^{2}-e^{-4\alpha^{2}}A_{2}^{2}\cos^{2}(\Psi_{2}+\Delta\Psi)-e^{-4\alpha^{2}}A_{2}^{2}\sin^{2}(\Psi_{2}+\Delta\Psi)\right]^{1/2}

(6.23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}\left[(1+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4})^{2}-e^{-4\alpha^{2}}A_{2}^{2}\right]^{1/2}

At long times, we have

\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\epsilon(t)=\epsilon_{0}(1+\frac{(\beta_{K}\theta)^{2}}{2\beta\epsilon_{0}})

(6.24)

The asymptotic emittance is not affected by the chromaticity.
At intermediate times, the time dependent factor is changed by the multiplicative factor

\exp[-4\alpha(t)^{2}]=\exp[-(4\chi\sigma_{\delta}\frac{\omega_{\beta}}{\omega_{s}}\sin(\omega_{s}t/2))^{2}]

Time constant for the emittance decay

\displaystyle 2\epsilon\frac{d\epsilon}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\epsilon_{0}^{2}\left[2e^{-4\alpha^{2}}A_{2}\frac{dA_{2}}{dt}-8\alpha\frac{d\alpha}{dt}e^{-4\alpha^{2}}A_{2}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\epsilon_{0}^{2}e^{-4\alpha^{2}}A_{2}\left[\frac{dA_{2}}{dt}-4\alpha\frac{d\alpha}{dt}A_{2}\right]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{1}{\epsilon}\frac{d\epsilon}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{[(1+\frac{b_{2}^{2}}{4})^{2}-e^{-4\alpha^{2}}A_{2}^{2}]}e^{-4\alpha^{2}}A_{2}\left[\frac{dA_{2}}{dt}-4\alpha\frac{d\alpha}{dt}A_{2}\right]

6.2
Echo amplitude with chromatic tune spread

Without the chromatic tune spread and in the simplified nonlinear dipole theory we had
for the dipole moment

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2\beta\epsilon_{0}}}{2\pi}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2\epsilon_{0}}]\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-z]T_{\phi}(z)

(6.25)

\displaystyle T_{\phi}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\{\int d\phi e^{i\phi}\exp\left[-a_{\theta}\sqrt{2z}\sin(\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-\tau\omega-\frac{1}{2}q+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})\right]\right\}

(6.26)

where before
\Delta\phi=\omega(J)t=(\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}u)t\equiv\Delta\omega_{\beta}
. To include the chromatic
effects, we replace
\Delta\phi
in
T_{\phi}
above by
\Delta\phi_{\beta}+\Delta\phi_{s}
where

\Delta\phi_{s}(t)=\chi\sigma_{\delta}\frac{\omega_{\beta}}{\omega_{s}}[\hat{\delta}_{0}\sin\omega_{s}t-\hat{z}_{0}(1-\cos\omega_{s}t)]\equiv b\hat{\delta}_{0}-c\hat{z}_{0}

(6.27)

and average over
(\hat{\delta}_{0},\hat{z}_{0})
.

7
Analysis of echoes with diffusion

I follow Chao’s notation. The coordinates used are
x,p
and action angle coordinates
J,\phi
are related as

\displaystyle x=\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi,\;\;\;

\displaystyle\;\;\;p=\alpha x+\beta x^{\prime}=-\sqrt{2\beta J}\sin\phi

\displaystyle J=\frac{1}{2\beta}(x^{2}+p^{2}),

\displaystyle\tan\phi=-\frac{p}{x}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial J}{\partial x}=\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\cos\phi

\displaystyle\frac{\partial J}{\partial p}=-\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\sin\phi

The initial distribution is exponential in the action

\psi_{0}(J)=\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}]

(7.1)

where
J_{0}
is the average action of the beam distribution and related to the rms emittance
\epsilon
by

J_{0}=\epsilon

Following the dipole kick by an angle
\theta
, the beam distribution is

\psi_{1}(J,\phi)=\psi_{0}(x,p-\beta\theta)\simeq\psi_{0}(J)-\beta\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}\frac{\partial J}{\partial p}+O[(\beta\theta)^{2}]

(7.2)

In the absence of diffusion, the distribution would obey

\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)=\psi_{1}(J,\phi-\omega(J)t)

In the presence of diffusion, the distribution
\psi_{2}
after the dipole kick evolves according to the diffusion equation

\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial t}=\frac{\partial}{\partial J}[D(J)\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial J}]

(7.3)

To solve this equation, transform from the independent variables
J,\phi
to
J,v=\phi-\omega(J)t
. The derivatives transform as

\frac{\partial}{\partial J}\rightarrow\frac{\partial}{\partial J}+\frac{\partial v}{\partial J}\frac{\partial}{\partial v}=\frac{\partial}{\partial J}-\omega^{\prime}t\frac{\partial}{\partial v},\;\;\;\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\rightarrow\frac{\partial v}{\partial\phi}\frac{\partial}{\partial v}=\frac{\partial}{\partial v}

The RHS of the diffusion equation transforms to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial J}[D(J)\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial J}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle[\frac{\partial}{\partial J}-\omega^{\prime}t\frac{\partial}{\partial v}][D(J)(\frac{\partial}{\partial J}-\omega^{\prime}t\frac{\partial}{\partial v})]\psi_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial J}[D(J)\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial J}]-t\frac{\partial}{\partial J}[D(J)\omega^{{}^{\prime}}\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial v}]-\omega^{\prime}tD(J)\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial v\partial J}+(\omega^{\prime}t)^{2}D(J)\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial v^{2}}

Assuming for the moment that
D(J)=D_{0}
, const. and
\omega(J)=\omega_{0}+\omega^{\prime}J
, the diffusion
equation simplifies to

\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial t}=D_{0}[\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial J^{2}}-2\omega^{\prime}t\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial J\partial v}+(\omega^{\prime}t)^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial v^{2}}]

Assuming that the phase variations in the distribution are more significant than the
amplitude variations and keeping only the term that increases fastest with time, we have
the simplified diffusion equation

\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial t}=D_{0}(\omega^{\prime}t)^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial v^{2}}

This assumes the following

\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial J^{2}},|2\omega^{\prime}t\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial J\partial v}|\ll(\omega^{\prime}t)^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial v^{2}}

We will check whether these conditions are satisfied when we have constructed some solutions.
Under these conditions, the above diffusion equation would also be valid when the diffusion
coefficient is a function of the action, hence we have

\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial t}=D(J)(\omega^{\prime}t)^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial v^{2}}

(7.4)

The solution to a PDE of the form

\psi_{t}=A(t)\psi_{vv}

is of the form (by separation of variables)

\psi(t)=\psi(0)\exp[-\int A(t)dt]\sin v

Hence the solution to Eq.(
7.4
) is

\psi_{2}(J,v,t)=\psi_{2}(J,v,0)\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t^{3}]\sin v

(7.5)

where
\psi_{2}(J,v,0)=\psi_{1}(J,v,0)=\psi_{0}(J)+\theta\sqrt{2\beta J}\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)
. Since the
1st term
\psi_{0}(J)
will not contribute to the dipole moment, it can be dropped. Hence

\psi_{2}(J,v,t)=\theta\sqrt{2\beta J}\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t^{3}]\sin v

(7.6)

We now check if the assumptions made in writing the simplified diffusion equation above
are valid for this solution. For simplicity here we assume
D(J)=D_{0}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial J^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta\sqrt{2\beta}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}\omega^{\prime}t^{3}]\sin v

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4J^{3/2}}[4J\psi_{0}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}+4J^{2}\psi_{0}^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}-\psi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}]

\displaystyle-\frac{2D_{0}}{3J^{1/2}}[2J\omega^{{}^{\prime}}\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\psi_{0}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}+\psi_{0}\omega^{{}^{\prime}}(J(\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}})^{2}+\omega^{{}^{\prime}}(\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}}+J\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}))]t^{3}

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{9}J^{1/2}D_{0}^{2}(\omega^{{}^{\prime}}\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}})^{2}\psi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}t^{6}

Keeping the dominant
t^{6}
term at long time, the condition

\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial J^{2}}\ll(\omega^{\prime}t)^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial v^{2}}

(7.7)

requires that

\frac{4}{9}D_{0}^{2}(\omega^{{}^{\prime}}\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}})^{2}t^{6}\ll(\omega^{{}^{\prime}}t)^{2}

We can consider two cases

•

\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}}=0

In this case, the
t^{3}
terms also vanish, and we are left with the condition

J^{2}\frac{\psi_{0}^{{}^{\prime\prime\prime}}}{\psi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}}+J\frac{\psi_{0}^{{}^{\prime\prime}}}{\psi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}}-\frac{1}{4}\ll J^{2}(\omega^{{}^{\prime}}t)^{2}

Applying this to the initial distribution

\psi_{0}(J)=\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}]

requires

(\frac{J}{J_{0}})^{2}-\frac{J}{J_{0}}-\frac{1}{4}\ll(\omega^{{}^{\prime}}t)^{2}

This implies that at a given time
t
, the approximation makes the diffusion equation valid
in actions utp a value
J
satisfying this inequality.

•

\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}}\neq 0

In this case, the analysis is valid for times
t
and weak diffusion
D_{0}
and
detuning so that

\frac{4}{9}D_{0}^{2}(\omega^{{}^{\prime\prime}})^{2}t^{4}\ll 1

Continuation of echo analysis

Assuming that these conditions are obeyed

|\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial J^{2}}|,2|\omega^{\prime}t\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial J\partial v}|\ll(\omega^{\prime}t)^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial v^{2}}

the distribution obeys

\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial t}=D(J)(\omega^{\prime}t)^{2}\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial v^{2}}

(7.8)

The solution of

\psi_{t}=f(t)\psi_{vv}

is of the form

\psi(v,t)=\psi(0)\exp[-\int f(t)dt]\sin(v+c)

where
\psi(0)
is the distribution at time
t=0
and
c
is an arbitrary constant. In our case to match the distribution at
t=0
, I will put
c=0
. Then

\psi_{2}(J.v,t)=\psi_{2}(J,v,0)\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t^{3}]\sin v

(7.9)

We have

\psi_{2}(J,v,0)=\psi_{1}(J,v,0)=\psi_{0}(J)+\theta\sqrt{2\beta J}\psi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(J)\sin v

Since
\psi_{0}(J)
does not contribute to the dipole moment, it can be dropped. Thus

\psi_{2}(J,v,t)=\theta\sqrt{2\beta J}\psi_{0}^{{}^{\prime}}(J)\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t^{3}]\sin v

(7.10)

where
v=\phi-\omega t
. Following the quad kick at time
t=\tau
,

\displaystyle\psi_{4}(J,\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{3}(x,p+qx)\approx\psi_{3}(J,\phi)+qx\frac{\partial\psi_{3}}{\partial p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{3}(J,\phi)-q\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi[\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\sin\phi\frac{\partial\psi_{3}}{\partial J}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\beta J}}\sin\phi\frac{\partial\psi_{3}}{\partial\phi}]

Keeping only the dominant term from
\partial\phi_{3}/\partial J
,

\frac{\partial\psi_{3}}{\partial J}\simeq-\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos(\phi-\omega\tau)

Hence, keeping only the term linear in quadrupole strength
q
,

\psi_{4}(J,\phi)=\theta q(\omega^{\prime}\tau)\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t^{3}]\sin 2\phi\cos(\phi-\omega\tau)

At times
t>\tau
, the distribution is given by the diffusion equation

\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial t}=\frac{\partial}{\partial J}[D(J)\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J}]

(7.11)

Changing the phase variable from
\phi
to
u=\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)

\frac{\partial}{\partial J}\rightarrow\frac{\partial}{\partial J}-\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial u}

and the diffusion equation for
\psi_{5}
is

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\psi_{5}=[\frac{\partial}{\partial J}-\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial u}][D(J)(\frac{\partial}{\partial J}-\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial u})]\psi_{5}

Under the approximation that
[\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau)]^{2}\partial^{2}\psi_{5}/\partial u^{2}
is the dominant term on the RHS,
the diffusion equation simplifies to

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\psi_{5}=[\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau)]^{2}D(J)\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{5}}{\partial u^{2}}

(7.12)

Assuming that the initial condition satisfies
\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t=\tau)=\psi_{4}(J,\phi)
, the solution
can be written as

\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t)=\psi_{4}(J,\phi)\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}(t-\tau)^{3}]\sin(u+c)

(7.13)

where

\psi_{4}(J,\phi)=\theta q(\omega^{\prime}\tau)\sqrt{2\beta J}J\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{3}]\sin 2\phi\cos(\phi-\omega\tau)

Writing the product of trigonometric terms as

\sin 2\phi\cos(\phi-\omega\tau)=\frac{1}{2}[\sin(3\phi-\omega\tau)+\sin(\phi+\omega\tau)]

The first term is a third harmonic and gives rise to a sextupole echo and may have a negligible
impact on the dipole echo. Assuming this is the case, er can keep only the second term above.

Matching the solutions for
\psi_{5}
and
\psi_{4}
at
t=\tau
requires

\sin(u+c)|_{t=\tau}=\sin(\phi+c)=\sin(\phi+\omega\tau),\;\;\;c=\omega\tau

Consider the consequences of ignoring the sextupole echo term. It has the same weight as the
dipole term but it could average out in the dipole moment. Also
\psi_{5}
and
\psi_{4}
could not be
matched at
t\tau
. Also the normalization is not preserved, i.e.

\int\psi_{4}(J,\phi)dJd\phi\neq\int\psi_{5}(J,\phi)dJd\phi

With the above and
u+c=\phi-\omega(t-2\tau)
, we have

\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t)=\frac{1}{2}\theta q(\omega^{\prime}\tau)\sqrt{2\beta J}J\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}((t-\tau)^{3}+\tau^{3})]\sin(\phi-\omega(t-2\tau))

(7.14)

The echo amplitude is

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta}\int dJ\sqrt{J}\int d\phi\cos\phi\psi_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\theta q(2\beta\tau)\int dJ\omega^{\prime}J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]\int\cos\phi\sin(\phi-\omega(t-2\tau))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\pi\beta\theta q\tau\int dJ\omega^{\prime}J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]\sin(\omega(t-2\tau))

where we defined

t_{1}^{3}=(t-\tau)^{3}+\tau^{3}

Consider the complexified form of the above integral

I={\rm Im}[\int dJ\omega^{\prime}J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]\exp[i(\omega(t-2\tau))]

In Ref.
[
15
]
, the statement is made that assuming that
\psi_{0}^{\prime}
is monotonic, a Schwarz
inequality can be used to show that the maximum of the integral occurs at
t=2\tau
where the
complex exponential factor assumes its largest value of unity.

In most of the following, we consider the initial distribution and the action dependent
transverse angular frequency to be of the form

\psi_{0}(J)=\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}],\;\;\;\;\omega(J)=\omega_{0}+\omega^{\prime}J

(7.15)

7.1
Constant diffusion coefficient

D(J)=D_{0}

The dipole moment at time
t
is

\langle x\rangle(t)=\frac{\pi\beta\theta q\tau\omega^{\prime}}{2\pi J_{0}^{2}}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]{\rm Im}[\exp[i\omega_{0}(t-2\tau)]\int J^{2}\exp[-\frac{i\omega^{\prime}(t-2\tau)J}{J_{0}}]dJ

Using the result

\int_{0}^{\infty}dJJ^{2}\exp[-aJ]=\frac{2}{a^{3}}

we have

\langle x\rangle(t)=\beta\theta q\tau\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]{\rm Im}[\frac{\exp[i\Phi]}{(1+i\xi)^{3}}]

Expanding we have

\langle x\rangle(t)=\frac{\beta\theta q\tau\omega^{\prime}J_{0}}{(1+\xi^{2})^{3/2}}[\xi(3-\xi^{2})\cos\Phi+(1-3\xi^{2})\sin\Phi]\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]

At
t=2\tau
, both
\xi and\Phi
vanish, hence so does
\langle x\rangle
. However the echo amplitude
achieves its maximum in the vicinity of
2\tau
.

The maximum amplitude near
t=2\tau
is

\langle x\rangle(2\tau)=\beta\theta q\tau\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\exp[-\frac{2}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{3}]

(7.16)

As a function of the delay
\tau
, the amplitude has a maximum at a delay
\tau_{m}
such that

\tau_{m}^{3}=\frac{1}{2D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}}

(7.17)

and the maximum amplitude at this delay is

\langle x\rangle(2\tau_{m})=\frac{\beta\theta qJ_{0}}{2D_{0}\omega^{\prime}}

(7.18)

From a knowledge of both
\tau_{m}
and the amplitude at
2\tau_{m}
, both the diffusion coefficient and the detuning
can be determined.

Recap of assumptions

1.

The dipole kick amplitude is much less than the beam size

\beta\theta\ll\sigma

2.

The quadrupole kick is also small enough for the 1st order Taylor expansion to be valid or that it satisfies

q\ll

3.

The distribution function obeys the diffusion equation in action
J
alone

\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\psi=\frac{\partial}{\partial J}[D(J)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial J}]

4.

The diffusion equation is solved under the approximations

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial J}[D(J)\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial J}]

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle(\omega^{\prime}t)^{2}D(J)\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial v^{2}}

\displaystyle|t\frac{\partial}{\partial J}[D(J)\omega^{\prime}\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial v}]|

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle(\omega^{\prime}t)^{2}D(J)\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial v^{2}}

5.

In the complete expression for the distribution
\psi_{4}
right after the quad kick is

\psi_{4}=\theta q(\omega^{\prime}\tau)\sqrt{2\beta J}J\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{3}]\frac{1}{2}[\sin(3\phi-\omega\tau)+\sin(\phi+\omega\tau)]

The 3rd harmonic term is dropped in the evolution of the distribution with diffusion following the quad kick because it is a sextupole term which will not contribute to the dipole echo.

Q: What are the consequences of dropping this term?

6.

In the diffusion equation for
\psi_{5}
, the assumptions are

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial J}[D(J)\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J}]

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle(\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau))^{2}D(J)\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{5}}{\partial v^{2}}

\displaystyle|(t-\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial J}[D(J)\omega^{\prime}\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial v}]|

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle(\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau))^{2}D(J)\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{5}}{\partial v^{2}}

Are these inequalities satisfied at time
t=\tau_{m},2\tau_{m}
?

7.

The solution of the diffusion equation

\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial t}=(\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau))^{2}D(J)\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{5}}{\partial u^{2}}

(7.19)

is of the form

\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t\geq\tau)=A\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}(t-\tau)^{3}]\sin(u+c)

has to match the solution at
t=\tau
. i.e.

\psi_{5}(J,\phi,\tau)=\psi_{4}(J,\phi)

This can be done only by dropping the
\sin(3\phi-\omega\tau)
term in
\psi_{4}
.

Is it possible to solve the equation Eq. (
7.19
) by use of a Fourier series with more terms?

Check inequalities for
\psi_{2}

Is this satisfied for
0\leq t\leq\tau

|\frac{\partial}{\partial J}[D(J)\frac{\partial\psi_{2}}{\partial J}]|\ll|(\omega^{\prime}t)^{2}D(J)\frac{\partial^{2}\psi_{2}}{\partial v^{2}}|

where

\psi_{2}=\theta\sqrt{2\beta}(\sqrt{J}\psi_{0}^{\prime})\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D(J)(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t^{3}]\sin v

For simplicity we assume that
D
and
\omega^{\prime}
are constants

D(J)=D_{0},\;\;\;\omega=\omega_{0}+\omega^{\prime}J

In this case, the inequality can be written as

|\frac{J^{2}}{J_{0}^{2}}-\frac{J}{J_{0}}-\frac{1}{4}|\ll(\omega^{{}^{\prime}}t)^{2}

The above can be written as

(\frac{J}{J_{0}}-\frac{1}{2})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\ll(\omega^{{}^{\prime}}t)^{2}J^{2}

Is this satisfied at
t=\tau_{m}=[(1/(2D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}))]^{1/3}
? The above inequality becomes

\frac{J}{J_{0}}\ll\frac{1}{2}+[\frac{1}{2}+J^{2}(\frac{\omega^{\prime}}{2D_{0}})^{2/3}]^{1/2}

Note: Explore the consequences of this inequality. Does it set limits on
D_{0}
?

Diffusion constant in terms of the optimum delay

Writing

\omega^{\prime}=\frac{\mu}{J_{0}}\omega_{rev}=\frac{\mu}{\epsilon}\omega_{rev}

where we have used
J_{0}=\epsilon
,
Then, the constant coefficient
D_{0}
is

D_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(\frac{\epsilon}{\omega_{rev}})^{2}\frac{1}{\mu^{2}\tau_{m}^{3}}

(7.20)

Optimum detuning
\mu_{m}

The maximum of the echo amplitude as a function of the detuning occurs when
(\omega_{m}^{\prime})^{2}=(3/4)/(D_{0}\tau^{3})
or
the diffusion coefficient
D_{0}
is

D_{0}=\frac{3}{16}\frac{1}{\mu_{m}^{2}\tau^{3}}

(7.21)

7.1.1
FWHM calculation

At a time
t=2\tau+\Delta t_{H}
, the amplitude falls to half the value at
t=2\tau
, hence

\frac{\beta\theta qJ_{0}\omega^{\prime}\tau}{[1+(\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\Delta t_{H})^{2}]^{3/2}}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}[(t+\Delta t_{H})^{3}+\tau^{3}]]=\frac{1}{2}\beta\theta qJ_{0}\omega^{\prime}\tau\exp[-\frac{2}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{3}]

Expanding to 1st order in
\Delta t_{H}/\tau
, so that
(\tau+\Delta t_{H})^{3}\simeq\tau^{3}+3\Delta t_{H}\tau^{2}
, we solve for

\Delta t_{H}
from the equation

\frac{1}{[1+(\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\Delta t_{H})^{2}]^{3/2}}\exp[-D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{2}\Delta t_{H}]=\frac{1}{2}

or equivalently

2^{2/3}\exp[-\frac{2}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{2}\Delta t_{H}]=1+(\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\Delta t_{H})^{2}

Expanding the exponential and keeping only to linear order in
D_{0}
, we have

(\omega^{\prime}J_{0})^{2}(\Delta t_{H})^{2}+\frac{2}{3}2^{2/3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{2}\Delta t_{H}-(2^{2/3}-1)=0

(7.22)

which on assuming that
D_{0}^{2}
is small enough to satisfy

(2^{2/3}-1)J_{0}^{2}\gg[\frac{2^{2/3}}{3}D_{0}\omega^{\prime}\tau^{2}]^{2}

we have for the full width
\Delta t_{FWHM}=2\Delta t_{H}

\Delta t_{H}=2\frac{\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}}{\omega^{\prime}J_{0}}-\frac{2^{5/3}}{3}D_{0}(\frac{\tau}{J_{0}})^{2}+\frac{2^{4/3}}{9\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}}(\frac{\tau^{4}\omega^{\prime}}{J_{0}^{3}})D_{0}^{2}

(7.23)

where again, the last term in
D_{0}^{2}
can be dropped.

7.1.2
Escape time

This time is given by

t_{esc}=\int dJ\frac{J}{D(J)}

(7.24)

With
D(J)=D_{0}
, we have

t_{esc}=\frac{J_{A}^{2}}{2D_{0}}

(7.25)

where
J_{A}
is the action at the aperture.
Express
J_{A}
in terms of the amplitude at the aperture; see Edwards and Syphers

Clearly, the escape time must be larger than the decoherence time
\tau_{D}\simeq 1/(\omega^{\prime}J_{0})=1/(\mu\omega_{rev})

or

\tau_{D}\ll t_{esc},\Rightarrow D_{0}\ll\frac{1}{2}\omega_{rev}\mu J_{A}^{2}

(7.26)

7.1.3
Summary of results with a constant diffusion coefficient

The amplitude of the echo at
t\simeq 2\tau

\langle x\rangle^{echo,maxamp}=\beta\theta qJ_{0}\omega^{\prime}\tau\exp[-\frac{2}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{3}]

(7.27)

assuming that
\omega(J)=\omega_{0}+\omega^{\prime}J
,
and the initial distribution is
\psi_{0}(J)=\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-J/J_{0}]
.
The constant angular frequency coefficient
\omega^{\prime}=(\omega_{rev}/\epsilon)\mu
, where
\mu
is the
detuning coefficient defined by the action dependent tune

\nu(J)-\nu_{0}=\frac{J}{\epsilon}\mu

Here
\epsilon
is the rms un-normalized emittance and
\omega_{rev}
is the angular revolution frequency.

At other times
t
, the echo amplitude behaves as

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle^{echo,amp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta\theta qJ_{0}\omega^{\prime}\tau}{(1+\xi^{2})^{3/2}}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]

(7.28)

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}(t-2\tau)J_{0},\;\;\;t_{1}^{3}=(t-\tau)^{3}+\tau^{3}

(7.29)

To find the half width time
\Delta t_{H}
at which the amplitude falls to half its maximum, we have

\frac{1}{(1+(\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\Delta t_{H})^{2})^{3/2}}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}((\tau+\Delta t_{H})^{3}+\tau^{3})]=\frac{1}{2}\exp[-\frac{2}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{3}]

Keeping terms to first order in
D_{0}
results in a quadratic for
\Delta t_{H}
. Taking the negative root
and defining
\Delta\tau_{FWHM}=2|\Delta t_{H}|
,
it follows that the FWHM of the echo pulse is

\Delta\tau_{FWHM}=\frac{2\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}}{\omega^{\prime}J_{0}}+\frac{2^{5/3}}{3}D_{0}(\frac{\tau}{J_{0}})^{2}+O(D_{0}^{2})

(7.30)

The width of the echo pulse vanishes for delays
\tau\geq\tau_{max}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\Delta\tau_{FWHM}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle 0,\;\;\;{\rm for}\;\;\;\tau\geq\tau_{max}

\displaystyle\tau_{max}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[(\frac{6\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}}{2^{5/3}})(\frac{J_{0}}{\omega^{\prime}D_{0}})\right]^{1/2}=\left[(\frac{6\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}}{2^{5/3}})(\frac{1}{\omega_{rev}\mu D_{0}})\right]^{1/2}\frac{\epsilon}{2}

(7.31)

One of the conditions for the solution in Eq.(
7.27
) to be valid is that the diffusion
must be sufficiently weak as to satisfy

D_{0}\ll D_{max}=2\omega_{rev}\mu\epsilon^{2}

(7.32)

If the echo amplitude is measured as a function of the detuning, the maximum amplitude occurs
at a value
\mu=\mu_{m}
such that

D_{0}=\frac{3}{4}(\frac{\epsilon}{\omega_{rev}})^{2}\frac{1}{\tau^{3}\mu_{m}^{2}}

(7.33)

This relation determines the diffusion coefficient
D_{0}
, given the delay
\tau
and
the optimum detuning
\mu_{m}
.

7.2
Polynomial diffusion coefficients

Consider the diffusion coefficient to be of the form

D(J)=D_{0}+\sum_{n=1}D_{n}(\frac{J}{J_{0}})^{n}

(7.34)

The average dipole moment is given by

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int x\psi_{5}(J,\phi)dJd\phi

(7.35)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\pi\beta\theta q\omega^{\prime}\tau\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]{\rm Im}[e^{[i\Phi_{0}]}\int J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}\sum_{n}D_{n}(J/J_{0})^{n}]e^{[i\Phi_{1}J]}dJ

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta\theta q\mu\tau\omega_{rev}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]{\rm Im}[e^{[i\Phi_{0}]}\int z^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}\sum_{n}D_{n}z^{n}]e^{[i\Phi_{1}J_{0}z]}dz

where

\Phi_{0}=\omega_{0}(t-2\tau),\;\;\;\;\Phi_{1}=\omega^{\prime}(t-2\tau)

and we used

\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)=-\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}^{2}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}]

The general form of the integral is

\displaystyle{\rm Int}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dzz^{2}\exp[-az]\exp[-\sum_{n}b_{n}z^{n}]

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-i\Phi_{1}J_{0}=1-\mu\omega_{rev}(t-2\tau),\;\;\;b_{n}=\frac{1}{3}D_{n}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}

7.3
Constant and linear diffusion coefficient

Here we assume that the diffusion is of the form

D(J)=D_{0}+D_{1}(\frac{J}{J_{0}})

(7.36)

where
D_{0}
and
D_{1}
have the same dimensions.

The integral is

{\rm Int}(J)=\int dJJ^{2}\exp[-(a+b_{1})J]=\frac{2}{(a+b_{1})^{3}}

where

a+b_{1}=\frac{1}{J_{0}}[(1+\frac{1}{3}D_{1}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3})-i\Phi_{1}J_{0}]

in this case, the dipole moment is given by

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta\theta q\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]{\rm Im}[\frac{e^{i\Phi}}{(\alpha-i\xi})^{3}]

(7.37)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta q\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]\frac{[(3\alpha^{2}-\xi^{2})\xi\cos\Phi+(\alpha^{2}-3\xi^{2})\alpha\sin\Phi]}{(\alpha^{2}+\xi^{2})^{3}}

(7.38)

\displaystyle t_{1}^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(t-\tau)^{3}+\tau^{3},\;\;\;\Phi=\omega_{0}(t-2\tau)

\displaystyle\alpha

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{1}{3}D_{1}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3},\;\;\;\xi=\omega^{\prime}(t-2\tau)J_{0}=\omega_{rev}\mu(t-2\tau)

(7.39)

The amplitude of this echo is determined by the amplitude of
e^{i\Phi}/(\alpha-i\xi)^{3}
. Since

\Phi
is a fast varying phase while
\xi
is slowly varying, it follows that

ampl[e^{i\Phi}/(\alpha-i\xi)^{3}]=ampl[1//(\alpha-i\xi)^{3}]=1/(\alpha^{2}+\xi^{2})^{3/2}
.
Hence the amplitude of the echo is

\langle x\rangle(t)^{amp}=\frac{\beta\theta q\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}}{(\alpha^{2}+\xi^{2})^{3/2}}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]

(7.40)

At the time of the 1st echo,
t=2\tau
, the echo amplitude is

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle(2\tau)^{amp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta\theta q\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}}{\alpha_{1}^{3}}\exp[-\frac{2}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{3}]

(7.41)

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{2}{3}D_{1}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{3}

(7.42)

At the time of the 2nd echo,
t=4\tau
, the different parameters are

\displaystyle t_{1}^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 28\tau^{3},\;\;\;\Phi_{2}=2\omega_{0}\tau

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+\frac{28}{3}D_{1}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{3},\;\;\;\xi_{2}=2\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}

and the echo amplitude is

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle(4\tau)^{amp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta\theta q\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}}{(\alpha_{2}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2})^{3/2}}\exp[-\frac{28}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{3}]

(7.43)

At the 3rd echo
t=6\tau
, the parameters are

\displaystyle t_{1}^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 126\tau^{3},\;\;\;\Phi_{3}=4\omega_{0}\tau

\displaystyle\alpha_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+42D_{1}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{3},\;\;\;\xi_{2}=4\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}

and the echo amplitude is

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle(6\tau)^{amp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta\theta q\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}}{(\alpha_{3}^{2}+\xi_{3}^{2})^{3/2}}\exp[-42D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{3}]

(7.44)

7.3.1
Summary of results with constant and linear diffusion coefficient

7.3.2
Maxima of the echo amplitude as functions of detuning and delay time

Using

\omega^{\prime}=\frac{\omega_{rev}}{J_{0}}\mu=\frac{\omega_{rev}}{\epsilon}\mu

define the following variables of the diffusion coefficients

d_{0}=\frac{2}{3}D_{0}(\frac{\omega_{rev}}{\epsilon})^{2},\;\;\;d_{1}=\frac{2}{3}D_{1}(\frac{\omega_{rev}}{\epsilon})^{2}

(7.45)

Then the echo amplitude at
t=2\tau
scaled by the dipole and quad kick strengths is

\frac{\langle x\rangle(2\tau)^{amp}}{\beta\theta q\omega_{rev}}=\frac{\mu\tau}{(1+d_{1}\mu^{2}\tau^{3})^{3}}\exp[-d_{0}\mu^{2}\tau^{3}]

(7.46)

Since we have two undetermined coefficients
d_{0},d_{1}
, we need two measured variables.

As a function of the time delay, this amplitude has a maximum at a delay
\tau=\tau_{m}
, such that
the two coefficients can be related as

d_{1}=\frac{1-3d_{0}\mu^{2}\tau_{m}^{3}}{\mu^{2}\tau_{m}^{3}(8+3d_{0}\mu^{2}\tau_{m}^{3})}

(7.47)

It is understood that
\mu
is held fixed at
\mu_{f}
while finding the optimum delay
\tau_{m}

Define

c_{\tau}=\mu_{f}^{2}\tau_{m}^{3}

(7.48)

Substituting this into the equation for the relative amplitude, we have using

1+d_{1}c_{\tau}=9/(8+3d_{0}c_{\tau})
for the maximum amplitude obtained at the delay

\tau_{m}

\frac{\langle x\rangle_{max}(\tau_{m})}{\beta\theta}=\omega_{rev}q\mu\tau_{m}[\frac{8+3d_{0}c_{\tau}}{9}]^{3}\exp[-d_{0}c_{\tau}]

(7.49)

This equation can be solved for
d_{0}
and subsequently
d_{1}
can be found. We require that the solution
for
d_{0}
obey
3d_{0}c_{\tau}\leq 1
in order for
d_{1}\geq 0
.

Similarly, as a function of the detuning, the amplitude has a maximum at
\mu=\mu_{m}
such that

d_{1}=\frac{1-2d_{0}\mu_{m}^{2}\tau^{3}}{\mu_{m}^{2}\tau^{3}(5+2d_{0}\mu_{m}^{2}\tau^{3})}

(7.50)

Here
\tau
is held fixed at
\tau_{f}
while finding the optimum in
\mu
. Define

c_{\mu}=\mu_{m}^{2}\tau_{f}^{3}

(7.51)

Again, substituting back for
d_{1}
, we can write the maximum relative amplitude at
\mu_{m}
as

\frac{\langle x\rangle_{max}(\mu_{m})}{\beta\theta}=\omega_{rev}q\mu_{m}\tau_{f}[\frac{5+2d_{0}c_{\mu}}{6}]^{3}\exp[-d_{0}c_{\mu}]

(7.52)

Here
d_{1}\geq 0
requires that the solution for
d_{0}
obey
2d_{0}c_{\mu}\leq 1
.

If both
\mu_{m}
and
\tau_{m}
are measured, then the diffusion coefficient
d_{0}
can be found from

\frac{1-3d_{0}c_{\tau}}{c_{\tau}(8+3d_{0}c_{\tau})}=\frac{1-2d_{0}c_{\mu}}{c_{\mu}(5+2d_{0}c_{\mu})}

(7.53)

which has the solutions

d_{0}=\frac{1}{12c_{\mu}c_{\tau}}\left[2c_{\mu}+3c_{\tau}\pm\sqrt{\frac{(2c_{\mu}-3c_{\tau})(2c_{\mu}^{2}+67c_{\mu}c_{\tau}+3c_{\tau}^{2})}{c_{\mu}-c_{\tau}}}\right]

(7.54)

The negative root is permissible if
d_{0}\geq 0
.
Once
d_{0}
is determined,
d_{1}
can be determined from either of Equations (
7.47
) or
(
7.50
). Positivity of
d_{1}
requires that the above solution obey

d_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2c_{\mu}},\;\;\;\;d_{0}\leq\frac{1}{3c_{\tau}}

Case

D_{1}=0

As a function of the delay
\tau
, the maximum amplitude occurs at
d_{0}=1/(3c_{\tau})
or

D_{0}=\frac{1}{2}(\frac{\epsilon}{\omega_{rev}})^{2}\frac{1}{\mu^{2}\tau_{m}^{3}}

(7.55)

while the solution for optimum detuning
\mu_{m}
gives
d_{0}=1/(2c_{\mu})
which is the same
as the solution in Eq.(
7.33
).

Case

D_{0}=0

The optimum delay
\tau_{m}
occurs at
d_{1}=1/(8c_{\tau})
or

D_{1}=\frac{3}{16}(\frac{\epsilon}{\omega_{rev}})^{2}\frac{1}{\mu^{2}\tau_{m}^{3}}

(7.56)

while the optimum detuning
\mu_{m}
occurs at
d_{1}=1/(5c_{\mu})
or

D_{1}=\frac{3}{10}(\frac{\epsilon}{\omega_{rev}})^{2}\frac{1}{\mu_{m}^{2}\tau^{3}}

(7.57)

7.3.3
Full width at half maximum

We make the following approximations

•

D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{2}\Delta t_{h}\ll 1
so that we can expand to 1st order

\exp[-\frac{2}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{2}\Delta t_{h}]\simeq 1-\frac{2}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau^{2}\Delta t_{h}

•

(\omega^{\prime}\tau)^{4}[(2^{5/3}/3)D_{0}+2D_{1}/\alpha_{1}]^{2}\ll 4(2^{2/3}-1)[(\omega^{\prime}J_{0})^{2}+D_{1}^{2}(\omega^{\prime}\tau)^{4}]/\alpha^{2}
, so that
