\displaystyle\sqrt{4(2^{2/3}-1)[(\omega^{\prime}J_{0})^{2}+D_{1}^{2}(\omega^{\prime}\tau)^{4}]/\alpha_{1}^{2}+(\omega^{\prime}\tau)^{4}[(2^{5/3}/3)D_{0}+2D_{1}/\alpha_{1}]^{2}}

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{(2^{2/3}-1)[(\omega^{\prime}J_{0})^{2}+D_{1}^{2}(\omega^{\prime}\tau)^{4}]}/\alpha_{1}+\frac{(\omega^{\prime}\tau)^{4}[(2^{5/3}/3)D_{0}+2D_{1}/\alpha_{1}]^{2}}{4\sqrt{(2^{2/3}-1)[(\omega^{\prime}J_{0})^{2}+D_{1}^{2}(\omega^{\prime}\tau)^{4}]}}\alpha_{1}

In the special case that
D_{1}=0
, this implies

D_{0}\ll\frac{\sqrt{(2^{2/3}-1)}}{2^{2/3}/3)}\frac{J_{0}}{\omega^{\prime}\tau^{2}}

while for the case
D_{0}=0
, this implies

D_{1}\ll\sqrt{\frac{2^{2/3}-1}{2-2^{2/3}}}\frac{J_{0}}{\omega^{\prime}\tau^{2}}

•

Terms of order
O(D_{0}^{2}),O(D_{1}^{2}),O(D_{0}D_{1})
and higher can be dropped,

Under these assumptions, we find for the full width at half maximum

\Delta t_{FWHM}=2\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}(\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\omega^{\prime}J_{0}})+2(\frac{\alpha_{1}\tau}{J_{0}})^{2}\left[\frac{2^{2/3}}{3}D_{0}+\frac{D_{1}}{\alpha_{1}}\right]

(7.58)

In terms of the coefficients
d_{0},d_{1}
, we have

D_{n}=\frac{3}{2}(\frac{\epsilon}{\omega_{rev}})^{2}d_{n},\;\;\;\alpha_{1}=1+\mu^{2}\tau^{3}d_{1}

and we can write

\Delta t_{FWHM}=2\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}(\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\omega_{rev}\mu})+3(\frac{\alpha_{1}\tau}{\omega_{rev}})^{2}\left[\frac{2^{2/3}}{3}d_{0}+\frac{d_{1}}{\alpha_{1}}\right]

(7.59)

This can be used to solve for
d_{0}
as

d_{0}=\frac{3}{2^{2/3}}\left\{\frac{1}{3}(\frac{\omega_{rev}}{\alpha_{1}\tau})^{2}\left[\Delta t_{FWHM}-2\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}(\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\omega_{rev}\mu})\right]-\frac{d_{1}}{\alpha_{1}}\right\}

(7.60)

If
D_{1}=0
, then
\alpha_{1}=1
and Eq.(
7.58
) reduces to Eq.(
7.30
) found in
the previous section.

If
D_{0}=0
, then

\Delta t_{FWHM}=2\alpha_{1}\left[\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}(\frac{1}{\omega^{\prime}J_{0}})+D_{1}(\frac{\tau}{J_{0}})^{2}\right]=\alpha_{1}\left[2\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}(\frac{1}{\omega_{rev}\mu})+3(\frac{\tau}{\omega_{rev}})^{2}d_{1}\right]

(7.61)

Vanishing echo pulse width

The pulse width will vanish for delay times
\tau\geq\tau_{max}
for a given
\mu
, when

\frac{2^{2/3}}{3}D_{0}+D_{1}=\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}\frac{J_{0}}{\omega^{\prime}\tau_{max}^{2}}

(7.62)

Here we dropped a term of
O(D_{0}D_{1})
, i.e. we approximated
\alpha_{1}D_{0}\simeq D_{0}
.
Hence, this relation can be used for another relation between
D_{0},D_{1}

given
\tau_{max}
. Alternatively, if the pulse width vanishes for
\mu\geq\mu_{max}
given
\tau
,
the same relation above can be used.

In terms of the scaled coefficients
d_{0},d_{1}
(they have dimension [
T^{-3}
], the above can be
written as

\frac{2^{2/3}}{3}d_{0}+d_{1}=\frac{2}{3}\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}\frac{\omega_{rev}}{\mu\tau_{max}^{2}}

(7.63)

However, given the many approximations needed to obtain the FWHM width in this case, it would
be preferred to use other constraints on obtaining the diffusion coefficients.

7.3.4
Escape time

This time is given by

t_{esc}=\int_{0}^{J_{a}}dJ\frac{J}{D(J)}=\int_{0}^{J_{a}}dJ\frac{J}{D_{0}+D_{1}(J/J_{0})}

(7.64)

where
J_{a}
is the action at the aperture.
Introducing a variable
y(J)=1+\frac{D_{1}}{D_{0}}\frac{J}{J_{0}}
, and
y_{a}=y(J_{a})
, we have

\displaystyle\tau_{esc}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{0}(\frac{J_{0}}{D_{1}})^{2}\int_{1}^{y_{a}}(1-\frac{1}{y})dy

(7.65)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{0}(\frac{J_{0}}{D_{1}})^{2}[\frac{D_{1}}{D_{0}}\frac{J_{a}}{J_{0}}-\ln(1+\frac{D_{1}}{D_{0}}\frac{J_{a}}{J_{0}})]

Ignoring the slowly growing log term, to leading order the escape time is

\tau_{esc}\approx\frac{J_{0}J_{a}}{D_{1}}

Note that this leading order term does not depend on the constant diffusion coefficient
D_{0}
.

7.3.5
Emittance growth

The diffusion coefficients determine the emittance growth as follows.
From the density distribution function
\psi(J)
of an unperturbed beam with diffusion coefficient

D(J)
, the average action as a function of time is

\langle J\rangle(t)=\int_{0}^{J_{a}}J\psi(J,t)dJ

(7.66)

where
J_{a}
is the action at the absorbing boundary.

Hence

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\langle J\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int J\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}dJ=\int J\frac{\partial}{\partial J}[D(J)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial J}]dJ

(7.67)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle JD(J)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial J}|_{0}^{J_{a}}-\int D(J)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial J}=-\int D(J)\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial J}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-D(J)\psi|_{0}^{J_{a}}+\int D^{\prime}(J)\psi dJ

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D(0)\psi(0)+\int D^{\prime}(J)\psi dJ

In the 2nd and 3rd lines, the integrated terms at
J=J_{a}
vanish if we assume that the slope

\partial\psi/\partial J
and
\psi
vanish at the boundary faster than the combination
JD(J)
and

D(J)
respectively.

Now, considering the case where
D(J)=D_{0}+D_{1}(J/J_{0})
, and
\psi(J)=\exp(-J/J_{0})/(2\pi J_{0})
,

\int\psi dJ=1/(2\pi)
, we have for the emittance growth rate

\frac{d\epsilon}{dt}=\frac{d\langle J\rangle}{dt}=\frac{1}{\pi\epsilon_{0}}(D_{0}+D_{1})

(7.68)

The above analysis needs to be revised for the following reasons

•

The distribution function with diffusion will in general also depend on the phase variable

\phi

•

Assuming that the action is still conserved, we would then have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\langle J\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int J\frac{d\psi(J,\phi,t)}{dt}dJ

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int J(\frac{\partial\psi(J,\phi,t)}{\partial t}+\frac{\partial\psi(J,\phi,t)}{\partial\phi}\frac{d\phi}{dt})dJ

This involves the equation of motion for
\phi
.

•

Another concern is that with only
D_{0}
, this predicts that the emittance grows linearly
with time. However, with a constant diffusion, one expects the underlying variable to grow
with time as
\sqrt{t}
.

That is a shortcoming of this calculation. Does the inclusion of the
\dot{\phi}(\partial\psi/\partial\phi)
term correct this problem?

7.3.6
Multiple Echoes

Multiple echoes may be observed at multiples of
2\tau
. We consider here the 2nd echo at time

4\tau
.

We write the echo amplitudes in terms of the scaled coefficients
d_{0},d_{1}
. We have

\langle x\rangle^{amp}(t)=\frac{\beta\theta q\omega_{rev}\mu\tau}{(\alpha^{2}+\xi^{2})^{3/2}}\exp[-\frac{1}{2}d_{0}\mu^{2}t_{1}^{3}],\;\;\;\;\alpha=1+\frac{1}{2}d_{1}\mu^{2}\tau^{3}

(7.69)

Hence the echo amplitude at
t=4\tau
(
t_{1}^{3}=28\tau^{3}
) is

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle^{amp}(4\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta\theta q\omega_{rev}\mu\tau}{(\alpha_{2}^{2}+\xi_{2}^{2})^{3/2}}\exp[-14d_{0}\mu^{2}\tau^{3}]

(7.70)

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+14d_{1}\mu^{2}\tau^{3},\;\;\;\xi_{2}=2\omega_{rev}\mu\tau

(7.71)

while for the third echo at
t=6\tau
,
t_{1}^{3}=126\tau^{3}
, and

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle^{amp}(6\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta\theta q\omega_{rev}\mu\tau}{(\alpha_{3}^{2}+\xi_{3}^{2})^{3/2}}\exp[-63d_{0}\mu^{2}\tau^{3}]

(7.72)

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+63d_{1}\mu^{2}\tau^{3},\;\;\;\xi_{2}=2\omega_{rev}\mu\tau

(7.73)

Hence the ratio of the amplitudes at the 2nd echo and 1st echo is

\frac{\langle x\rangle^{amp}(4\tau)}{\langle x\rangle^{amp}(2\tau)}=\frac{(1+d_{1}\mu^{2}\tau^{3})^{3}}{[(1+14d_{1}\mu^{2}\tau^{3})^{2}+(2\omega_{rev}\mu\tau)^{2}]^{3/2}}\exp[-13d_{0}\mu^{2}\tau^{3}]

(7.74)

and the ratio of amplitudes of the 3rd echo and 1st echo is

\frac{\langle x\rangle^{amp}(6\tau)}{\langle x\rangle^{amp}(2\tau)}=\frac{(1+d_{1}\mu^{2}\tau^{3})^{3}}{[(1+63d_{1}\mu^{2}\tau^{3})^{2}+(4\omega_{rev}\mu\tau)^{2}]^{3/2}}\exp[-62d_{0}\mu^{2}\tau^{3}]

(7.75)

Since fewer approximations were used in obtaining this, it may preferable to use this constraint on
the coefficients
d_{0},d_{1}
instead of the pulse width.

Case
D_{1}=0

\frac{\langle x\rangle^{amp}(4\tau)}{\langle x\rangle^{amp}(2\tau)}=\frac{1}{[1+(2\omega_{rev}\mu\tau)^{2}]^{3/2}}\exp[-13d_{0}\mu^{2}\tau^{3}]

(7.76)

Case
D_{0}=0

\frac{\langle x\rangle^{amp}(4\tau)}{\langle x\rangle^{amp}(2\tau)}=\frac{(1+d_{1}\mu^{2}\tau^{3})^{3}}{[(1+14d_{1}\mu^{2}\tau^{3})^{2}+(2\omega_{rev}\mu\tau)^{2}]^{3/2}}

(7.77)

7.4
Constant, Linear and quadratic diffusion coefficients

Here we have

D(J)=D_{0}+D_{2}(\frac{J}{J_{0}})^{2}

(7.78)

From Eq.(
7.35
) it follows that the dipole moment is

\langle x(t)\rangle=-\pi\beta\theta q\omega^{\prime}\tau\exp[-\frac{1}{3}D_{0}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}]{\rm Im}[e^{[i\Phi_{0}]}\int J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}D_{2}(J/J_{0})^{2}]e^{[i\Phi_{1}J]}dJ

(7.79)

The amplitude of the moment is given by setting
|e^{i\Phi_{0}}|=1=|e^{i\Phi_{1}J}|
. Using

\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{2}\exp[-(ax+bx^{2})]dx=\frac{1}{8b^{5/2}}[\sqrt{\pi}(a^{2}+2b)e^{a^{2}/(4b)}{\rm Erfc}(\frac{a}{2\sqrt{b}})-2a\sqrt{b}]

The general time dependent form of the echo at time
t=2\tau+\Delta t
where
\Delta t
can have either
sign is

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle^{amp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta_{G}\theta q\omega_{rev}\mu\tau\exp[-\frac{1}{2}d_{0}\mu^{2}t_{1}^{3}]{\rm Im}[e^{i\Phi_{0}}H_{02}(\Delta t)]

(7.80)

\displaystyle H_{02}(\Delta t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int z^{2}\exp[-a_{0}z-\frac{1}{2}b_{2}z^{2}]dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\frac{1}{b_{2}})^{5/2}\left\{\sqrt{\pi}\left[a_{0}^{2}+b_{2}\right]\exp(\frac{a_{0}^{2}}{2b_{2}}){\rm Erfc}(\frac{a_{0}}{\sqrt{2b_{2}}})-a_{0}\sqrt{2b_{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle a_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-i\mu\Delta t\omega_{rev}),\;\;\;b_{2}=d_{2}\mu^{2}t_{1}^{3}=d_{2}\mu^{2}[(\tau+\Delta t)^{3}+\tau^{3}]

Here Erfc is the complementary error function.

Hence at time
t=2\tau
where
t_{1}^{3}=2\tau^{3}

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle^{amp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta_{G}\theta q\omega_{rev}\mu\tau\exp[-\frac{1}{2}d_{0}\mu^{2}t_{1}^{3}]H_{02}(0)]

(7.82)

\displaystyle H_{02}(0)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int z^{2}\exp[-a_{0}z-\frac{1}{2}b_{2}z^{2}]dz

(7.83)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\frac{1}{b_{2}})^{5/2}\left\{\sqrt{\pi}\left[1+b_{2}\right]\exp(\frac{1}{2b_{2}}){\rm Erfc}(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2b_{2}}})-\sqrt{2b_{2}}\right\}

In the case

D(J)=D_{1}(\frac{J}{J_{0}})+D_{2}(\frac{J}{J_{0}})^{2}

The time dependent centroid position is

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\pi\beta\theta q\omega^{\prime}\tau{\rm Im}[e^{[i\Phi_{0}]}\int J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[-\frac{1}{3}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}(D_{1}(J/J_{0})+D_{2}(J/J_{0})^{2})]e^{[i\Phi_{1}J]}dJ

(7.84)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta\theta q\omega^{\prime}\tau{\rm Im}[e^{[i\Phi_{0}]}\int\frac{J}{J_{0}}^{2}\exp[-J/J_{0}]\exp[-\frac{1}{3}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}(D_{1}(J/J_{0})+D_{2}(J/J_{0})^{2})]e^{[i\Phi_{1}J]}dJ

(7.85)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi\beta\theta q\mu N_{\tau}{\rm Im}[e^{[i\Phi_{0}]}\int z^{2}\exp[-(1-i\Phi_{1}J_{0})z]-\frac{1}{3}(\omega^{\prime})^{2}t_{1}^{3}(D_{1}z+D_{2}z^{2})]dz

(7.86)

where we replaced

\tau=N_{\tau}T_{rev}

This evaluates to

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle^{amp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta_{G}\theta q\omega_{rev}\mu\tau{\rm Im}[e^{i\Phi_{0}}H_{12}(\Delta t)]

(7.88)

\displaystyle H_{12}(\Delta t)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int z^{2}\exp[-a_{1}z-\frac{1}{2}b_{2}z^{2}]dz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\frac{1}{b_{2}})^{5/2}\left\{\sqrt{\pi}\left[a_{1}^{2}+b_{2}\right]\exp(\frac{a_{1}^{2}}{2b_{2}}){\rm Erfc}(\frac{a_{1}}{\sqrt{2b_{2}}})-a_{1}\sqrt{2b_{2}}\right\}

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+b_{1})-i\mu\Delta t\omega_{rev},\;\;\;b_{1}=d_{1}\mu^{2}t_{1}^{3}=d_{1}\mu^{2}[(\tau+\Delta t)^{3}+\tau^{3}]

At
t=2\tau
, both
\Phi_{0},\Phi_{1}
vanish while
t_{1}^{3}=2\tau^{3}
.
Hence

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle^{amp}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta_{G}\theta q\omega_{rev}\mu\tau H_{12}(0)

(7.90)

\displaystyle a_{1}(0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+b_{1}(0)=1+2d_{1}\mu^{2}\tau^{3},\;\;\;b_{2}(0)=2d_{2}\mu^{2}\tau^{3}

At
nearby times
t=2\tau+\Delta t
,

\Phi_{0}=\omega_{0}\Delta t,\;\;\;\Phi_{1}J_{0}=\omega^{\prime}J_{0}(t-2\tau)=\mu\omega_{rev}\Delta t,\;\;\;\;t_{1}^{3}=(\tau+\Delta t)^{3}+\tau^{3}

If we consider times
\Delta t/\tau\ll 1
, then we can approximate

t_{3}\approx 2\tau^{3}+3\tau^{2}\Delta t

8
Scaled diffusion coefficient

From the expressions for the diffusion coefficients
D_{0},D_{1}
when one of them vanishes, it is clear
that the scale of the diffusion coefficients is set by the parameter

D_{sc}=(\frac{\epsilon}{\omega_{rev}})^{2}\frac{1}{\mu^{2}\tau^{3}}

(8.1)

The decoherence time
\tau_{D}=1/(\omega^{\prime}J_{0})=1/(\omega_{rev}\mu)
, hence

\omega_{rev}\mu\tau=\frac{\tau}{\tau_{D}}

and the diffusion scale is therefore determined by

D_{sc}=[\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{\tau}](\frac{\tau_{D}}{\tau})^{2}

(8.2)

In most cases,
D_{0},D_{1}\approx(0.1-1)D_{sc}
. Clearly, larger delay times
\tau
and smaller
decoherence times
\tau_{D}
lead to smaller
D_{sc}
which allows measuring weaker diffusion coefficients.

With RHIC parameters,
\tau_{D}=114
turns and typically
\tau/\tau_{D}\approx 4
. This ratio should
preferably be about 10, as seems to have been possible in the SPS experiment on transverse echoes.
For RHIC parameters,

D_{sc}=2.8\times 10^{-13}\;{\rm m^{2}/s}

(8.3)

Check of numerical assumptions made in deriving the pulse width

\mu=0.0014\Rightarrow\omega^{\prime}=\frac{2}{\epsilon_{0}}\omega_{rev}\mu=8.6\times 10^{9}{\rm rad/(m-s)}

•

D_{0}(\omega^{\prime}\tau)^{2}\Delta t_{h}\ll 1

If we take
\Delta t_{h}=10
turns

\Rightarrow D_{0}\ll 3.2\times 10^{-12}{\rm m^{2}/s}

•

When
D_{1}=0
then

\displaystyle D_{0}

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{(2^{2/3}-1)}}{2^{2/3}/3)}\frac{J_{0}}{\omega^{\prime}\tau^{2}}

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle 4.1\times 10^{-13}{\rm m^{2}/s}

•

When
D_{0}=0
then,

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2^{2/3}-1}{2-2^{2/3}}}\frac{J_{0}}{\omega^{\prime}\tau^{2}}

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle 3.3\times 10^{-13}{\rm m^{2}/s}

Since the echo amplitude as a function of the delay time is not available, here we use the following
two observations

•

Echo amplitude as a function of detuning with maximum at
\mu_{m}=0.001
.

•

Maximum delay time
\tau_{max}
before the echo disappears. For gold, the number is

\tau_{max}=550
turns. For copper, the number is
\tau_{max}=1000
turns.

We solve these two equations for
d_{0},d_{1}

\displaystyle d_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1-2d_{0}c_{\mu}}{c_{\mu}(5+2d_{0}c_{\mu})}

(8.4)

\displaystyle d_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2}{3}\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}\frac{\omega_{rev}}{\mu\tau_{max}^{2}}-\frac{2^{2/3}}{3}d_{0}

(8.5)

where as before :
c_{\mu}=\mu_{m}^{2}\tau^{3}
,
d_{i}=(8/3)(\omega_{rev}/\epsilon)^{2}D_{i}

9
Multiple quadrupole kicks

We now analyze multiple quad kicks. Some of the assumptions in the analysis with a single quad
kick have to be reconsidered.

Recap on the distribution functions

\psi_{0}(J)
is the initial distribution

\psi_{0}(J)=\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}]

(9.1)

\psi_{1}(J,\phi)
is the distribution function (DF) after the dipole kick

\psi_{1}(J,phi)=\psi_{0}(x,p-\beta_{K}\theta)\simeq\psi_{0}(J)+\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\sin\phi

(9.2)

\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)
is the DF at time
t
after the dipole kick

\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)=\psi_{0}(J)+\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\sin(\phi-\omega(J)t)

(9.3)

\psi_{3}(J,\phi,\tau)
is the DF at time
\tau
after the dipole kick

\psi_{3}(J,\phi,\tau)=\psi_{0}(J)+\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\sin(\phi-\omega(J)\tau)

(9.4)

\psi_{4}(J,\phi,\tau)
is the DF at time
\tau
right after the first quad kick

\displaystyle\psi_{4}(J,\phi,\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{3}(x,p+q_{1}x)\approx\psi_{3}(J,\phi,\tau)+q_{1}x\frac{\partial\psi_{3}}{\partial p}

(9.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{3}+q_{1}x[\frac{\partial\psi_{3}}{\partial J}\frac{\partial J}{\partial p}+\frac{\partial\psi_{3}}{\partial\phi}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial p}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{3}-q_{1}\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi[\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\sin\phi\frac{\partial\psi_{3}}{\partial J}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\beta J}}\cos\phi\frac{\partial\psi_{3}}{\partial\phi}]

In evaluating the terms in [], Chao had kept only the single term with
\omega^{\prime}\tau
arguing that for
long
\tau
, that term dominates. That argument perhaps cannot be made here, since the next kick
may be applied soon after
\tau
. Keeping all terms, and using

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi_{3}}{\partial J}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{\prime}+\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\left[(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}\sin(\phi-\omega(J)\tau)-\omega^{\prime}(J)\tau\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}\cos(\phi-\omega(J)\tau)\right]

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi_{3}}{\partial\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\cos(\phi-\omega(J)\tau)

Putting all terms together

\displaystyle\psi_{4}(J,\phi,\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(J)+\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\sin(\phi-\omega(J)\tau)

(9.6)

\displaystyle-q_{1}\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi\left\{\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\sin\phi\left[\psi_{0}^{\prime}+\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.[(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}\sin(\phi-\omega(J)\tau)-\omega^{\prime}(J)\tau\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}\cos(\phi-\omega(J)\tau)]\right]

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\beta J}}\cos\phi\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\cos(\phi-\omega(J)\tau)\right\}

We can drop the
\psi_{0}
term as it does not contribute to the dipole moment.
Hence the DF at time
mT_{rev}
after time
\tau
is

\psi_{5}(J,\phi,\tau+mT_{rev})=\psi_{4}(J,\phi-m\omega(J)T_{rev})

(9.7)

Introduce some shorthand notation

\displaystyle c_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cos(\phi-m\omega(J)T_{rev})\;\;\;s_{5}=\sin(\phi-m\omega(J)T_{rev})

\displaystyle c_{5\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cos(\phi-\omega(J)\tau-m\omega(J)T_{rev})\;\;\;s_{5\tau}=\sin(\phi-\omega(J)\tau-m\omega(J)T_{rev})

Then we can write

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(J,\phi,\tau+mT_{rev})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}s_{5\tau}

(9.8)

\displaystyle-q_{1}\sqrt{2\beta J}c_{5}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}s_{5}\left[\psi_{0}^{\prime}+\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}[(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}s_{5\tau}-\omega^{\prime}(J)\tau\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}c_{5\tau}]\right]\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{\beta_{K}}{\beta}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}c_{5}c_{5\tau}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}s_{5\tau}

\displaystyle-q_{1}\left\{Js_{25}\left[\psi_{0}^{\prime}+\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}[(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}s_{5\tau}-\omega^{\prime}(J)\tau\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}c_{5\tau}]\right]\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}c_{5}^{2}c_{5\tau}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}s_{5\tau}-q_{1}Js_{25}\psi_{0}^{\prime}

\displaystyle-q_{1}\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\left\{Js_{25}\left[(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}s_{5\tau}-\omega^{\prime}(J)\tau\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}c_{5\tau}\right]+\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}c_{5}^{2}c_{5\tau}\right\}

where we defined

s_{25}=2c_{5}s_{5}=\sin 2(\phi-m\omega(J)T_{rev})

Anticipating that the echo must involve the dipole kick, we drop the second term so that

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(J,\phi,\tau+mT_{rev})

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\left\{\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}s_{5\tau}\right.

(9.9)

\displaystyle\left.-q_{1}Js_{25}\left[(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}s_{5\tau}-\omega^{\prime}(J)\tau\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}c_{5\tau}\right]-q_{1}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}c_{5}^{2}c_{5\tau}\right\}

At time
\tau+mT_{rev}
, there is a second quad kick
q_{2}
which changes the DF to

\psi_{6}(J,\phi,\tau+mT_{rev})=\psi_{5}(x,p+q_{2}x)\approx\psi_{5}(J,\phi,\tau+mT_{rev})+q_{2}\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi[\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J}\frac{\partial J}{\partial p}+\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi}\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial p}]

Using

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial J}c_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}(J)mT_{rev}s_{5},\;\;\;\frac{\partial}{\partial J}s_{5}=-\omega^{\prime}(J)mT_{rev}c_{5}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial J}c_{5\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}(J)(\tau+mT_{rev})s_{5\tau},\;\;\;\frac{\partial}{\partial J}s_{5\tau}=-\omega^{\prime}(J)(\tau+mT_{rev})c_{5\tau}

Hence

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\left\{(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}s_{5\tau}-\omega^{\prime}(J)(\tau+mT_{rev})\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}c_{5\tau}\right.

\displaystyle-q_{1}Js_{25}\left[(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime\prime}s_{5\tau}-\omega^{\prime}(J)(\tau+mT_{rev})(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}c_{5\tau}\right.

\displaystyle-\omega^{\prime\prime}(J)\tau(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})c_{5\tau}-\omega^{\prime}(J)\tau(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}c_{5\tau}

\displaystyle-\left.(\omega^{\prime}(J))^{2}\tau(\tau+mT_{rev})(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})s_{5\tau}]\right]

\displaystyle-q_{1}(s_{25}-2J\omega^{\prime}(J)mT_{rev}c_{25})\left[(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}s_{5\tau}-\omega^{\prime}(J)\tau\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}c_{5\tau}\right]

\displaystyle\left.-q_{1}\left[\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}c_{5}^{2}c_{5\tau}+\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}\omega^{\prime}(J)(2mT_{rev}s_{5}c_{5}c_{5\tau}+(\tau+mT_{rev})c_{5}^{2}s_{5\tau})\right]\right\}

Now I drop terms that are independent of
\omega^{\prime}(J)\tau
. I also now assume the following model
for the action dependence of
\omega(J)

\omega(J)=\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J

(9.10)

where
\omega^{\prime}
is a constant. Now we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle-\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\omega^{\prime}

\displaystyle\left\{\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}\left[(\tau+mT_{rev})c_{5\tau}+q_{1}\left(2mT_{rev}s_{5}c_{5}c_{5\tau}+(\tau+mT_{rev})c_{5}^{2}s_{5\tau}-\tau s_{25}c_{5\tau}\right)\right]\right.

\displaystyle-q_{1}Js_{25}\left[(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}\left((\tau+mT_{rev})c_{5\tau}+\tau c_{5\tau}\right)+\omega^{\prime}\tau(\tau+mT_{rev})(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})s_{5\tau}\right]

\displaystyle\left.-2q_{1}JmT_{rev}c_{25}\left[(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}s_{5\tau}-\omega^{\prime}\tau\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}c_{5\tau}\right]\right\}

The derivative with respect to the phase
\phi
under the same approximation of dropping
terms independent of
\omega^{\prime}
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}q_{1}\omega^{\prime}J\tau(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})\frac{\partial}{\partial\phi}\left[s_{25}c_{5\tau}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}q_{1}\omega^{\prime}J\tau(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})\left[2c_{25}c_{5\tau}-s_{25}s_{5\tau}\right]

Hence the DF right after the 2nd quadrupole kick is

\psi_{6}(J,\phi,\tau+mT_{rev})=\psi_{5}-q_{2}(J\sin 2\phi\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J}+\cos^{2}\phi\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi})

(9.11)

while the DF at time
t
after the dipole kick and after the 2nd quadrupole kick is

\psi_{7}(J,\phi,t>\tau+mT_{rev})=\psi_{6}(J,\phi-\omega(J)(t-(\tau+mT_{rev})))

(9.12)

Under the transform
\phi\rightarrow\phi-\omega(J)(t-(\tau+mT_{rev}))
, the different
trigonometric terms transform as

\displaystyle\cos\phi

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\cos(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau-mT_{rev}))\equiv c_{\tau,m}

\displaystyle c_{5}=\cos(\phi-\omega(J)mT_{rev})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\cos(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))\equiv c_{\tau}

\displaystyle c_{5\tau}=\cos(\phi-\omega(J)(\tau+mT_{rev})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\cos(\phi-\omega(J)t)\equiv c_{t}

\displaystyle c_{25}=\cos(2(\phi-\omega(J)mT_{rev}))

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\cos(2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)))\equiv c_{2\tau}

and similarly the sine terms. Also, define

\sin 2\phi\rightarrow 2c_{\tau,m}s_{\tau_{m}}\equiv s_{2\tau,m}

The DF
\psi_{5}
transforms to (after keeping only the
\omega^{\prime}
dependent terms)

\psi_{5}=q_{1}\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}Js_{2\tau}\omega^{\prime}\tau(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})c_{t}

(9.13)

while the derivative terms transform as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\omega^{\prime}

\displaystyle\left\{\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}\left[(\tau+mT_{rev})c_{t}+q_{1}\left(2mT_{rev}s_{\tau}c_{\tau}c_{t}+(\tau+mT_{rev})c_{\tau}^{2}s_{t}-\tau s_{2\tau}c_{t}\right)\right]\right.

\displaystyle-q_{1}J(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}\left[\left((\tau+mT_{rev})c_{t}+\tau c_{t}\right)s_{2\tau}+2mT_{rev}c_{2\tau}s_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\left.-q_{1}\omega^{\prime}\tau J(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})\left[(\tau+mT_{rev})s_{2\tau}s_{t}-2mT_{rev}c_{2\tau}c_{t}\right]\right\}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}q_{1}\omega^{\prime}\tau J(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})\left[2c_{2\tau}c_{t}-s_{2\tau}s_{t}\right]

and the DF is

\psi_{7}(J,\phi,t)=\psi_{5}-q_{2}(Js_{2\tau,m}\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J}+c_{\tau,m}^{2}\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi})

(9.14)

The dipole moment at time
t
is

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta}\int\sqrt{J}\cos\phi\psi_{7}(J,\phi,t)d\phi dJ

(9.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta}\int\sqrt{J}\cos\phi\left[\psi_{5}-q_{2}(Js_{2\tau,m}\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J}+c_{\tau,m}^{2}\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi})\right]d\phi dJ

The term that is independent of
q_{1},q_{2}
vanishes after integrating over
\phi
. The
remaining terms can be categorized into three groups as
O(q_{1}),O(q_{2}),O(q_{1}q_{2})
. Since
the dimensionless quadrupole kicks
q_{1},q_{2}\ll 1
, we may expect the terms in the last
group to be negligible. We label these terms as
T(q_{1}),T(q_{2})
and
T(q_{1},q_{2})
where

\langle x\rangle(t)\equiv T(q_{1})+T(q_{2})+T(q_{1}q_{2})

Writing

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\omega^{\prime}\left\{\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}(\tau+mT_{rev})c_{t}+q_{1}(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J})_{q1}\right\}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J})_{0}-[\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\omega^{\prime}]q_{1}(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J})_{q1}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle[\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\omega^{\prime}]q_{1}(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi})_{q1}

We have

\displaystyle T(q_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta}\int\sqrt{J}\cos\phi\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t)d\phi dJ

(9.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q_{1}\sqrt{2\beta}\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\omega^{\prime}\tau\int dJd\phi\sqrt{J}\cos\phi Js_{2\tau}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}c_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q_{1}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau\int dJJ^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\int d\phi\cos\phi s_{2\tau}c_{t}

(9.17)

\displaystyle T(q_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-q_{2}\sqrt{2\beta}\int\sqrt{J}\cos\phi Js_{2\tau,m}(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J})_{0}d\phi dJ

(9.18)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-q_{2}\sqrt{2\beta}(-\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\omega^{\prime})\int dJd\phi\sqrt{J}\cos\phi Js_{2\tau,m}\left\{\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}(\tau+mT_{rev})c_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\int dJJ^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\int d\phi\cos\phi s_{2\tau,m}\left[(\tau+mT_{rev})c_{t}\right]

(9.19)

and

\displaystyle T(q_{1},q_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-q_{2}\sqrt{2\beta}\int\sqrt{J}\cos\phi(-\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\omega^{\prime})\left\{Js_{2\tau,m}q_{1}(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J})_{q1}-q_{1}c_{\tau,m}^{2}(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi})_{q1}\right\}d\phi dJ

(9.20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q_{1}q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\int dJ\sqrt{J}\int d\phi\cos\phi\left\{Js_{2\tau,m}(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J})_{q1}-c_{\tau,m}^{2}(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi})_{q1}\right\}

\displaystyle(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J})_{q1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}\left(2mT_{rev}s_{\tau}c_{\tau}c_{t}+(\tau+mT_{rev})c_{\tau}^{2}s_{t}-\tau s_{2\tau}c_{t}\right)

(9.21)

\displaystyle-J(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}\left[\left((\tau+mT_{rev})c_{t}+\tau c_{t}\right)s_{2\tau}+2mT_{rev}c_{2\tau}s_{t}\right]

\displaystyle-\omega^{\prime}\tau J(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})\left[(\tau+mT_{rev})s_{2\tau}s_{t}-2mT_{rev}c_{2\tau}c_{t}\right]

(9.22)

\displaystyle(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi})_{q1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tau J(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})\left[2c_{2\tau}c_{t}-s_{2\tau}s_{t}\right]

(9.23)

Writing
\omega^{\prime}=(\omega_{rev}/J_{0})\mu
where
\mu
is the detuning parameter,

\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}=2\pi N_{d}\mu
where
N_{d}
is the delay in turns. With
N_{d}\simeq 400,\mu\simeq 0.001
,
\omega^{\prime}\tau J_{0}\simeq O(1)
, hence it is of the same order as the other
terms. It appears that all terms in
T(q_{1},q2)
have to be kept.

The
\phi
integration in
T(q_{1})
yields

\int d\phi\cos\phi\sin(2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)))\cos(\phi-\omega(J)t)=-\frac{\pi}{2}\sin(\omega(J)(t-2\tau))

with a similar
\phi
integration in
T(q_{2})

\int d\phi\cos\phi\sin(2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau-mT_{rev})))\cos(\phi-\omega(J)t)\\
=-\frac{\pi}{2}\sin(\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev})))\\

Hence

\displaystyle T(q_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\pi q_{1}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau\int dJ\;J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sin(\omega(J)(t-2\tau))

\displaystyle T(q_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\pi q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}(\tau+mT_{rev})\int dJ\;J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sin[\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))]

To 1st order in the quad strength, each quad has a similar contribution with a time dependent
factor depending on the time the kick is applied.

If there are
N_{q}
kicks applied at times
\tau+mT_{rev}
,
m=0,...,N_{q}
, then again to
1st order in the kicks, the dipole moment is

\langle x\rangle(t)=-\pi\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\int dJ\;J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sum_{m=0}^{N_{q}}q_{m}(\tau+mT_{rev})\sin[\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))]

(9.24)

We consider two cases of the above

Case 1: All kicks have the same strength:
q_{m}=q
, then

\langle x\rangle(t)=-\pi q\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\int dJ\;J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sum_{m=0}^{N_{q}}(\tau+mT_{rev})\sin[\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))]

(9.25)

Using

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{N_{q}}\sin[\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sin[\omega(J)(N_{q}+1)T_{rev}]\sin[\omega(J)(t-2\tau-N_{q}T_{rev})]}{\sin(\omega(J)T_{rev})}

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{N_{q}}m\sin[\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{2\sin(\omega(J)T_{rev})})^{2}\left\{-\sin[\omega(J)(t-2\tau)]\right.

(9.26)

\displaystyle+(N_{q}+1)\sin[\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+N_{q}T_{rev}))]

\displaystyle\left.-N_{q}\sin[\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+(N_{q}+1)T_{rev}))]\right\}

N_{q}=0
corresponds to the case with the single quad kick at
\tau
. In this case, the second
sum vanishes and the first sum contributes
\sin[\omega(J)(t-2\tau)]
, the same as before.

Case 2: Alternating sign quad kicks:
q_{m}=(-1)^{m}q

\langle x\rangle(t)=-\pi q\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\int dJ\;J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sum_{m=0}^{N_{q}}(-1)^{m}(\tau+mT_{rev})\sin[\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))]

(9.27)

Using

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{N_{q}}(-1)^{m}\sin[\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\cos[N_{q}\pi/2-(N_{q}+1)\omega(J)T_{rev}]}{\cos(\omega(J)T_{rev})}

\displaystyle\times\sin[N_{q}\pi/2+\omega(J)(t-2\tau-N_{q}T_{rev})]

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{N_{q}}(-1)^{m}m\sin[\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{2\cos(\omega(J)T_{rev})})^{2}\left\{-\sin[\omega(J)(t-2\tau)]\right.

(9.28)

\displaystyle+(N_{q}+1)\sin[N_{q}\pi+\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+N_{q}T_{rev}))]

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\left.+N_{q}\sin[N_{q}\pi+\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+(N_{q}+1)T_{rev}))]\right\}

The integration over the action however cannot be done analytically.
So instead of summing over the trigonometric term, it may be preferable to do the integration
first and then do a numerical sum over the integrated terms. Since

\psi_{0}(J)=\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}],\;\;\;\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)=-\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}^{2}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}]

we have

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\sum_{m=0}^{N_{q}}q_{m}(\tau+mT_{rev})\times

(9.29)

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\left[e^{[i\omega_{\beta}(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))]}\int dz\;z^{2}\exp[-z]\exp[i\omega^{\prime}J_{0}z(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\sum_{m=0}^{N_{q}}q_{m}(\tau+mT_{rev}){\rm Im}\left[\frac{\exp[i\Phi_{m}]}{(1-i\xi_{m})^{3}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\sum_{m=0}^{N_{q}}q_{m}(\tau+mT_{rev})\frac{\left[\xi_{m}(3-\xi_{m}^{2})\cos\Phi_{m}+(1-3\xi_{m}^{2})\sin\Phi_{m}\right]}{(1+\xi_{m}^{2})^{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\sum_{m=0}^{N_{q}}q_{m}(\tau+mT_{rev})\frac{\sin(\Phi_{m}+3\tan^{-1}[\xi_{m}])}{(1+\xi_{m}^{2})^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\beta}(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))

\displaystyle\xi_{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}J_{0}(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))

In doing the integration over
z
, we used

\int_{0}^{\infty}dz\;z^{2}\exp[-az]=\frac{2}{a^{3}}

In the above we assumed that the quad kicks are applied every turn from
2\tau
to

2\tau+N_{q}T_{rev}
. If instead the kicks are applied with the same gap
n_{gap}
between kicks,
then the above formula is easily generalized by replacing
T_{rev}
by
n_{gap}T_{rev}
with

n_{gap}\geq 1
.
Thus if
n_{gap}=2
, there is a gap of a single turn between kicks. If the gaps between
successive kicks are different, then
n_{gap}
will depend on the
m
th kick.

As a special case of the above, consider a
stimulated echo
at later times with 2 quad
kicks, i.e.
N_{q}=1
and
the 2nd kick is applied at time
p\tau
after the 1st kick. In this case, we have

n_{gap}T_{rev}=p\tau
and

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}J_{0}[q_{0}(\tau)\frac{\sin(\Phi_{0}+3\tan^{-1}[\xi_{0}])}{(1+\xi_{0}^{2})^{3/2}}+q_{1}((p+1)\tau)\frac{\sin(\Phi_{1}+3\tan^{-1}[\xi_{1}])}{(1+\xi_{1}^{2})^{3/2}}]

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\beta}(t-2\tau),\;\;\;\Phi_{1}=\omega_{\beta}(t-2(p+1)\tau)

\displaystyle\xi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}J_{0}(t-2\tau),\;\;\;\xi_{1}=\omega^{\prime}J_{0}(t-2(p+1)\tau)

Note that the second term is only applicable for times
t\geq(p+1)\tau
. If we assume that the
two kicks have the same sign,
q_{0}=q_{1}=q
and introducing the dimensionless quad strength
parameter

Q=q\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\tau

We have therefore

\langle x(t)\rangle=\beta_{K}\theta Q[\frac{\sin(\Phi_{0}+3\tan^{-1}[\xi_{0}])}{(1+\xi_{0}^{2})^{3/2}}+(p+1)\frac{\sin(\Phi_{1}+3\tan^{-1}[\xi_{1}])}{(1+\xi_{1}^{2})^{3/2}}]

(9.30)

The stimulated echo at time
t=4\tau
is given by this expression with

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\omega_{\beta}\tau 3,\;\;\;\Phi_{1}=2\omega_{\beta}\tau(2-(p+1))

\displaystyle\xi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\tau,\;\;\;\xi_{1}=2\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\tau(2-(p+1))

9.1
2nd order in quad kicks

Now we consider the terms in
T(q_{1},q_{2})
. We had

T(q_{1},q_{2})=2q_{1}q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\int dJ\sqrt{J}\int d\phi\cos\phi\left\{Js_{2\tau,m}(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J})_{q1}-c_{\tau,m}^{2}(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi})_{q1}\right\}\equiv T(q_{1},q_{2})|_{J}+T(q_{1},q_{2})|_{\phi}

(9.31)

We simplify the earlier expression Eq.(
9.22
) for the partial derivative

\displaystyle(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J})_{q1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}\left((mT_{rev}-\tau)s_{2\tau}c_{t}+(\tau+mT_{rev})c_{\tau}^{2}s_{t}\right)

\displaystyle-J(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}\left[(2\tau+mT_{rev})s_{2\tau}c_{t}+2mT_{rev}c_{2\tau}s_{t}\right]

\displaystyle-\omega^{\prime}\tau J(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})\left[(\tau+mT_{rev})s_{2\tau}s_{t}-2mT_{rev}c_{2\tau}c_{t}\right]

There are six distinct terms in the derivative w.r.t the action. Doing the integrations
over the phase, we have the five distinct integrals

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos\phi s_{2\tau,m}s_{2\tau}c_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos\phi\sin 2[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau-mT_{rev})]\sin 2[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)]\cos[\phi-\omega(J)t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}\cos[\omega(J)t]\cos[2\omega(J)mT_{rev}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}\left(\cos[\omega(J)(t-2mT_{rev})]+\cos[\omega(J)(t+2mT_{rev})]\right)

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos\phi s_{2\tau,m}c_{\tau}^{2}s_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos\phi\sin 2[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau-mT_{rev})]\cos^{2}[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)]\sin[\phi-\omega(J)t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}[\cos[\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))]-\sin[\omega(J)t]\sin[2\omega(J)mT_{rev}]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}\left[\cos[\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))]-\frac{1}{2}\left\{\cos[\omega(J)(t-2mT_{rev})]-\cos[\omega(J)(t+2mT_{rev})]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos\phi s_{2\tau,m}c_{2\tau}s_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos\phi\sin 2[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau-mT_{rev})]\cos 2[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)]\sin[\phi-\omega(J)t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\pi}{2}\sin[\omega(J)t]\sin[2\omega(J)mT_{rev}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\pi}{4}\left[\cos[\omega(J)(t-2mT_{rev})]-\cos[\omega(J)(t+2mT_{rev})]\right]

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos\phi s_{2\tau,m}s_{2\tau}s_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos\phi\sin 2[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau-mT_{rev})]\sin 2[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)]\sin[\phi-\omega(J)t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\pi}{2}\sin[\omega(J)t]\cos[2\omega(J)mT_{rev}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\pi}{4}\left[\sin[\omega(J)(t-2mT_{rev})]+\sin[\omega(J)(t+2mT_{rev})]\right]

\displaystyle I_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos\phi s_{2\tau,m}c_{2\tau}c_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos\phi\sin 2[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau-mT_{rev})]\cos 2[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)]\cos[\phi-\omega(J)t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}\cos[\omega(J)t]\sin[2\omega(J)mT_{rev}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\pi}{4}\left[\sin[\omega(J)(t-2mT_{rev})]-\sin[\omega(J)(t+2mT_{rev})]\right]

Combining all the terms in
(\partial\psi_{5}/\partial J)_{q1}
,

\displaystyle\frac{T(q1,q2)|_{J}}{2q_{1}q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dJ\sqrt{J}\int d\phi\cos\phi Js_{2\tau,m}(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial J})_{q1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int J^{3/2}\left[\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J}\left\{[(mT_{rev}-\tau)I_{1}+(\tau+mT_{rev})I_{2}]-\omega^{\prime}\tau J[(\tau+mT_{rev})I_{4}-2mT_{rev}I_{5}]\right\}\right.

\displaystyle-\left.J(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}[(2\tau+mT_{rev})I_{1}+2mT_{rev}I_{3}]\right]\;dJ

Now the term from
(\partial\psi_{5}/\partial\phi)_{q1}
,
Substituting from Eq.(
9.31
) and Eq.(
9.23
),

\displaystyle T(q_{1},q_{2})|_{\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2q_{1}q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\int dJ\sqrt{J}\int d\phi\cos\phi c_{\tau,m}^{2}(\frac{\partial\psi_{5}}{\partial\phi})_{q1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q_{1}q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau\int dJJ^{3/2}\int d\phi\cos\phi c_{\tau,m}^{2}(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})\left[2c_{2\tau}c_{t}-s_{2\tau}s_{t}\right]

The
\phi
integrations involve

\displaystyle I_{6}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\;\cos\phi c_{\tau,m}^{2}c_{2\tau}c_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\;\cos\phi\cos^{2}[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau-mT_{rev})]\cos 2[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)]\cos[\phi-\omega(J)t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}\cos[2\omega(J)mT_{rev}]\cos[\omega(J)t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}[\cos[\omega(J)(t-2mT_{rev})]+\cos[\omega(J)(t+2mT_{rev})]]

\displaystyle I_{7}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\;\cos\phi c_{\tau,m}^{2}s_{2\tau}s_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\;\cos\phi\cos^{2}[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau-mT_{rev})]\sin 2[\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)]\sin[\phi-\omega(J)t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}\sin[\omega(J)t]\sin[2\omega(J)mT_{rev}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}[\cos[\omega(J)(t-2mT_{rev})]-\cos[\omega(J)(t+2mT_{rev})]]

Combining the two terms, we have

\displaystyle T(q_{1},q_{2})|_{\phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q_{1}q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau\int dJJ^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}[2I_{6}-I_{7}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}q_{1}q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta(\omega^{\prime})^{2}\tau\int dJJ^{2}(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\{\cos[\omega(J)(t-2mT_{rev})]+3\cos[\omega(J)(t+2mT_{rev})]\}

In the expressions for the integrated terms
I_{j}
,
j=1,...7
, there are only three different arguments.
Define

\Phi_{\tau,m}=\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev})),\;\;\;\Phi_{m,\pm}=\omega(J)(t\pm 2mT_{rev})

Then we have

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}\left\{{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]+{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,+})]\right\}

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}\left\{{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{\tau,m})]-\frac{1}{2}{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]+\frac{1}{2}{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,+})]\right\}

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}\left\{{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,+})]-{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]\right\}

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\pi}{4}\left\{{\rm Im}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]+{\rm Im}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,+})]\right\}

\displaystyle I_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\pi}{4}\left\{{\rm Im}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]-{\rm Im}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,+})]\right\}

\displaystyle I_{6}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I_{1}=\frac{\pi}{4}\left\{{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]+{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,+})]\right\}

\displaystyle I_{7}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-I_{3}=\frac{\pi}{4}\left\{{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]-{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,+})]\right\}

Defining

\tau_{\pm}=\tau\pm mT_{rev}

and combining all terms, we have

\displaystyle T(q_{1},q_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle T(q_{1},q_{2})|_{J}+T(q_{1},q_{2})|_{\phi}

(9.32)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q_{1}q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\int\left[J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\left\{[\tau_{+}I_{2}-\tau_{-}I_{1}]-\omega^{\prime}\tau J[\tau_{+}I_{4}-2mT_{rev}I_{5}]\right\}\right.

\displaystyle-\left.J^{5/2}(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}[(\tau+\tau_{+})I_{1}+2mT_{rev}I_{3}]\right]\;dJ

\displaystyle+2q_{1}q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\tau\int J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}[2I_{1}+I_{3}]dJ

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q_{1}q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\times

\displaystyle\int\left[J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\left\{[\tau_{+}(I_{2}+I_{1})+\tau I_{3}]-\omega^{\prime}\tau J[\tau_{+}I_{4}-2mT_{rev}I_{5}]\right\}\right.

\displaystyle-\left.J^{5/2}(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}[(\tau+\tau_{+})I_{1}+2mT_{rev}I_{3}]\right]\;dJ

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q_{1}q_{2}\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}(T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3})

There are three types of terms in the
J
integration:

\int dJJ^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[iaJ],\;\;\;\int dJJ^{3}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[iaJ]\;\;\;\int dJJ^{5/2}(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}\exp[iaJ]

With

\displaystyle\psi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-J/J_{0}],\;\;\;\psi_{0}^{\prime}=-\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}^{2}}\exp[-J/J_{0}]

\displaystyle J^{5/2}(\psi_{0}^{\prime}\sqrt{J})^{\prime}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{J^{5/2}}{2\pi J_{0}^{2}}[-\sqrt{\frac{J}{J_{0}}}+\frac{1}{2\sqrt{J}}]\exp[-J/J_{0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}^{2}}[-\frac{J^{3}}{J_{0}}+\frac{1}{2}J^{2}]\exp[-J/J_{0}]

we have

\displaystyle\int dJJ^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[iaJ]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}^{2}}\int dJ\;J^{2}\exp[-J/J_{0}]\exp[iaJ]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{J_{0}}{2\pi}\int dz\;z^{2}\exp[-(1-iaJ_{0})z]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{J_{0}}{\pi}\frac{1}{(1-iaJ_{0})^{3}}

\displaystyle\int dJJ^{3}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\exp[iaJ]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{3J_{0}}{\pi}\frac{1}{(1-iaJ_{0})^{4}}

Hence

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int dJ\;J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\left\{[\tau_{+}(I_{2}+I_{1})+\tau I_{3}]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}\int dJ\;J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\left\{[\tau_{+}\left({\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{\tau,m})]+\frac{1}{2}{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]+\frac{3}{2}{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,+})]\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\tau\left({\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,+})]-{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}\int dJ\;J^{2}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\left\{\tau_{+}{\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{\tau,m})]++(\frac{1}{2}\tau_{+}-\tau){\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(\frac{3}{2}\tau_{+}+\tau){\rm Re}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{J_{0}}{4}\left\{\tau_{+}{\rm Re}[\frac{e^{i\omega_{\beta}\Delta t_{m}}}{(1-i\xi_{\tau,m})^{3}}]+(\frac{1}{2}\tau_{+}-\tau){\rm Re}[\frac{e^{i\omega_{\beta}t_{m,-}}}{(1-i\xi_{m,-})^{3}}]+(\frac{3}{2}\tau_{+}+\tau){\rm Re}[\frac{e^{i\omega_{\beta}t_{m,+}}}{(1-i\xi_{m,+})^{3}}]\right\}

where
\Delta t_{m},t_{m,\pm},\xi_{\tau,m},\xi_{m,\pm}
are defined by

\displaystyle\Phi_{\tau,m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(J)(t-2(\tau+mT_{rev}))\equiv(\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J)\Delta t_{m}

\displaystyle\Phi_{m,\pm}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(J)(t\pm 2mT_{rev})\equiv(\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J)t_{m,\pm}

\displaystyle\xi_{\tau,m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\Delta t,\;\;\;\xi_{m,\pm}=\omega^{\prime}J_{0}t_{m,\pm}

Next

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\omega^{\prime}\tau\int dJ\;J^{3}\psi_{0}^{\prime}[\tau_{+}I_{4}-2mT_{rev}I_{5}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\omega^{\prime}\tau(-\frac{\pi}{4})\int dJ\;J^{3}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\left\{\tau_{+}({\rm Im}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]+{\rm Im}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,+})])\right.

\displaystyle\left.-2mT_{rev}({\rm Im}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]-{\rm Im}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,+})])\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{4}\omega^{\prime}\tau\int dJ\;J^{3}\psi_{0}^{\prime}\left\{(\tau_{+}-2mT_{rev}){\rm Im}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,-})]+(\tau_{+}+2mT_{rev}){\rm Im}[\exp(i\Phi_{m,+})]\right\}

10
Nonlinear quad kicks

Here a theory to find the echo amplitude with a nonlinear dependence on the quad strength is developed.
A Lagrangian theory was developed in
[
2
]
. Here instead we develop an Eulerian theory
by following the flow of the density distribution, similar to that in
[
4
]
.

We start with the usual definitions of the phase space variables

x=\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi,\;\;\;\;p=\beta x^{\prime}+\alpha x=-\sqrt{2\beta J}\sin\phi

and the inverse relations

J=\frac{1}{2\beta}[x^{2}+p^{2}],\;\;\;\;\phi=\tan^{-1}(\frac{-p}{x})

\psi_{0}(J)
is the initial distribution with initial emittance
\epsilon_{0}=J_{0}

\psi_{0}(J)=\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}]

(10.1)

\psi_{1}(J,\phi)
is the distribution function (DF) after the dipole kick
\Delta p=\beta\Delta x^{\prime}=\beta\theta

\psi_{1}(J,\phi)=\psi_{0}(x,p-\beta_{K}\theta)\simeq\psi_{0}(J)+\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\sin\phi

(10.2)

In the second equality, the DF was expanded to first order in
\theta
.

\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)
is the DF at time
t
after the dipole kick

\psi_{2}(J,\phi,t)=\psi_{0}(J)+\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\sin(\phi-\omega(J)t)

(10.3)

\psi_{3}(J,\phi,\tau)
is the DF at time
\tau
after the dipole kick

\psi_{3}(J,\phi,\tau)=\psi_{0}(J)+\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\sin(\phi-\omega(J)\tau)

(10.4)

Since
\psi_{0}(J)
will not contribute to the dipole moment, it will be dropped.
The quad kick
\Delta p=-qx
changes the distribution to

\psi_{4}(x,p,\tau)=\psi_{3}(x,p+qx,\tau)

(10.5)

Under this change, we have in the argument of the density distribution,

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\beta}[x^{2}+(p+qx)^{2}]=J+\frac{1}{2\beta}(2qpx+q^{2}x^{2})

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle J+J(-q\sin 2\phi+q^{2}\cos^{2}\phi)\equiv J+A(q,\phi)J

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\tan^{-1}(-\frac{p+qx}{x})=\tan^{-1}(\tan\phi-q)

where

A(q,\phi)=(-q\sin 2\phi+q^{2}\cos^{2}\phi)

Hence

\displaystyle\psi_{4}(J,\phi,\tau)=\psi_{0}(J+A(q,\phi)J)+\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J+A(q,\phi)J)\sqrt{\frac{2(J+A(q,\phi)J)}{\beta}}\sin\left[\tan^{-1}(\tan\phi-q)-\omega(J+A(q,\phi)J)\tau\right]

At any time after the quad kick, the distribution function at time
t
(measured from the start of the
dipole kick) is simply a rotation at the betatron frequency at the action
J
(and not at

J+A(q,\phi)J
). In the Eulerian
description, we stay at a fixed phase space location and follow the change of density at that
location.

\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t)=\psi_{4}(J,\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))

(10.7)

and the dipole moment is

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\sqrt{2\beta}\int dJ\int d\phi\sqrt{J}\cos\phi\phi_{5}(J,\phi,t)

(10.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\beta_{K}\theta\int dJ\int d\phi\sqrt{J}\cos\phi\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J+A(q,\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))J)

\displaystyle\times\sqrt{(J+A(q,\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))J)}

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\tan^{-1}\left(\tan(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))-q\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\omega(J+A(q,(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)))J)\tau\right]

Now we start making approximations.

We assume that
q\ll 1
, this is almost always satisfied in experiments. Hence we can approximate

A(q,\phi)=-q\sin 2\phi+q^{2}\cos^{2}\phi\approx-q\sin 2\phi

And

A(q,(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)))\approx-q\sin 2[(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))]

Previously I made these approximations

•

In the pure action term

\displaystyle\sqrt{(J+A(q,\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))J)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{J[1+A(q,\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))]}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\sqrt{J\{1-q\sin 2[(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))]\}}<\sqrt{J\{1+|q|\}}\approx\sqrt{J}

•

In the same spirit

\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J+A(q,\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))J)\approx\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)

These will be improved here
Recall that

A(q,\phi)=-B(q)\sin 2\phi+\frac{1}{2}q^{2},\;\;\;\;B(q)=\sqrt{q^{2}+q^{4}/4}

Now I keep terms to the next leading order in
A(q,\phi)
assuming that
A(q,\phi)\ll 1

First we introduce some shorthand notation

\Delta\phi=\omega(J)(t-\tau),\;\;\;\phi_{-}=\phi-\Delta\phi

(10.9)

Expand the square root to first order in
A(q)
as

\displaystyle\sqrt{J[1+A(q,\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))]}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\sqrt{J}\left[1+\frac{1}{2}A(q,\phi_{-}\right]

(10.10)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\sqrt{J}\left[1+\frac{q^{2}}{4}-\frac{1}{2}B(q)\sin(2(\phi_{-}))\right]

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(J+A(q,\phi_{-})J)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}(1+A(q,\phi_{-}))]

(10.11)

A(q,\phi_{-})
depends on
J
via
\Delta\phi
. Thus

\frac{\partial}{\partial J}A(q,\phi_{-})=-2B(q)\cos 2\phi_{-}(-\frac{\partial}{\partial J}\Delta\phi)=2B(q)\cos 2\phi_{-}\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau)\equiv 2B(q)\cos 2\phi_{-}\Delta\phi^{\prime}

Hence the slope of the distribution function is

\displaystyle\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J+A(q,\phi_{-})J)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}^{2}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}(1+A(q,\Delta\phi))]\left[1+A(q,\phi_{-})+J(\frac{\partial}{\partial J}A(q,\phi_{-}))\right]

(10.12)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}^{2}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}(1+A(q,\Delta\phi))]

\displaystyle\times\left[1+A(q,\phi_{-})+2B(q)\cos 2\phi_{-}\Delta\phi^{\prime}J\right]

where

\Delta\phi=\omega(J)(t-\tau),\;\;\;\Delta\phi^{\prime}=\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau)

and assumed that
\omega(J)=\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J
.

The dipole moment is now from Eq.
10.8

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\beta_{K}\theta\int dJ\int d\phi\sqrt{J}\cos\phi\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J+A(q,\phi_{-})J)

\displaystyle\times\sqrt{(J+A(q,\phi_{-})J)}\sin\left[\phi_{-}-\omega(J+A(q,\phi_{-})J)\tau\right]

and introduce
\omega_{+}(J)
as

\displaystyle\omega(J+A(q,\phi_{-})J)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J(1+A(q,\phi_{-}))=\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2}-B(q)\sin(2\phi_{-}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{+}-\omega^{\prime}JB(q)\sin 2\phi_{-},\;\;\;\omega_{+}=\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})

With the above approximations, we have

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\beta_{K}\theta(-\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}^{2}})\int dJ\;J\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})]\int d\phi\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]\cos\phi

(10.13)

\displaystyle\times[1+\frac{1}{2}A(q,\phi_{-})]\left(1+A(q,\phi_{-})+2B(q)\cos 2\phi_{-}\Delta\phi^{\prime}J\right)\sin\left[\phi_{-}-\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))\right]

Using

\sin A\cos B=\frac{1}{2}[\sin(A+B)+\sin(A-B)]

we have

\displaystyle\cos\phi\sin\left[\phi_{-}-\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left\{\sin[2\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))]-\sin[\Delta\phi+\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))]\right\}

Multiplying out the other terms

\displaystyle[1+\frac{1}{2}A(q,\phi_{-})]\left(1+A(q,\phi_{-})+2B(q)\cos 2\phi_{-}\Delta\phi^{\prime}J\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[1+\frac{1}{4}q^{2}-B(q)\sin 2\phi_{-}]\left(1+\frac{1}{4}q^{2}-B(q)\sin 2\phi_{-}+2B(q)\cos 2\phi_{-}\Delta\phi^{\prime}J\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\frac{1}{4}q^{2})(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})-\frac{1}{2}B(q)[3+q^{2}]\sin 2\phi_{-}

\displaystyle+2(1+\frac{1}{4}q^{2})B(q)\Delta\phi^{\prime}J\cos 2\phi_{-}+\frac{1}{2}B^{2}(q)\sin^{2}2\phi_{-}

\displaystyle-B^{2}(q)\Delta\phi^{\prime}J\sin 2\phi_{-}\cos 2\phi_{-}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle C_{1}+C_{3}\sin 2\phi_{-}+C_{5}\frac{J}{J_{0}}\cos 2\phi_{-}+C_{7}\sin^{2}2\phi_{-}+C_{9}\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}\cos 2\phi_{-}

where the dimensionless constants
C_{i}
independent of
\phi,J
are

\displaystyle C_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\frac{1}{4}q^{2})(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})\sim O(1)

\displaystyle C_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}B(q)[3+q^{2}]\sim O(q)

\displaystyle C_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(1+\frac{1}{4}q^{2})B(q)\Delta\phi^{\prime}J_{0}\sim O(q)

\displaystyle C_{7}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}B^{2}(q)\sim O(q^{2})

\displaystyle C_{9}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-B^{2}(q)\Delta\phi^{\prime}J_{0}\sim O(q^{2})

(10.14)

where
\Delta\phi^{\prime}=\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau)
is time dependent.

Hence we can write

\langle x(t)\rangle=-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta}{2\pi J_{0}^{2}}\int dJJ\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})]\ \left\{\Phi_{1}-\Phi_{2}+\Phi_{3}-\Phi_{4}+\frac{J}{J_{0}}(\Phi_{5}-\Phi_{6})+\Phi_{7}-\Phi_{8}+\frac{J}{J_{0}}(\Phi_{9}-\Phi_{10})\right\}

(10.15)

where

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}\int d\phi\sin[2\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

(10.16)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}\int d\phi\sin[\Delta\phi+\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

(10.17)

\displaystyle\Phi_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{3}\int d\phi\sin 2\phi_{-}\sin[2\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

(10.18)

\displaystyle\Phi_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{3}\int d\phi\sin 2\phi_{-}\sin[\Delta\phi+\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

(10.19)

\displaystyle\Phi_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{5}\int d\phi\cos 2\phi_{-}\sin[2\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

(10.20)

\displaystyle\Phi_{6}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{5}\int d\phi\cos 2\phi_{-}\sin[\Delta\phi+\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

(10.21)

\displaystyle\Phi_{7}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{7}\int d\phi\sin^{2}2\phi_{-}\sin[2\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

(10.22)

\displaystyle\Phi_{8}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{7}\int d\phi\sin^{2}2\phi_{-}\sin[\Delta\phi+\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

(10.23)

\displaystyle\Phi_{9}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{9}\int d\phi\sin 2\phi_{-}\cos 2\phi_{-}\sin[2\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

(10.24)

\displaystyle\Phi_{10}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{9}\int d\phi\sin 2\phi_{-}\cos 2\phi_{-}\sin[\Delta\phi+\tau\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

(10.25)

From the definition of
\omega_{+}
, we have
\omega(J+JA(q,\phi_{-}))=\omega_{+}-B(q)\omega^{\prime}J\sin 2\phi_{-}
. All the
above integrals are of the form

\int d\phi\exp[i(m\phi+a\sin(2\phi-2\Delta\phi))]

for different integer values
m
and complex constants
a
. We first expand into Bessel functions

\displaystyle\int d\phi\exp[im\phi]\exp[ia\sin(2\phi-2\Delta\phi)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\exp[im\phi]\sum_{k}J_{k}(a)\exp[ik(2\phi-2\Delta\phi)]

(10.26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}J_{k}(a)\exp[-2ik\Delta\phi]2\pi\delta(m+2k,0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi J_{-m/2}(a)\exp[im\Delta\phi]

Working through the integrals

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}{\rm Im}\left\{\int d\phi\exp[i(2\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+}+\tau B(q)\omega^{\prime}J\sin 2\phi_{-})+B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[-i(\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]\int d\phi\exp[i(2\phi+z_{1}J\sin(2\phi-2\Delta\phi))]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi C_{1}{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i(\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+})]J_{-1}(z_{1}J)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\pi C_{1}{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i(\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+})]J_{1}(z_{1}J)\right\}

where we defined the complex parameter

z_{1}=\frac{B(q)}{J_{0}}[\tau\omega^{\prime}J_{0}-i]

and used
J_{-1}(z)=-J_{1}(z)
. Noting that
\omega^{\prime}J_{0}=1/\tau_{D}
, we can write

z_{1}=\frac{B(q)}{J_{0}}[\frac{\tau}{\tau_{D}}-i],\;\;\;\;\Rightarrow|\frac{{\rm Im}[z_{1}]}{{\rm Re}[z_{1}]}|\ll 1

(10.27)

Next

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}{\rm Im}\left\{\int d\phi\exp[i(\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+}-\tau B(q)\omega^{\prime}J\sin 2\phi_{-})+B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{1}{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i(\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]\int d\phi\exp[-iz_{1}^{*}J\sin(2\phi-2\Delta\phi)]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi C_{1}{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i(\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]J_{0}(z_{1}^{*}J)\right\}

where we used
J_{0}(-z)=J_{0}(z)
.

Next we use

\sin A\sin B=\frac{1}{2}[\cos(A-B)-\cos(A+B)]

to decompose
\Phi_{3},\Phi_{4}
as

\Phi_{3}=\Phi_{3.1}-\Phi_{3.2}

where

\displaystyle\Phi_{3.1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}C_{3}\int d\phi\cos[-\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+}-\tau\omega^{\prime}JB(q)\sin 2\phi_{-}]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}C_{3}{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[i(-\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]\int d\phi\exp[-z_{1}^{*}J\sin 2\phi_{-}]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi C_{3}{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[i(-\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]J_{0}(z_{1}^{*}J)\right\}

and

\displaystyle\Phi_{3.2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}C_{3}\int d\phi\cos[4\phi-3\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+}+\tau\omega^{\prime}JB(q)\sin 2\phi_{-}]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}C_{3}{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[-i(3\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]\int d\phi\exp[i(4\phi+z_{1}J\sin 2\phi_{-})]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi C_{3}{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[-i(3\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]J_{-2}(z_{1}J)\exp[i4\Delta\phi]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi C_{3}{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[i(\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+})]J_{2}(z_{1}J)\right\}

and

\Phi_{3}=\pi C_{3}{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[i(-\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]J_{0}(z_{1}^{*}J)-\exp[i(\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+})]J_{2}(z_{1}J)\right\}

Similarly

\Phi_{4}=\Phi_{4.1}-\Phi_{4.2}

where

\displaystyle\Phi_{4.1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}C_{3}\int d\phi\cos[2\phi-3\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+}+\tau\omega^{\prime}JB(q)\sin 2\phi_{-}]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}C_{3}\;{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[i(-3\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+})]\int d\phi\exp[i(2\phi+z_{1}J\sin 2\phi_{-})]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi C_{3}\;{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[i(-3\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+})]J_{-1}(z_{1}J)\exp[2i\Delta\phi]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\pi C_{3}\;{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[-i(\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]J_{1}(z_{1}J)\right\}

using
J_{-1}(z)=-J_{1}(z)
.

Next

\displaystyle\Phi_{4.2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}C_{3}\int d\phi\cos[2\phi-\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+}-\tau\omega^{\prime}JB(q)\sin 2\phi_{-}]\exp[B(q)\frac{J}{J_{0}}\sin 2\phi_{-}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}C_{3}\;{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[i(-\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]\int d\phi\exp[i(2\phi-z_{1}^{*}J\sin 2\phi_{-})]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi C_{3}\;{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[i(-\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]J_{-1}(-z_{1}^{*}J)\exp[2i\Delta\phi]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi C_{3}\;{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[i(\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]J_{1}(z_{1}^{*}J)\right\}

using
J_{-1}(-z)=J_{1}(z)
. Hence

\Phi_{4}=-\pi C_{3}\;{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[-i(\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]J_{-1}(z_{1}J)+\exp[i(\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]J_{1}(z_{1}^{*}J)\right\}

Next

\Phi_{5}=\Phi_{5.1}+\Phi_{5.2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}C_{5}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[-i(3\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]\int d\phi\exp[i(4\phi+z_{1}J\sin 2\phi_{-})]\right\}

\displaystyle\pi C_{5}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i(\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+})]J_{2}(z_{1}J)\right\}

\displaystyle\Phi_{5.2}

For

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}C_{7}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i(-3\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+})]\int d\phi\exp[i(2\phi-z_{1}^{*}J\sin 2\phi_{-})]\right\}

\displaystyle=
