Consider the exponent terms in the
\Phi_{i}

\displaystyle\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J)(t-\tau)+\tau(\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2}))=\omega_{\beta}t+(t+\frac{1}{2}q^{2}\tau)\omega^{\prime}J

\displaystyle\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J)(t-\tau)-\tau(\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2}))=\omega_{\beta}(t-2\tau)+(t-2\tau-\frac{1}{2}q^{2}\tau)\omega^{\prime}J

Then

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta_{K}\theta}{J_{0}^{2}}C_{1}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\int dJ\;J\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})]\exp[i(\omega_{\beta}(t-2\tau)+(t-2\tau-\frac{1}{2}q^{2}\tau)\omega^{\prime}J)]J_{1}(z_{1}J)\right\}

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta}{J_{0}^{2}}C_{1}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\int dJ\;J\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})]\exp[i(\omega_{\beta}t+(t+\frac{1}{2}q^{2}\tau)\omega^{\prime}J)]J_{0}(z_{1}J)\right\}

Introduce the dimensionless integration variable
u=J/J_{0}
and define

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\beta}(t-2\tau)

(10.30)

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})-i(t-2\tau-\frac{1}{2}q^{2}\tau)\omega^{\prime}J_{0}

(10.31)

\displaystyle a_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})-i(t+\frac{1}{2}q^{2}\tau)\omega^{\prime}J_{0}

(10.32)

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z_{1}J_{0}=B(q)\left(\tau\omega^{\prime}J_{0}-i\right)

(10.33)

where
a_{1},a_{2},b
are complex dimensionless parameters, independent of
J
.
It follows that

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta C_{1}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi]\int du\;u\exp[-a_{1}u]J_{1}(bu)\right\}

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\beta_{K}\theta C_{1}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\omega_{\beta}t]\int du\;u\exp[-a_{2}u]J_{0}(bu)\right\}

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta C_{3}\;{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[-i\Phi]\int du\;u\exp[-a_{1}^{*}u]J_{0}(b^{*}u)-\exp[i\Phi]\int du\;u\exp[-a_{1}u]J_{2}(bu)\right\}

\displaystyle I_{4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta J_{0}^{2}C_{3}\;{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[-i\omega_{\beta}t]\int du\;u\exp[-a_{2}^{*}u]J_{1}(bu)+\exp[i\omega_{\beta}t]\int du\;u\exp[-a_{2}u]J_{1}(b^{*}u)\right\}

Using the integration results in the appendix

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta C_{1}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi]\frac{b}{(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{3/2}}\right\}

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\beta_{K}\theta C_{1}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\omega_{\beta}t]\frac{a_{2}}{(a_{2}^{2}+b^{2})^{3/2}}\right\}

Consider only the terms that depend on
t-2\tau
rather than on
t
alone. These are likely to be the
dominant terms at long times. Besides
I_{1},I_{3}
, these are

\displaystyle I_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}J_{0}\theta C_{5}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi]\int du\;u^{2}\exp[-a_{1}u][J_{0}(bu)+J_{2}(buJ)]\right\}

\displaystyle I_{6.2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta J_{0}C_{5}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi]\int du\;u^{2}\exp[-a_{1}u]J_{1}(bu)\right\}

\displaystyle I_{7}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta C_{7}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi]\int du\;u\exp[-a_{1}u][J_{1}(bu)+\frac{1}{2}J_{3}(bu)]\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{1}{2}\exp[-i\Phi]\int du\;u\exp[-a_{1}u]J_{1}(b^{*}u)\right\}

\displaystyle I_{8.3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\beta_{K}\theta C_{7}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\omega_{\beta}(t-2\tau)]\int du\;u\exp[-a_{1}u]J_{2}(bu)\right\}

\displaystyle I_{9}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\beta_{K}\theta J_{0}C_{9}{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[-i\Phi]\int du\;u^{2}\exp[-a_{1}u]J_{1}(b^{*}u)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\exp[i\Phi]\int du\;u^{2}\exp[-a_{1}u]J_{3}(bu)\right\}

The different integrals can be represented as the complex function

H_{m,n}(a,b)=\int du\;u^{m}\exp[-au]J_{n}(bu)

(10.34)

where the integers
m,n
take one of the values
m=1,2
and
n=0,1,2,3
and
(a,z)
are complex.
In terms of this function
H_{m,n}
and phase, we can express the different terms as

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta C_{1}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi]H_{1,1}(a_{1},b)\right\}

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta C_{3}\;{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[-i\Phi]H_{1,0}(a_{1}^{*},b^{*})-\exp[i\Phi]H_{1,2}(a_{1},b)\right\}

\displaystyle I_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta J_{0}C_{5}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi](H_{2,0}(a_{1},b)+H_{2,2}(a_{1},b))\right\}

\displaystyle I_{6.2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta J_{0}C_{5}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi]H_{2,1}(a_{1},b)\right\}

\displaystyle I_{7}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta C_{7}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi][H_{1,1}(a_{1},b)+\frac{1}{2}H_{1,3}(a_{1},b)]+\frac{1}{2}\exp[-i\Phi]H_{1,1}(a_{1},b^{*})\right\}

\displaystyle I_{8.3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\beta_{K}\theta C_{7}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi]H_{1,2}(a_{1},b)\right\}

\displaystyle I_{9}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\beta_{K}\theta J_{0}C_{9}{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[-i\Phi]H_{2,1}(a_{1},b^{*})+\exp[i\Phi]H_{2,3}(a_{1},b)\right\}

Using the integrations from the appendix, we have

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta C_{1}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi]\frac{b}{(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{3/2}}\right\}

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta C_{3}\;{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[-i\Phi]\frac{a_{1}^{*}}{((a_{1}^{*})^{2}+(b^{*})^{2})^{3/2}}-\exp[i\Phi]\frac{2(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{3/2}-a_{1}(2a_{1}^{2}+3b^{2})}{b^{2}(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{3/2}}\right\}

\displaystyle I_{5}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta J_{0}C_{5}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi](\frac{(2a_{1}^{2}-b^{2})}{(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{5/2}}+\frac{3b^{2}}{(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{5/2}})\right\}

\displaystyle I_{6.2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta J_{0}C_{5}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi]\frac{3a_{1}b}{(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{5/2}}\right\}

\displaystyle I_{7}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta C_{7}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi]\left(\frac{b}{(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{3/2}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{8a_{1}^{4}+12a_{1}^{2}b^{2}+3b^{4}-8a_{1}(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{3/2}}{b^{3}(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{3/2}}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{1}{2}\exp[-i\Phi]\frac{b^{*}}{(a_{1}^{2}+(b^{*})^{2})^{3/2}}\right\}

\displaystyle I_{8.3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\beta_{K}\theta C_{7}\;{\rm Im}\left\{\exp[i\Phi]\frac{2(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{3/2}-a_{1}(2a_{1}^{2}+3b^{2})}{b^{2}(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{3/2}}\right\}

\displaystyle I_{9}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{4}\beta_{K}\theta J_{0}C_{9}{\rm Re}\left\{\exp[-i\Phi]\frac{3a_{1}b^{*}}{(a_{1}^{2}+(^{*})^{2})^{5/2}}+\exp[i\Phi]\frac{8(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{5/2}-a_{1}(8a_{1}^{4}+20a_{1}^{2}b^{2}+15b^{4})}{b^{3}(a_{1}^{2}+b^{2})^{5/2}}\right\}

10.1
FWHM of the 1D pulse in the nonlinear quad theory

•

conservation of pulse area

•

minimum of fwhm as a function of parameters

Definitions of notation

\displaystyle\tau_{D}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}},\;\;\;\eta=\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}\tau

\displaystyle\Rightarrow Q

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle q\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}\tau=q\eta

\displaystyle\xi(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(t-2\tau)\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}

Recap of the linear quad kick theory

The echo amplitude un the linear quad kick approximation is

\langle x(t)\rangle_{linear}=\beta_{K}\theta\frac{Q}{[(1+\xi^{2}(t)]^{3/2}}\sin(\Phi(t)+3{\rm Arctan}[\xi(t)]-\frac{1}{2}q)

(10.35)

At
t\simeq 2\tau
, the amplitude takes the maximum value

{\rm Ampl}=\beta_{K}\theta\frac{Q}{[(1+\xi^{2}(t)]^{3/2}}

(10.36)

The phase term in the echo shape leads only to fast oscillations at the betatron frequency, The shape of the
echo is determined by the envelope
The max echo amplitude is therefore

{\rm Ampl}^{max}=\beta_{K}\theta Q\;\;\;\;\Rightarrow\;\;\frac{{\rm Ampl}^{max}}{\beta_{K}\theta}=Q

(10.37)

The second equation above defines the echo amplitude scaled by the dipole kick.
The echo amplitude falls to half the max value at times
\Delta t_{HFHM}
(the half width at half max) before and after the time
2\tau
.
Hence this time is given by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{[1+\xi^{2}(2\tau\pm\Delta t_{HFHM}^{3/2}]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}

(10.38)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{1}{1+\Delta t_{HFHM}\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{1}{2})^{2/3}

(10.39)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\Delta t_{HFHM}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}}{\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}

(10.40)

Hence
\Delta t_{FWHM}=2\Delta t_{HFHM}=\frac{1.532}{\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}}
; hence the pulse width is independent of
the quad strength.

{\rm Scaled\;Echo\;amplitude}\times\Delta t_{FWHM}=2\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}q\tau

(10.41)

The term in the LHS is roughly the pulse area , In this linear quad kick theory, the pulse area depends only on the
dipole and quad kicks and the delay time, i.e only on the external parameters. The area is independent of the
internal variables, the detuning and the emittance.

Echo amplitude and FWHM in the nonlinear theory

The time dependent echo pulse in the nonlinear quad theory is given by Eq.(2.39) and (2.40) in the 2018
PRAB paper

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\sin[\Phi(t)+3\Theta(t)-\frac{1}{2}q]

(10.42)

The echo amplitude at
t=2\tau
is approximated by

\langle x(t=2\tau)\rangle^{amp}\approx\beta_{k}\theta\frac{Q}{(1+Q^{2})^{3/2}}

(10.43)

The FWHM time can therefore be found from

\frac{Q}{[(1-\xi^{2}(\Delta t_{HFHM})+Q^{2})^{2}+4\xi^{2}(\Delta t_{HFHM})]^{3/4}}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{Q}{(1+Q^{2})^{3/2}}

(10.44)

which leads to (writing
r_{HF}=\xi^{2}(\Delta t_{HFHM})
and
y=(1+Q^{2})

\displaystyle[(y-r_{HF})^{2}+4r_{HF}]^{3/4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2y^{3/2}

which (using Mathematica) has the solutions (the positive root is
\Delta t^{+}_{HWFM}
)

\xi^{+}_{HFHM}=\left\{Q^{2}-1+\sqrt{2}\left[2^{1/3}(1+Q^{2})^{2}-2Q^{2}\right]^{1/2}\right\}^{1/2}\\

(10.45)

Hence
which results in the expression for the FWHM

\displaystyle\Delta t^{+}_{HFHM}=\xi^{+}_{HFHM}\tau_{Dec}=\tau_{Dec}\left\{Q^{2}-1+\sqrt{2}\left[2^{1/3}(1+Q^{2})^{2}-2Q^{2}\right]^{1/2}\right\}^{1/2}

(10.46)

Expanding this expression to
O(Q^{2})
for small
Q
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta t^{+}_{HFHM}}{\tau_{Dec}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}+\frac{2^{2/3}-2^{1/3}+1}{2\sqrt{2^{2/3}-1}}Q^{2}+O(Q^{4})

(10.48)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.766+0.866Q^{2}+O(Q^{4})

(10.49)

10.2
Superposition of Nonlinear Quadrupole Kicks

We consider the case of two quadrupole kicks
q_{0},q_{1}
applied at times
\tau,\tau+\Delta t_{1}

respectively. In most cases we expect
\Delta t_{1}
to be within a few turns, so
\Delta t_{1}=mT_{rev},m=1,2,...
. After the 1st quad kick, the action and phase in the arguments of the distribution function had
transformed to

J\rightarrow J+A(q_{0},\phi),\;\;\;\;\phi\rightarrow\tan^{-1}(\tan\phi-q_{0})

and the distribution function at any time
t
after
\tau
was written as

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J+A(q,\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))J)\sqrt{(J+A(q,\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))J)}

(10.50)

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\tan^{-1}\left(\tan(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))-q\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\omega(J+A(q,(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)))J)\tau\right]

We define

\delta\varphi_{0}(J,t)=\omega(J)(t-\tau)

(10.51)

which we note is independent of
\phi
.
Making the same approximations as before, we simplify
\psi_{5}
to

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

(10.52)

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[(\phi-\delta\varphi_{0}(t))-\tau\omega(J+A(q_{0},(\phi-\delta\varphi_{0}(t))J)\right]

Hence at time
t=t_{1}=\tau+\Delta t_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

(10.53)

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[(\phi-\delta\varphi_{0})-\tau\omega(J+A(q_{0},(\phi-\delta\varphi_{0}))J)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[(\phi-\delta\varphi_{0})-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J[1+A(q_{0},(\phi-\delta\varphi_{0}))]\right\}\right]

At this time, the second quadrupole kick with strength
q_{1}
is applied changing the distribution
function to

\psi_{6}(J,\phi,t_{1,+})=\psi_{5}(x,p+q_{1}x,t_{1})\simeq\psi_{5}(J+A(q_{1},\phi)J,\phi,t_{1})

(10.54)

where as before we dropped the small change in the phase
\phi
.
Under this transformation,
\delta\varphi_{0}
transforms as

\delta\varphi_{0}(J,\tau+\Delta t_{1})\rightarrow\Delta\phi_{0}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{1})=\omega(J+A(q_{1},\phi)J)\Delta t_{1}=[\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J(1+A(q_{1},\phi))]\Delta t_{1}

(10.55)

Here, unlike
\delta\varphi_{0}
,
\Delta\phi_{0}
is a function of
\phi
but evaluated at the fixed time

t=t_{1}=\tau+\Delta t_{1}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\psi_{6}(J,\phi,t_{1,+})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\phi-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi)-\tau\omega\left(J+A(q_{1},\phi)J+A(q_{0},\phi-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi))(J+A(q_{1},\phi)J)\right)\right]

Expanding the argument of
\omega

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle A(q_{1},\phi)J+A(q_{0},(\phi-\Delta\phi_{0}))(J+A(q_{1},\phi)J)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J+A(q_{1},\phi)J+A(q_{0},(\phi-\Delta\phi_{0})J+A(q_{0},\phi-\Delta\phi_{0})A(q_{1},\phi)J

Since
A(q,\phi)=-q\sin 2\phi+q^{2}\cos^{2}\phi
and we drop terms of
O(q^{2})
from the argument of
the sine function, we can drop the product terms
A(q_{0},\phi-\Delta\phi_{0})A(q_{1},\phi)
, so we can
write

\displaystyle\psi_{6}(J,\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\phi-\Delta\phi_{0}-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left(1++A(q_{1},\phi)J+A(q_{0},(\phi-\Delta\phi_{0}))\right)\right\}\right]

At any time
t>t_{1}=\tau+\Delta t_{1}
, the distribution evolves by rotation to

\psi_{7}(J,\phi,t)=\psi_{6}(J,\phi-\omega(J)(t-(\tau+\Delta t_{1})))

(10.56)

Define

\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t)=\omega(J)(t-(\tau+\Delta t_{1}))

(10.57)

which like
\delta\varphi_{0}
is also independent of
\phi
.
Then at times
t\geq t_{1}=\tau+\Delta t_{1}

\displaystyle\psi_{7}(J,\phi,t

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle t_{1})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

(10.58)

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\phi-\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t)-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t))\right.

\displaystyle-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{1},\phi-\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t))\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+A(q_{0},\phi-\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t)-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t)))\right]\right\}\right]

I need to consider a third kick at time
t=\tau+\Delta t_{1}+\Delta t_{2}
to get the correct pattern.
From the above, it is clear that
\phi-\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t)
will be replaced by

\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t)
where
\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t)=\omega(J)(t-[\tau+\Delta t_{1}+\Delta t_{2}])
.
It is not yet clear how
\Delta\phi_{0}
will be replaced. The next variable in this series will be

\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})=\omega(J+A(q_{2},\phi)J)\Delta t_{2}=[\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J(1+A(q_{2},\phi))]\Delta t_{2}

At time
t_{2}=\tau+\Delta t_{1}+\Delta t_{2}
, a quadrupole kick with strength
q_{2}
is applied.
The distribution changes to

\psi_{8}(J,\phi,t_{2})=\psi_{7}(x,p+q_{2}x,t)=\psi_{7}(J+A(q_{2},\phi)J,\phi,t_{2})

(10.59)

Now
\delta\varphi_{1}
transforms to

\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t-(\tau+\Delta t_{1}+\Delta t_{2}))\rightarrow\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})=\omega(J+A(q_{2},\phi)J)\Delta t_{2}=[\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J(1+A(q_{2},\phi))]\Delta t_{2}

(10.60)

Hence

\displaystyle\psi_{8}(J,\phi,t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{2})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

(10.61)

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2}))\right.

\displaystyle-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J[1+A(q_{2},\phi)]\left[1+A(q_{1},\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2}))\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+A(q_{0},\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})))\right]\right\}\right]

Expanding the terms in [] and keeping only terms linear in
A
, so dropping terms
A(q_{2},...)A(q_{1},..)

and
A(q_{2},...)A(q_{0},..)

\displaystyle[1+A(q_{2},\phi)]

\displaystyle\left[1+A(q_{1},\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2}))\right.

\displaystyle\left.+A(q_{0},\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})))\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+A(q_{2},\phi)+A(q_{1},\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2}))

\displaystyle A(q_{0},\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})))

Then

\displaystyle\psi_{8}(J,\phi,t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{2})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2}))\right.

\displaystyle-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{2},\phi)+A(q_{1},\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2}))\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+A(q_{0},\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})))\right]\right\}\right]

At times
t\geq\tau+\Delta t_{1}+\Delta t_{2}
, we define

\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t)=\omega(J)(t-[\tau+\Delta t_{1}+\Delta t_{2}])

(10.62)

The distribution function at these times is therefore

\psi_{9}(J,\phi,t\geq t_{2})=\psi_{8}(J,\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t))

(10.63)

Hence

\displaystyle\psi_{9}(

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle,\phi,t\geq t_{2})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t))-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t)))\right.

\displaystyle-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{2},\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t))+A(q_{1},\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t)))\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+A(q_{0},\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t))-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t))))\right]\right\}\right]

Let

\Phi(J,\phi,t)=\phi-\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t)

(10.64)

We can rewrite

\displaystyle\psi_{9}(

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle,\phi,t\geq t_{2})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

(10.65)

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\Phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi))\right.

\displaystyle-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{2},\Phi)+A(q_{1},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi))\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi)))\right]\right\}\right]

At time

, a kick
q_{3}
is applied. Due to the replacement of

, we have

\delta\varphi_{2}(J,t)\rightarrow\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{3})=[\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J(1+A(q_{3},\phi))]\Delta t_{3}

(10.67)

We had

\displaystyle\psi_{7}(J,\phi,t

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle t_{1})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\phi-\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t)-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t))\right.

\displaystyle-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{1},\phi-\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t))\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+A(q_{0},\phi-\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t)-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\delta\varphi_{1}(J,t)))\right]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\psi_{8}(J,\phi,t

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{2})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2}))\right.

\displaystyle-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{2},\phi)+A(q_{1},\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2}))\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+A(q_{0},\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})-\Delta\phi_{0}(\phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{2})))\right]\right\}\right]

Hence, by direct inspection, we can write
\psi_{10}
by replacing

in

above by

\Delta\phi_{2}
and add the additional term
A(q_{3},\phi)

\displaystyle\psi_{9}(

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle,\phi,t\geq t_{2})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\Phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi))\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi)))\right]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\psi_{10}(

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle,\phi,t=t_{3})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

\displaystyle-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{3},\phi)+A(q_{2},\Phi^{\prime})+A(q_{1},\Phi^{\prime}-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi^{\prime}))\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+A(q_{0},\Phi^{\prime}-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi^{\prime})-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi^{\prime}-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi^{\prime})))\right]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\phi,\Delta t_{3})

Now define
\delta\varphi_{3}
and redefine
\Phi

\delta\varphi_{3}(J,t)=\omega(J)(t-(\tau+\Delta t_{1}+\Delta t_{2}+\Delta t_{3})),\;\;\;\Phi=\phi-\delta\varphi_{3}(J,t)

(10.68)

We observe that
\psi_{9}
is obtained from
\psi_{8}
by the replacement

.
We can obtain the DF

at any time

by replacing

in

. Hence

\displaystyle\psi_{11}(

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle,\phi,t\geq t_{3})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

(10.69)

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi))\right.

\displaystyle-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)))

\displaystyle-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{3},\Phi)+A(q_{2},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi))\right.\right.

\displaystyle+A(q_{1},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)))

\displaystyle+A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi))

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi))))\right]\right\}\right]

Let us consider the DF at times
t
after each quadrupole kick

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t\geq\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}\sin\left[\Phi-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J[1+A(q_{0},\Phi)]\right\}\right]

(10.71)

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi))\right]\right\}\right]

(10.72)

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi)))\right]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\psi_{11}(J,\phi,t\geq t_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}

\displaystyle+A(q_{1},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)))

\displaystyle+A\left(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi))\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.\left.-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)))\right)\right]\right\}\right]

In each case, the definition of
\Phi
is determined by the number of kicks

\Phi(J,\phi,t)=\phi-\omega(J)[t-(\tau+\sum_{n=0}\Delta t_{n})]

(10.74)

We can write the last DF in the form

\displaystyle\psi_{11}(J,\phi,t\geq t_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}\sin\left[\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{2}-\Delta\Phi_{1}-\Delta\Phi_{0}\right.

(10.75)

\displaystyle-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{3},\Phi)+A(q_{2},\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{2})+A(q_{1},\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{2}-\Delta\Phi_{1})\right.\right.

\displaystyle+\left.\left.\left.A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{2}-\Delta\Phi_{1}-\Delta\Phi_{0})\right]\right\}\right]

where the
\Delta\Phi_{N-j}
is obtained recursively from
\Delta\Phi_{N-j+1}
.
Now the pattern is clear.

Define the functions
\Delta\Phi
as follows

\Delta\Phi_{N-j}=\Delta\phi_{N-j}(J,\Phi-\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\Delta\Phi_{N-k}),\;\;\;j=0,1,...N

(10.76)

The first function in the chain
\Delta\phi_{N}\propto\Delta t_{N+1}\equiv 0
, hence

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi_{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi_{N-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{N-1}(J,\Phi)

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi_{N-2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{N-2}(J,\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{N-1})=\Delta\phi_{N-2}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{N-1}(J,\Phi))

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi_{N-3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{N-2}(J,\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{N-1}-\Delta\Phi_{N-2})=...

After
(N+1)
kicks applied at times
t_{0}\equiv\tau,t_{1}=t_{0}+\Delta t_{1},...,t_{N}=t_{N-1}+\Delta t_{N}
, the distribution function is

\displaystyle\psi(

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle,\phi,t\geq t_{N})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}\sin\left[\Phi-\sum_{j=0}^{N}\Delta\Phi_{N-j}\right.

(10.77)

\displaystyle\left.-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+\sum_{j=0}^{N}A\left(q_{j},\Phi-\sum_{k=j}^{N}\Delta\Phi_{k}\right)\right]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\Phi(J,\phi,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi-\omega(J)[t-(\tau+\sum_{n=0^{N-1}}\Delta t_{n})]

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi_{N-j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{N-j}(J,\Phi-\sum_{k=0}^{j-1}\Delta\Phi_{N-k})

(10.78)

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{j}(J,\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J(1+A(q_{j+1},\phi))]\Delta t_{j+1}

(10.79)

\displaystyle t_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle t_{j-1}+\Delta t_{j}=\tau+\sum_{k=1}^{j}\Delta t_{k}

(10.80)

Check the expansion for the different cases.

N=0
. Then after the kick at
t_{0}=\tau
, Here by definition
\Delta\Phi_{0}=0
and there are no
lower members in the recursive chain.

\displaystyle\psi(J,\phi,t\geq t_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}\sin\left[\Phi-\tau\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J[1+A(q_{0},\Phi)]\}\right]

\displaystyle\Phi(J,\phi,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi-\omega(J)[t-\tau]

This agrees with Eq.(
10.70
).

N=1
. Here we have

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{0}(J,\Phi),\;\;\;\;\Delta\Phi_{1}=0

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{1}A(

\displaystyle q_{j},

\displaystyle\Phi-\sum_{k=j}^{1}\Delta\Phi_{k})=A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{0})+A(q_{1},\Phi)

\displaystyle\psi(

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle,\phi,t\geq t_{1})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}\sin\left[\Phi-\Delta\phi_{0}(J,\Phi)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{0}(J,\Phi))+A(q_{1},\Phi)\right]\right\}\right]

This agrees with Eq.(
10.71
).

N-2
. Now
\Delta\Phi_{2}=0
,

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi_{1}`

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\sum_{k=0}^{0}\Delta\Omega_{2-k})=\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi)

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{0}(J,\Phi-\sum_{k=0}^{1}\Delta\Phi_{2-k})=\Delta\phi_{0}(J,\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{0}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi))

\displaystyle\psi(J,\phi,t\geq t_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}\sin\left[\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{1}-\Delta\Phi_{0}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{2},\Phi)+A(q_{1},\Phi-\sum_{k=1}^{2}\Delta\Phi_{j})+A(q_{0},\Phi-\sum_{k=0}^{2}\Delta\Phi_{j})\right]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}\sin\left[\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{1}-\Delta\Phi_{0}\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{2},\Phi)+A(q_{1},\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{1})+A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{0}-\Delta\Phi_{1})\right]\right\}\right]

This agrees with Eq.(
10.72
).

N=3
. Here
\Delta\Phi_{3}=0
, and

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\sum_{k=0}^{1}\Delta\Phi_{3-k})=\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi))

\displaystyle\Delta\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{0}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)-\Delta\phi_{1}(J,\Phi-\Delta\phi_{2}(J,\Phi)))

\displaystyle\psi(J,\phi,t\geq t_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}\sin\left[\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{2}-\Delta\Phi_{1}-\Delta\Phi_{0}\right.

\displaystyle-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{3},\Phi)+A(q_{2},\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{2})\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.+A(q_{1},\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{2}-\Delta\Phi_{1})+A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\Phi_{2}-\Delta\Phi_{1}-\Delta\Phi_{0})\right]\right\}\right]

This agrees with Eq.(
10.75
) which is the symbolic form of the expanded form
Eq.(
10.73
).

The dipole moment at a time
t\geq t_{N}
is given by

\displaystyle\langle x(t\geq t_{N})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dJd\phi\;\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi\psi(J,\phi,t\geq t_{N})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\beta_{K}\theta\int dJd\phi\;J\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\cos\phi\sin\left[\Phi-B(J,\phi)-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J[1+C(J,\phi)]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle B(J,\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N}\Delta\Phi_{N-j}

(10.82)

\displaystyle C(J,\phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N}A\left(q_{j},\Phi-\sum_{k=j}^{N}\Delta\Phi_{k}\right)

(10.83)

Caveats

•

The approximations made at each kick of replacing

\sqrt{(J+A(q,\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))J)}\approx\sqrt{J}
and similarly in the argument of

\psi_{0}(J)^{\prime}
will start to accumulate larger errors with increasing kicks.

We will also approximate

\displaystyle\cos\phi\sin[\Phi+f(J,\phi)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}[\sin(\Phi+f(J,\phi)+\phi)+\sin(\Phi+f(J,\phi)-\phi)]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sin(\Phi+f(J,\phi)+\phi)

since the dropped second term decreases rapidly with time, compared to the first term.
Hence

\displaystyle\langle x(t\geq t_{N})\rangle

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\int dJd\phi\;J\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sin\left[\Phi+\phi-B(J,\phi)\right.

(10.84)

\displaystyle\left.-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J[1+C(J,\phi)]\right\}\right]

Now, I will need to consider specific cases of
N
in order to do the integration.
First consider
N=1
with the distribution function

\displaystyle\psi(J,\phi,t\geq t_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}\beta_{K}\theta\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sqrt{J}\sin\zeta

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\Phi-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi)-\tau\left\{\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J\left[1+A(q_{1},\Phi)+A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi))\right]\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi-\omega(J)(t-t_{1})

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J(1+A(q_{1},\Phi))]\Delta t_{1}=[\omega(J)+\omega^{\prime}JA(q_{1},\Phi)]\Delta t_{1}

\displaystyle A(q_{1},\Phi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-q_{1}\sin 2\Phi

\displaystyle A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-q_{0}\sin 2(\Phi-\Delta\phi_{0})\Phi))

Using
t_{1}=\tau+\Delta t_{1}
, we have

\Phi-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi)=\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)-\omega^{\prime}JA(q_{1},\Phi)\Delta t_{1}

Now approximate
A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi))
as

\displaystyle A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-q_{0}\sin 2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)-\omega^{\prime}JA(q_{1},\Phi)\Delta t_{1})

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle-q_{0}\sin 2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))

by dropping the last term. We can estimate this term as follows by replacing
J\rightarrow\epsilon
and

\omega^{\prime}\epsilon A(q_{1},\Phi)\Delta t_{1}=-\frac{\Delta t_{1}}{\tau_{D}}q_{1}\sin 2\Phi\ll\sin 2\Phi

Both factors
q_{1}\ll 1
and
\Delta t_{1}/\tau_{D}\ll 1

for time intervals
\Delta t_{1}\ll\tau_{D}
, the decoherence time justifying dropping this term.
Hence we have

\displaystyle\zeta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)-\omega^{\prime}JA(q_{1},\Phi)\Delta t_{1}-\omega(J)\tau-\tau[A(q_{1},\Phi)+A(q_{0},\Phi-\Delta\phi_{0}(\Phi))]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi-\omega(J)t+\omega^{\prime}J[q_{0}\tau\sin 2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))+q_{1}t_{1}\sin 2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-t_{1}))]

The last form shows how it can be generalized for any
N
.

The dipole moment is (after dropping the term that decreases rapidly with time)

\displaystyle\langle x(t\geq t_{1})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\int dJ\;d\phi\;J\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sin[\phi+\zeta]

(10.86)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\beta_{K}\theta\int dJ\;d\phi\;J\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)\sin\left[2\phi-\omega(J)t\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\omega^{\prime}J\left\{q_{0}\tau\sin 2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau))+q_{1}t_{1}\sin 2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-t_{1}))\right\}\right]

This form suggests that after
N+1
kicks at times
t_{0}=\tau,t_{1},...,t_{N}
, the dipole moment will be

\displaystyle\langle x(t\geq t_{N})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\int dJ\;d\phi\;J\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)

(10.87)

\displaystyle\times\sin\left[2\phi-\omega(J)t+\omega^{\prime}J\sum_{j=0}^{N}q_{j}t_{j}\sin 2(\phi-\omega(J)(t-t_{j}))\right]

In order to do the integration over
\phi
, first I need to combine the sum over the sine functions into
a single sine function. Writing

\displaystyle\sum_{j}B_{j}\sin(2\phi+C_{j})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{j}(B_{j}\cos C_{j})\sin 2\phi+\sum_{j}(B_{j}\sin C_{j})\cos 2\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle Q\sin(2\phi+\xi)

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[(\sum_{j}B_{j}\cos C_{j})^{2}+(\sum_{j}B_{j}\sin C_{j})^{2}\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\tan)^{-1}\left[\frac{\sum_{j}B_{j}\sin C_{j}}{\sum_{j}B_{j}\cos C_{j}}\right]

The expressions for
Q,\xi
show the nonlinear superposition of sinusoidal harmonic forces.

In our case, we have

B_{j}\equiv\omega^{\prime}Jq_{j}t_{j},\;\;\;\;C_{j}=-2\omega(J)(t-t_{j})

Hence here we have

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}J\left[\sum_{j=0}^{N}(q_{j}t_{j})^{2}\cos^{2}[2\omega(J)(t-t_{j})]+\sum_{j=0}^{N}(q_{j}t_{j})^{2}\sin^{2}[2\omega(J)(t-t_{j})]\right]^{1/2}\equiv\omega^{\prime}JQT_{N}

\displaystyle\xi(J)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tan^{-1}\left[\frac{\sum_{j=0}^{N}q_{j}t_{j}\sin 2\omega(J)(t-t_{j})}{\sum_{j=0}^{N}q_{j}t_{j}\cos 2\omega(J)(t-t_{j})}\right]

(10.89)

where
QT_{N}
has the dimension of time but is independent of
J
.
Hence

\displaystyle\langle x(t\geq t_{N})\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\int dJ\;J\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J){\rm Im}\left[e^{-i\omega(J)t}\int d\phi\;\exp[i\{2\phi+Q\sin(2\phi+\xi)\}]\right]

(10.90)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\pi\beta_{K}\theta{\rm Im}\left[\int dJ\;J\psi_{0}^{\prime}(J)e^{-i\omega(J)t}e^{-i\xi(J)}J_{1}(Q)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta{\rm Im}\left[\int dz\;ze^{-z}e^{-i\omega(\epsilon z)t}e^{-i\xi(\epsilon z)}J_{1}(\omega^{\prime}\epsilon QT_{N}z)\right]

where as before,
J_{1}
is the Bessel function and we substituted the form of
\psi_{0}
.
QT_{N}
is
independent of
z
.

Evaluating this analytically may not be possible because of the complicated dependence of
\xi(\epsilon z)
.

The maximum amplitude of this can be estimated by setting the phase factors to unity. In that case

\displaystyle\langle x(t\geq t_{N})\rangle^{max}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\int dz\;ze^{-z}J_{1}(\omega^{\prime}\epsilon QT_{N}z)

(10.91)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\frac{\omega^{\prime}\epsilon QT_{N}}{[1+(\omega^{\prime}\epsilon QT_{N})^{2}]^{3/2}}

With only the single kick at time
t_{0}=\tau
, we have
QT_{0}=q_{0}\tau
and the above maximum amplitude
agrees with the result obtained earlier for the amplitude at the time
t=2\tau
, see
Eq.(
10.43
).

Now the depressing part: As a function of
Q=\omega^{\prime}\epsilon QT_{N}
, this is exactly the same
functional form of
Q
as with a single quadrupole kick. Hence the optimum value of
Q
is the same as
before, i.e.
Q^{2}=1/2
and the maximum possible amplitude is the same as before

\langle x(t\geq t_{N})\rangle^{max,amp}/(\beta_{K}\theta)=\frac{2}{3\sqrt{3}}=0.38

10.3
Stimulated Echoes

In the field of magnetic resonance imaging, the standard spin echo is generated by using two rf pulses, the first to
excite the spins and the second to refocus the dephasing spins
[
16
]
. The addition of a third rf pulse again
refocuses the spins again after the first echo and leads to additional echoes. In
Section
11
, we saw that a single quadrupole kick
can lead to multiple echoes at times
4\tau,6\tau
etc. In this section, we briefly
consider the possibility of amplifying the echoes at
2\tau,4\tau,...
with additional quadrupole kicks.

We consider first the linear theory, where the echo amplitude is given by Eq.(
10.35
) in
Section
10

By linear superposition, it follows that if there are
N_{q}
kicks applied at times
\tau_{m}
,
m=1,...,N_{q}

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}\sum_{m=1}^{N_{q}}q_{m}\tau_{m}\frac{\sin(\Phi_{m}+3\tan^{-1}[\xi_{m}])}{(1+\xi_{m}^{2})^{3/2}}

(10.92)

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\beta}(t-2\tau_{m})\;\;\;\xi_{m}=\omega^{\prime}\epsilon(t-2\tau_{m})

We know that this is valid only in the regime of small
q<q_{opt}
. We consider first the case of amplifying the
echo at 2
tau
by using several small quadrupole kicks.
We consider two cases: 1) all kicks have the same strength:
q_{m}=q
; 2)
alternating sign quadrupole kicks:
q_{m}=(-1)^{m}q
.

As a
special case of the above, consider a
stimulated echo
at later times with 2 quadrupole
kicks, i.e.
N_{q}=1
and
the 2nd kick is applied at time
p\tau
after the 1st kick. In this case, we have

n_{gap}T_{rev}=p\tau
and

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}[q_{0}(\tau)\frac{\sin(\Phi_{0}+3\tan^{-1}[\xi_{0}])}{(1+\xi_{0}^{2})^{3/2}}+q_{1}((p+1)\tau)\frac{\sin(\Phi_{1}+3\tan^{-1}[\xi_{1}])}{(1+\xi_{1}^{2})^{3/2}}]

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\beta}(t-2\tau),\;\;\;\Phi_{1}=\omega_{\beta}(t-2(p+1)\tau)

\displaystyle\xi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}(t-2\tau),\;\;\;\xi_{1}=\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}(t-2(p+1)\tau)

Note that the second term is only applicable for times
t\geq(p+1)\tau
. If we assume that the
two kicks have the same sign,
q_{0}=q_{1}=q
and introducing the dimensionless quadrupole strength
parameter

Q=q\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}\tau

We have therefore

\langle x(t)\rangle=\beta_{K}\theta Q[\frac{\sin(\Phi_{0}+3\tan^{-1}[\xi_{0}])}{(1+\xi_{0}^{2})^{3/2}}+(p+1)\frac{\sin(\Phi_{1}+3\tan^{-1}[\xi_{1}])}{(1+\xi_{1}^{2})^{3/2}}]

(10.93)

The stimulated echo at time
t=4\tau
is given by this expression with

\displaystyle\Phi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\omega_{\beta}\tau 3,\;\;\;\Phi_{1}=2\omega_{\beta}\tau(2-(p+1))

\displaystyle\xi_{0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}\tau,\;\;\;\xi_{1}=2\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}\tau(2-(p+1))

11
Nonlinear dipole and quad kicks theory

Here I consider the complete distribution function following the dipole kick, instead of the 1st order Taylor expansion. At time
\tau
after the dipole kick, the DF is

\psi_{3}(J,\phi\tau)=\psi_{0}(J+\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{2J/\beta}\sin\phi_{-\tau}+(1/2)\beta_{k}\theta^{2}),\;\;\;\phi_{-\tau}\equiv\phi-\omega(J)\tau

(11.1)

After the quad kick,

J\rightarrow J(1+A(q,\phi)),\;\;\;\phi\rightarrow\phi-q

Hence the DF right after the quad kick is

\psi_{4}(J,\phi,\tau)=\psi_{0}(J(1+A(q,\phi))+\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{2/\beta}\sqrt{J(1+A(q,\phi))}\sin(\phi_{-\tau}-q)+(1/2)\beta_{k}\theta^{2})

(11.2)

where now

\phi_{-\tau}=\phi-\tau[\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J(1+A(q,\phi))]

(11.3)

At a time
t>\tau
, the DF is

\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t>\tau)=\psi_{4}(J,\phi_{-\Delta\phi}),\;\;\;\phi_{-\Delta\phi}=\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)\equiv\phi-\Delta\phi

(11.4)

The dipole moment is

\langle x(t)\rangle=\sqrt{2\beta}\int dJ\;\sqrt{J}\int d\phi\cos\phi\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t)

(11.5)

Under the change
\phi\rightarrow\phi_{-\Delta\phi}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{-\tau}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\phi-\omega(J)(t-\tau)-\omega(J+A(q,\phi)J)\tau=\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-\tau[\omega_{\beta}+J(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})-\omega^{\prime}JB(q)\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+}+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi},\;\;\;\;Q=\tau\omega^{\prime}B(q)\epsilon_{0},\;\;\;z=J/\epsilon_{0},\;\;\;\omega_{+}=\omega_{\beta}+(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})\omega^{\prime}J

Note that
\Delta\phi
is independent of the phase
\phi
but depends on
J
. Let

D(z)=\Delta\phi(z)+\tau\omega_{+}(z)+q,\;\;\;\Rightarrow\phi_{-\tau}-q\rightarrow\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-D(z)+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}

(11.6)

and we have

\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t)=\psi_{0}(J(1+A(q,\phi_{-\Delta\phi}))+\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{2/\beta}\sqrt{J(1+A(q,\phi_{-\Delta\phi}))}\sin(\phi-D(z)+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})+(1/2)\beta_{k}\theta^{2})

(11.7)

Since

\psi_{0}(J)=\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{J}{J_{0}}]

we have

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]\exp\left\{-\frac{1}{J_{0}}[J(1+A(q,\phi_{-\Delta\phi}))+\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{2/\beta}\sqrt{J(1+A(q,\phi_{-\Delta\phi}))}\right.

(11.8)

\displaystyle\left.\times\sin(\phi-D(z)+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})]\right\}

Making the approximation

\sqrt{1+A(q,\phi)}\approx 1+\frac{1}{2}A(q,\phi)=1+\frac{1}{4}q^{2}-\frac{1}{2}B(q)\sin(2\phi-q/2)\equiv C_{1}+C_{3}\sin(2\phi-q/2)

Caution: Does this approximation hold for
A(q,\phi_{-\Delta\phi})=-B(q)\sin[2(\phi-\omega(t-\tau))-q/2]+\frac{1}{2}q^{2}
when
t\gg\tau
? Yes, since that occurs in the argument of the sine.

The argument of the exponential inside the
\phi
integration is

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{J_{0}}\left\{J(-B(q)\sin(2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-q/2)+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})+\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}\left(1+\frac{1}{4}q^{2}-\frac{1}{2}B(q)\sin(2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-q/2)\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times\sin(\phi-D(z)+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}q^{2}\frac{J}{J_{0}}-\frac{1}{J_{0}}\left\{-B(q)J\sin(2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-q/2)+\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}(1+\frac{1}{4}q^{2})\sin(\phi-D(z)+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{1}{2}\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}B(q)\sin(2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-q/2)\sin(\phi-D(z)+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}q^{2}z+b_{1}z\sin(2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-q/2)-b_{2}\sqrt{z}\sin(\phi-D(z)+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})

\displaystyle+2b_{3}\sqrt{z}\sin(2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-q/2)\sin(\phi-D(z)+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})

where we replaced
z=J/J_{0}
and defined the positive definite dimensionless parameters

\displaystyle a_{\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\beta_{k}\theta}{\sigma_{0}},\;\;\;\;\;C_{1}=1+\frac{1}{4}q^{2}

\displaystyle b_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B(q)

\displaystyle b_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta J_{0}}}(1+\frac{1}{4}q^{2})=\sqrt{2}\frac{\beta_{k}\theta}{\sigma_{0}}(1+\frac{1}{4}q^{2})=\sqrt{2}C_{1}a_{\theta}

\displaystyle b_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta J_{0}}}B(q)=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}\frac{\beta_{k}\theta}{\sigma_{0}}B(q)=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}B(q)a_{\theta},\;\;\;\;\;b_{i}\geq 0

The dimensionless parameter
a_{\theta}
is the rms dipole kick in units of the rms beam size.
Clearly for
q\ll 1
, we have
b_{1}\ll 1
. Now using

\sin A\sin B=\frac{1}{2}(\cos(A-B)-\cos(A+B))

and

\displaystyle 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-q/2-(\phi-D(z)+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(\phi-\omega(t-\tau))-q/2-(\phi-\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}-q+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi-\omega(t-\tau)+\tau\omega_{+}-Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}+q/2

\displaystyle 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-q/2+(\phi-D(z)+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\phi-3\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-3q/2

Transforming from the variable
J
to
z=J/J_{0}
, we can write the exponent as

\displaystyle arg

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}q^{2}z+b_{1}z\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-b_{2}\sqrt{z}\sin(\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+}-q+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})

\displaystyle+b_{3}\sqrt{z}\cos[\phi-\omega(t-\tau)+\tau\omega_{+}-Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}+q/2]

\displaystyle-b_{3}\sqrt{z}\cos[3\phi-3\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-3q/2]

Thus we have for the dipole moment

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2\beta J_{0}}}{2\pi}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})z]T_{\phi}(z)

(11.9)

\displaystyle T_{\phi}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos\phi\exp\left[b_{1}z\sin(2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-q/2)-b_{2}\sqrt{z}\sin(\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+}-q+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})\right.

(11.11)

\displaystyle+b_{3}\sqrt{z}\cos\left(\phi-\omega(t-\tau)+\tau\omega_{+}-Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}+q/2\right)

\displaystyle\left.-b_{3}\cos\left(3\phi-3\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-3q/2\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\{\int d\phi e^{i\phi}\exp\left[b_{1}z\sin(2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-q/2)-b_{2}\sqrt{z}\sin(\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+}-q+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})\right.\right.

\displaystyle+b_{3}\sqrt{z}\cos\left(\phi-\omega(t-\tau)+\tau\omega_{+}-Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}+q/2\right)

\displaystyle\left.\left.-b_{3}\sqrt{z}\cos\left(3\phi-3\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-3q/2\right)\right]\right\}

One way to do the integration over
\phi
is to use the generating function for the modified Bessel functions

\exp[\frac{1}{2}(t+\frac{1}{t})z]=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}I_{n}(z)t^{n}

Firs setting
t=\pm e^{i\theta}
and then
t=ie^{\mp i\theta}
, we obtain

e^{\pm z\cos\theta}=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}(\pm 1)^{n}I_{n}(z)e^{in\theta},\;\;\;e^{\pm z\sin\theta}=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}i^{n}I_{n}(z)e^{\mp in\theta}

With these expansions

\displaystyle T_{\phi}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}}\sum_{k_{3}}\sum_{k_{4}}i^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(-1)^{k_{4}}I_{k_{1}}(b_{1}z)I_{k_{2}}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{3}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{4}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})\right.

\displaystyle\int d\phi e^{i\phi}\exp\left[i(-k_{1}(2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-q/2)+k_{2}(\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+}-q+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})\right.

\displaystyle+k_{3}(\phi-\omega(t-\tau)+\tau\omega_{+}-Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}+q/2)

\displaystyle\left.\left.+k_{4}(3\phi-3\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}-3q/2))\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}}\sum_{k_{3}}\sum_{k_{4}}i^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(-1)^{k_{4}}I_{k_{1}}(b_{1}z)I_{k_{2}}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{3}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{4}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})\right.

\displaystyle\times\exp[i(k_{1}(2\omega(t-\tau)+q/2)-k_{2}(\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}+q)-k_{3}(\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}-q/2)

\displaystyle-k_{4}(3\omega(t-\tau)+\tau\omega_{+}+3q/2))]

\displaystyle\left.\int d\phi\exp\left[i\left([1-2k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}+3k_{4}]\phi+(k_{2}+k_{4}-k_{3})Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}\right)\right]\right\}

To do the
\phi
integration, I have to expand into a Bessel function

\exp[i(k_{2}+k_{4}-k_{3})Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}]=\sum_{l=-\infty}^{infty}J_{l}((k_{2}+k_{4}-k_{3})Qz)\exp[il2(\phi-\Delta\phi)]

We have therefore

\displaystyle T_{\phi}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}}\sum_{k_{3}}\sum_{k_{4}}\sum_{l}i^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(-1)^{k_{4}}I_{k_{1}}(b_{1}z)I_{k_{2}}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{3}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{4}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})J_{l}((k_{2}+k_{4}-k_{3})Qz)\right.

\displaystyle\times\exp[i(k_{1}(2\omega(t-\tau)+q/2)-k_{2}(\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}+q)-k_{3}(\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}-q/2)

\displaystyle-k_{4}(3\omega(t-\tau)+\tau\omega_{+}+3q/2))-2l\Delta\phi]

\displaystyle\left.\int d\phi\exp\left[i(1-2k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}+3k_{4}+2l)\phi\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi{\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}}\sum_{k_{3}}\sum_{k_{4}}\sum_{l}i^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(-1)^{k_{4}}I_{k_{1}}(b_{1}z)I_{k_{2}}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{3}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{4}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})J_{l}((k_{2}+k_{4}-k_{3})Qz)\right.

\displaystyle\times\exp[i(k_{1}(2\omega(t-\tau)+q/2)-k_{2}(\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}+q)-k_{3}(\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}-q/2)

\displaystyle\left.-k_{4}(3\omega(t-\tau)+\tau\omega_{+}+3q/2))-2l\Delta\phi]\delta(1-2k_{1}+k_{2}+k_{3}+3k_{4}+2l)\right\}

We can replace
k_{3}
by
2k_{1}-k_{2}-3k_{4}-2l-1
, drop the sum over
k_{3}
and for convenience, replace
k_{4}
by
k_{3}
.
Writing

\tau\omega_{+}=\omega\tau+\frac{1}{2}q^{2}\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{o}\tau z=\omega\tau+Q_{2}z,\;\;\;\;Q_{2}=\frac{1}{2}q^{2}\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}\tau

where
Q_{2}\sim O(q^{2})
and dimensionless. The phase factor simplifies to

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{1}(2\omega(t-\tau)+q/2)-k_{2}(\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}+q)-(2k_{1}-k_{2}-3k_{3}-2l-1)(\omega(t-\tau)-\tau\omega_{+}-q/2)

\displaystyle-k_{3}(3\omega(t-\tau)+\tau\omega_{+}+3q/2))-2l\omega(t-\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega\left[t-2\tau(-k_{1}+2k_{3}+l+11)\right]+Q_{2}z(2k_{1}-4k_{3}-2l-1)+q((3/2)k_{1}-(3/2)k_{2}-3k_{3}-1/2)

while the argument of the Bessel function is

\displaystyle(k_{2}+k_{4}-k_{3})Qz

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(k_{2}+k_{4}-(2k_{1}-k_{2}-3k_{4}-2l-1))Qz=(2(l+k_{2}+2k_{4}-k_{1})+1)Qz

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle(2(l+k_{2}+2k_{3}-k_{1})+1)Qz

Hence

\displaystyle T_{\phi}(J)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi{\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}}\sum_{k_{3}}\sum_{l}i^{k_{1}+k_{2}}(-1)^{k_{3}}\exp[iq((3/2)k_{1}-(3/2)k_{2}-3k_{3}-1/2)]\right.

\displaystyle I_{k_{1}}(b_{1}z)I_{k_{2}}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{3}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})I_{2k_{1}-k_{2}-3k_{3}-1}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})J_{l}([2(l+k_{2}+2k_{3}-k_{1})+1]Qz)

\displaystyle\left.\times\exp\left(i\left[\omega(t-2\tau(-k_{1}+2k_{3}+l+1))+Q_{2}z(2k_{1}-4k_{3}-2l-1)\right]\right)\right\}

Comments

•

The form of the phase factor shows the possibility of multiple echoes. Since the amplitude is
locally maximum when the phase factor vanishes, the form above shows that echoes occur at (dropping the small contribution
from
q
) when

t-2\tau(2k_{3}+l-k_{1}+1)=0

This predicts echoes only at multiples of 2
\tau

1
Since we want to identify the amplitudes of the lowest order echoes at
2\tau,4\tau
, we replace

-k_{1}+2k_{3}+l=m,\;\;\;k_{1}=2k_{3}+l-m

\displaystyle(3/2)k_{1}-(3/2)k_{2}-3k_{3}-1/2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(3l-3m-k_{2}-1)

\displaystyle 2k_{1}-k_{2}-3k_{3}-1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4k_{3}+2l-2m-k_{2}-3k_{3}-1=k_{3}+2l-k_{2}-2m-1

\displaystyle l+k_{2}+2k_{3}-k_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle l+k_{2}+2k_{3}-2k_{3}-l+m=k_{2}+m

\displaystyle 2k_{1}-4k_{3}-2l-1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4k_{3}+2l-2m-4k_{3}-2l-1=-2m-1

Subsequently we can replace
k_{2}
by
k_{1}
and
k_{3}
by
k_{2}
. Hence

\displaystyle T_{\phi}(J)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi{\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{m}\sum_{k_{2}}\sum_{k_{3}}\sum_{l}i^{k_{2}+2k_{3}+l-m}(-1)^{k_{3}}\exp[i\frac{1}{2}q(3l-3m-k_{2}-1)]\right.

\displaystyle I_{2k_{3}+l-m}(b_{1}z)I_{k_{2}}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{3}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{3}+2l-k_{2}-2m-1}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})J_{l}([2(k_{2}+m)+1]Qz)

\displaystyle\left.\times\exp\left(i\left[\omega(t-2\tau(m+1))-Q_{2}z(2m+1)\right]\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi{\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{m}\sum_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}}\sum_{l}i^{k_{1}+l-m}\exp\left(i\left[\omega(t-2\tau(m+1))-Q_{2}z(2m+1)\right]\right)\exp[i\frac{1}{2}q(3l-3m-k_{1}-1)]\right.

\displaystyle\left.I_{2k_{2}+l-m}(b_{1}z)I_{k_{1}}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{2}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{2}+2l-k_{1}-2m-1}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})J_{l}([2(k_{1}+m)+1]Qz)\right\}

\displaystyle\times I_{k_{2}+2l-k_{1}-1}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})J_{l}([2k_{1}+1]Qz)

\displaystyle+e^{i[\omega(t-4\tau)-3Q_{2}z]}\sum_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}}\sum_{l}i^{k_{1}+l-1}e^{i\frac{1}{2}q(3l-k_{1}-4)}I_{2k_{2}+l-1}(b_{1}z)I_{k_{1}}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{2}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})

\displaystyle\times I_{k_{2}+2l-k_{1}-3}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})J_{l}([2k_{1}+3]Qz)

\displaystyle\left.+...\right\}

where in the last form we identified the first two terms as those contributing to the echoes at times
2\tau
and

.

Comment

•

I would like to use a summation formula for the Bessel functions to remove the sum over
k_{1}
if possible. I know of these
Neumann summation formulas

\sum_{k}(-1)^{k}I_{p+k}(z_{1})I_{k}(z_{2})=I_{p}(z_{1}-z_{2}),\;\;\;\sum_{k}I_{p-k}(z_{1})I_{k}(z_{2})=I_{p}(z_{1}+z_{2})

We can attempt a small amplitude approximation for the
I_{n}(z)
as

I_{n}(z)\simeq_{\lim z\rightarrow 0)}\frac{(z/2)^{n}}{\Gamma(n+1)},\;\;\;n\neq-1,-2,...

This could be applied to the argument
b_{1}J/J_{0}
for small actions
J/J_{0}\ll 1
. This single power expansion is questionable
for the other arguments
b_{2}\sqrt{J/J_{0}},b_{3}\sqrt{J/J_{0}}
.

If we consider the dominant terms contributing to the 1st and 2nd echoes at 2
\tau
and 4
\tau
respectively,

\displaystyle\langle x(2\tau)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})z]\bar{T}_{1,\phi}(z)

(11.12)

\displaystyle T_{1,\phi}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\{e^{i[\omega(t-2\tau)-Q_{2}z]}\sum_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}}\sum_{l}i^{k_{1}+l}e^{i\frac{1}{2}q(3l-k_{1}-1)}I_{2k_{2}+l}(b_{1}z)I_{k_{1}}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{2}}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})\right.

(11.14)

\displaystyle\left.\times I_{k_{2}+2l-k_{1}-1}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})J_{l}([2k_{1}+1]Qz)\right\}

\displaystyle\langle x(4\tau)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})z]\bar{T}_{2,\phi}(z)

(11.15)

\displaystyle=

(11.17)

\displaystyle\left.\times I_{k_{2}+2l-k_{1}-3}(b_{3}\sqrt{z})J_{l}([2k_{1}+3]Qz)\right\}

We can write

\displaystyle\omega(t-2m\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\beta}(t-2m\tau)+\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}(t-2m\tau)z\equiv\Phi_{m}+\xi_{m}z

\displaystyle\Phi_{m}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\beta}(t-2m\tau),\;\;\;\xi_{m}=\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}(t-2m\tau)

Thus the echo at
2\tau
can be written as

\displaystyle\langle x(2\tau)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-(1+\frac{1}{2}q^{2})z]\bar{T}_{1,\phi}(z)

\displaystyle T_{1,\phi}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\{e^{i[\Phi_{1}-Q_{2}z]}\sum_{k_{1}}\sum_{k_{2}}\sum_{l}i^{k_{1}+l}e^{i\frac{1}{2}q(3l-k_{1}-1)}I_{2k_{2}+l}(B(q)z)I_{k_{1}}(\sqrt{2}C_{1}a_{\theta}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{2}}(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}B(q)a_{\theta}\sqrt{z})\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times I_{k_{2}+2l-k_{1}-1}(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}B(q)a_{\theta}\sqrt{z})J_{l}([2k_{1}+1]Qz)\right\}

Limiting cases
We use the properties

I_{0}(0)=1=J_{0}(0),I_{n,n\neq 0}(0)=0=J_{n,n\neq 0}(0)

•

No dipole kick
a_{\theta}=0

This implies
k_{1}=0=k_{2}=k_{2}+2l-k_{1}-1
. The last has no solution for integer
l
, hence in this limit the Bessel functions
depending on
a_{\theta}
all vanish and hence so does the echo as expected.

•

No quadrupole kick

Consider the limiting case of no quadrupole kick, then
b_{1}=0=b_{3}
. The only terms
contributing to the dipole moment at
t=2\tau
are those with
2k_{2}+l=0=k_{2}=k_{2}+2l-k_{1}+1=l
, we have
as the only solution
k_{2}=0=l
and
k_{1}=0
.

•

1st order in the dipole kick, all orders in the quad kick

We can use the ascending series expansion for the Bessel functions
[
17
]

\displaystyle I_{n}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{z}{2})^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!(n+k)!}(\frac{z}{2})^{2k},\;\;\;I_{-n}(z)=I_{n}(z)

(11.19)

\displaystyle J_{n}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\frac{z}{2})^{n}\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{k}\frac{1}{k!(n+k)!}(\frac{z}{2})^{2k},\;\;\;J_{-n}(z)=(-1)^{n}J_{n}(z)

(11.20)

where
n
is an integer.
This shows for example that
I_{\pm 1}(z)
are the only Bessel functions to have a term linear in
z
. Since we have the product of
three Bessel functions each of whose arguments is
\propto a_{\theta}
,

I_{k_{1}}(\sqrt{2}C_{1}a_{\theta}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{2}}(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}B(q)a_{\theta}\sqrt{z})I_{k_{2}+2l-k_{1}-1}(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}B(q)a_{\theta}\sqrt{z})

we can have the following
combinations of terms that can result in a term linear in
a_{\theta}
.

\displaystyle k_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pm 1,\;\;\;k_{2}=0,\;\;\;k_{2}+2l-k_{1}-1=0;\;\;\;\Rightarrow 2l=k_{1}+1=0,2

\displaystyle k_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,\;\;\;k_{2}=\pm 1,\;\;\;k_{2}+2l-k_{1}-1=0;\;\;\;\Rightarrow 2l=-k_{2}+1=0,2

\displaystyle k_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,\;\;\;k_{2}=0,\;\;\;k_{2}+2l-k_{1}-1=\pm 1;\;\;\;\Rightarrow 2l=0,2

Hence we have these possibilities:
(k_{1},k_{2},l)=(\pm 1,0,0),(\pm 1,0,1),(0,\pm 1,0),(0,\pm 1,1),(0,0,0),(0,0,1)

Hence the following terms contribute to terms of
O(a_{\theta})
using
\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}I_{0}(z)=1
and
\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}I_{1}(z)=z/2

\displaystyle T_{1,\phi}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\{e^{i[\Phi_{1}-Q_{2}z]}\left(\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2}C_{1}a_{\theta}\sqrt{z}\sum_{k_{1}=\pm 1}i^{k_{1}}\sum_{l=0,1}e^{i\frac{1}{2}q(3l-k_{1}-1)}I_{l}(B(q)z)J_{l}([2k_{1}+1]Qz)\right.\right.

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}B(q)a_{\theta}\sqrt{z}\sum_{k_{2}=\pm 1}\sum_{l=0,1}i^{l}e^{i\frac{1}{2}q(3l-1)}I_{2k_{2}+l}(B(q)z)J_{l}(Qz)

\displaystyle\left.\left.+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}B(q)a_{\theta}\sqrt{z}\sum_{l=0,1}i^{l}e^{i\frac{1}{2}q(3l-1)}I_{l}(B(q)z)J_{l}(Qz)\right)\right\}

11.1
A more approximate but simpler calculation

While doing the nonlinear quad kick only calculation, a good approximation to the final result was initially obtained by
not changing the action
J
in the distribution function but only the phase. Here I’ll do the same to obtain a simpler
result and check if that also gives the multiple echoes.

Bessel function property

I_{-n}(z)=I_{n}(z),\;\;\;\;J_{n}(-z)=(-1)^{n}J_{n}(z),\;\;\;J_{-n}(z)=(-1)^{n}J_{n}(z)

In this approximation, we have

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(J,\phi,t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{0}(J+\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{2J/\beta}\sin(\phi-D(z)+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})+\frac{1}{2}\beta_{k}\theta^{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]\exp[-\frac{1}{J_{0}}[J+\beta_{k}\theta\sqrt{2J/\beta}\sin(\phi-D(z)+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})]]

In this case, the simplified expressions can be obtained from the previous section by replacing

b_{1}=0=b_{3},\;\;\;b_{2}=\sqrt{2}a_{\theta},\;\;\;C_{1}=1,\;\;\;C_{3}=0

(11.22)

We have for the dipole moment

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2\beta J_{0}}}{2\pi}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-z]T_{\phi}(z)

(11.23)

\displaystyle T_{\phi}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi\cos\phi\exp\left[-b_{2}\sqrt{z}\sin(\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+}-q+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})\right]

(11.24)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\{\int d\phi e^{i\phi}\exp\left[-b_{2}\sqrt{z}\sin(\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+}-q+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi})\right]\right\}

Using the expansion:
e^{-z\sin\theta}=\sum_{n}i^{n}I_{n}(z)e^{in\theta}

\displaystyle T_{\phi}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{k}i^{k}I_{k}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})\int d\phi e^{i\phi}\exp[i(k(\phi-\Delta\phi-\tau\omega_{+}-q+Qz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}))]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{k}i^{k}I_{k}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})\exp[i(-k(\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+}+q)]\right.

\displaystyle\left.\int d\phi\exp\left[i\left((1+k)\phi+kQz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}\right)\right]\right\}

To do the
\phi
integration, I have to expand into a Bessel function

\exp[ikQz\sin 2\phi_{-\Delta\phi}]=\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}J_{l}(kQz)\exp[2il(\phi-\Delta\phi)]

Then

\displaystyle T_{\phi}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{k}\sum_{l}i^{k}I_{k}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})J_{l}(kQz)\exp[i(-k(\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+}+q)-2l\Delta\phi)]\int d\phi\exp\left[i([1+k+2l]\phi)\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi{\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{l}i^{-(2l+1)}I_{-(2l+1)}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})J_{l}(-(2l+1)Qz)\exp[i((2l+1)(\Delta\phi+\tau\omega_{+}+q)-2l\Delta\phi)]\right\}

where we replaced
k\rightarrow-(2l+1)

The phase factor simplifies to

\displaystyle(2l+1)[\Delta\phi+\tau(\omega+Q_{2}z+q)-2l\Delta\phi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(t-\tau)+(2l+1)\tau(\omega+Q_{2}z+q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega t+2l\omega\tau+(2l+1)(Q_{2}z+q)

Since the sum extends over positive and negative values of
l
, I can replace
l=-n
and write

T_{\phi}(z)={\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}i^{2n-1)}I_{2n-1)}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})J_{-n}(2n-1)Qz)\exp[i\left\{\omega(t-2n\tau)-(2n-1)(Q_{2}z+q)\right)]\right\}

This form predicts echoes at multiples of 2
\tau
.
In this approximation, the amplitude of the echo at
2tau
corresponds to the term with
n=1
. Writing

\omega(t-2n\tau)=\omega_{\beta}(t-2n\tau)+\omega^{\prime}\epsilon_{0}(t-2n\tau)z\equiv\Phi_{n}+\xi_{n}z

and using
J_{-n}(z)=(-1)^{n}J_{n}(z)
, we have

T_{\phi}(z)={\rm Re}\left\{\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}i^{2n-1)}(-1)^{n}I_{2n-1)}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})J_{n}(2n-1)Qz)\exp[i(\Phi_{n}-(2n-1)q)]\exp[iz(\xi_{n}-(2n-1)Q_{2})]\right\}

(11.25)

Hence

\displaystyle\langle x(t=2\tau)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sqrt{2\beta J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]{\rm Re}\left\{ie^{i(\Phi_{1}-q)}\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-z\{1-i(\xi_{1}-Q_{2})\}]I_{1}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})J_{1}(Qz)\right\}

(11.26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]{\rm Im}\left\{e^{i(\Phi_{1}-q)}\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-z\{1-i(\xi_{1}-Q_{2})\}]I_{1}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})J_{1}(Qz)\right\}

where in the last form we used the fact that
-{\rm Re}[if(z)]={\rm Im}[f(z)]
for a complex function
f(z)
. In this form
it has nearly the same form obtained as obtained with the linearized dipole kick approximation, except that

\beta_{k}\theta
is replaced by
I_{1}(\sqrt{2}a_{\theta}\sqrt{z})
and there is a factor of
\exp[-\beta_{k}\theta^{2}/(2J_{0})]
.
The presence of
I_{1}(\sqrt{2}a_{\theta}\sqrt{z})
shows that without a dipole kick

a_{\theta}=0
, the dipole moment also vanishes, as it does in the linearized dipole approximation.
For ease of comparison, the form obtained in the linear dipole approximation for
I_{1}
in the linearized approximation was

\langle x(t=2\tau)\rangle=\beta_{k}\theta{\rm Im}\left\{e^{i[\Phi_{1}-q]}\int dz\;\exp[-(1-i(\xi_{1}-Q_{2})z)]J_{1}(Qz)\right\}

(11.27)

If in Eq.(
11.26
) we replace
I_{1}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})
by its 1st order approximation

\frac{1}{2}b_{2}\sqrt{z}=(\sqrt{2}/2)(\beta_{k}\theta/\sqrt{\beta J_{0}})z
and
\exp[-\beta_{k}\theta^{2}/(2J_{0})]
by 1, then it
reduces to exactly the same equation above.

The amplitude of the echo at 4
\tau
corresponds to the term with
n=2
. Hence

\displaystyle\langle x(t=4\tau)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]{\rm Re}\left\{i^{3}e^{i(\Phi_{2}-3q)}\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-z\{1-i(\xi_{2}-3Q_{2})\}]I_{3}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})J_{2}(Qz)\right\}

(11.28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta J_{0}}\exp[-\frac{\beta_{k}\theta^{2}}{2J_{0}}]{\rm Im}\left\{e^{i(\Phi_{2}-3q)}\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-z\{1-i(\xi_{2}-3Q_{2})\}]I_{3}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})J_{2}(Qz)\right\}

Note that since the lowest order term in
I_{3}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})
is
(b_{2}\sqrt{z})^{3}=(2z)^{3/2}a_{\theta}^{3}
, there is no
echo at 4
\tau
in the linearized dipole kick approximation. Note also the phase factor
\exp[i(\Phi_{2}-3q)]
shows that
the
4\tau
echo will be shifted slightly from the time
t=4\tau
.

Neither of these integrations can be done analytically by Mathematica. These integrals also do not seem to appear in the
tables of integrals in
[
18
]
. However they can be evaluated numerically.
Putting the phase factors to zero, the ratio of the amplitudes can be found from

\frac{\langle x(t=4\tau)\rangle^{amp}}{\langle x(t=2\tau)\rangle^{amp}}=\frac{\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-z]I_{3}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})J_{2}(Qz)}{\int dz\;\sqrt{z}\exp[-z]I_{1}(b_{2}\sqrt{z})J_{1}(Qz)}

(11.29)

12
Multiple Echoes

Multiple echoes at 4
\tau
, 6
\tau
with calculated echo amplitudes

•

What additional information is present in these multiple echoes?

•

How do these multiple echoes help confirm / validate information from the echo at
2\tau
?

•

What can we say if no multiple echoes are observed, besides the obvious fact that diffusion may be too strong? In the RHIC studies, why were multiple echoes not observed in most cases? For example, consider the dipole kick amplitudes: does this kick
have to be larger (or smaller) than some critical value for multiple echoes to be seen?

13
Vlasov equation solution for the echo response

Consider the case where the external force is an impulse, i.e. dipole shock excitation at
t=0
.
For simplicity, consider the 1D transverse case with a betatron tune spread. Thus,
consider the betatron frequency to be
\omega_{x}(J_{x})
.
The transformation from the variables
(x,x^{\prime})
to
(J_{x}.\phi_{x})
are

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\cos\phi_{x},\;\;\;p=\alpha_{x}x+\beta_{x}x^{\prime}=-\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\sin\phi_{x}

(13.1)

\displaystyle J_{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\beta_{x}}[x^{2}+p^{2}]\;\;\;\phi_{x}={\rm Arctan}[\frac{-p}{x}]

(13.2)

13.1
Linearized Vlasov solution with a dipole kick and quadrupole kick.

The Hamiltonian and equations of motion following a dipole kick are

\displaystyle H(J_{x},\phi_{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{0}(J_{x})-\epsilon xf_{x}(t)=H_{0}(J_{x})-\epsilon\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\cos\phi_{x}f_{x}(t)

(13.3)

\displaystyle\dot{\phi_{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial H}{\partial J_{x}}=\omega_{x}(J_{x})-\epsilon\sqrt{\beta_{x}}\frac{\cos\phi_{x}}{\sqrt{2J_{x}}}f_{x}(t)

(13.4)

\displaystyle\dot{J_{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial H}{\partial\phi_{x}}=-\epsilon\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\sin\phi_{x}f_{x}(t)

(13.5)

where
\epsilon
is a suitably small parameter.

The Vlasov equation is

\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial t}+\dot{\phi_{x}}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial\phi_{x}}+\dot{J_{x}}\frac{\partial\psi}{\partial J_{x}}=0

(13.6)

Expanding

\psi(J_{x},\phi_{x})=\psi_{0}(J_{x})+\epsilon\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x})

Hence the Vlasov equation reduces to

\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial t}+(\omega_{x}(J_{x})-\epsilon\sqrt{\beta_{x}}\frac{\cos\phi_{x}}{\sqrt{2J_{x}}}f_{x}(t))\epsilon\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial\phi_{x}}-\epsilon\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\sin\phi_{x}f_{x}(t)[\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}+\epsilon\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial J_{x}}]=0

Keeping terms to
O(\epsilon)
, we have

\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial t}+\omega_{x}(J_{x})\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial\phi_{x}}-\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\sin\phi_{x}f_{x}(t)\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}=0

(13.7)

The perturbation is of the form

f_{x}(t)=B\delta(t)

Unlike a harmonic perturbation, we cannot make the ansatz of the density responding at the single
frequency of the driving force.

13.1.1
Solution by Fourier transform

One possibility is to take the Fourier transform w.r.t time and write

\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},t)=e^{i\phi_{x}}\int d\omega e^{-i\omega t}\tilde{\psi_{1}}(J_{x},\phi_{x},\omega),\;\;\;\delta(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int d\omega e^{-i\omega t}

(13.8)

Question: Are there subtleties associated with taking the Fourier transform here? Why is the Laplace
transform used in some cases?

Then we have from the Vlasov equation

ie^{i\phi_{x}}\int d\omega e^{i\omega t}\tilde{\psi_{1}}[-\omega+\omega_{x}(J_{x})]=B\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\frac{e^{i\phi_{x}}-e^{-i\phi_{x}}}{2i}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\exp[-i\omega t]\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.9)

Equating the integrands

[\omega-\omega_{x}(J_{x})]\tilde{\psi_{1}}=\frac{1}{4\pi}B(1-e^{-2i\phi_{x}})\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

Averaging this equation over the phase
\phi_{x}
removes the
\phi_{x}
dependent term on the RHS
leaving

\tilde{\psi_{1}}=\frac{B}{4\pi}\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_{x}(J_{x})}\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.10)

and the complete solution for the time dependent perturbed density is

\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},t)=\frac{B}{4\pi}e^{i\phi_{x}}\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}\int d\omega e^{-i\omega t}\frac{1}{\omega-\omega_{x}(J_{x})}

(13.11)

From Mathematica,

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\omega\frac{e^{-i\omega t}}{\omega-\omega_{x}}=-i\pi e^{-i\omega_{x}t}{\rm Sign}(t)

(13.12)

In our case
t>0
and we have

\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},t)=-i\frac{1}{4}Be^{i(\phi_{x}-\omega_{x}(J_{x})t)}\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.13)

Comment: The Sign(
t
) function could be a source of a problem suggesting a lack of causality.
Prior to the kick at
t=0
, there is no perturbation and so we should have
\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},t<0)=0
.
The solution obtained here does not obey that. This could be corrected by multiplying the above
expression by the Heavyside theta function
\Theta(t)
to take this into account. Thus,

\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},t)=-i\frac{B}{4}e^{i(\phi_{x}-\omega_{x}(J_{x})t)}\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}\Theta(t)

(13.14)

The inverse Laplace transform of an arbitrary function
f(s)
is

{\cal L}^{-1}[f(s)]=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{\gamma-i\infty}^{\gamma+i\infty}f(s)e^{st}ds

where
\gamma
is an arbitrary positive constant so that all singularities of
f(s)
lie to the right of the
contour. If
f(s)=1
, we can choose
\gamma=0
and we have the
the inverse Laplace transform representation of the delta function as

\delta(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}e^{st}ds

This becomes a Fourier transform on replacing
si\omega
.

The dipole moment is after averaging over
(J_{x},\phi_{x})

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dJ_{x}\int\frac{d\phi_{x}}{2\pi}(\psi_{0}+\epsilon\psi_{1})\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\cos\phi_{x}

(13.15)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\frac{}{4}(\epsilon\beta_{x}B)\int dJ_{x}e^{-i\omega_{x}(J_{x})t}2J_{x}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}\int\frac{d\phi_{x}}{2\pi}e^{i\phi_{x}}\cos\phi_{x}

(13.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\frac{1}{4}(\epsilon\beta_{x}B)\int dJ_{x}e^{-i\omega_{x}(J_{x})t}J_{x}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.17)

13.1.2
Solution by Laplace transform

Usually problems with delta function impulses are solved by the Laplace transform

First write

\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},t)=e^{i\phi_{x}}g(J_{x},t)

(13.18)

Substituting into the Vlasov equation, we have

e^{i\phi_{x}}[\frac{\partial g}{\partial t}+i\omega_{x}(J_{x})g(J_{x},t)]=\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\frac{e^{i\phi_{x}}-e^{-i\phi_{x}}}{2i}B\delta(t)\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.19)

Averaging over
\phi_{x}
, we have

i\frac{\partial g}{\partial t}-\omega_{x}(J_{x})g(J_{x},t)]=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}B\delta(t)\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.20)

The Laplace transform of a function
f(t)
is defined as

F(s)\equiv{\cal L}(f)=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t)e^{-st}dt,\;\;\;{\cal L}(\delta(t))=1

while the Laplace transforms of derivatives are

{\cal L}(\dot{f})=s{\cal L}(f)-f(0),\;\;\;{\cal L}(\ddot{f})=s^{2}{\cal L}(f)-sf(0)-\dot{f}(0)

These can be shown by integrating by parts, e.g.

\displaystyle{\cal L}(\dot{f})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\dot{f(t)}e^{-st}dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{d}{dt}(f(t)e^{-st})-\int_{0}^{\infty}f(t)[-se^{-st}]dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle s{\cal L}(f)-f(0)

Define the Laplace transform of
g(J_{x},t)
w.r.t time as

G(J_{x},s)=\int g(J_{x},t)e^{-st}dt

Now we take the Laplace transform of Eq. (
13.20
) and we use
g(J_{x},t=0)=0
to obtain

\displaystyle G(J_{x},s)(is-\omega_{x}(J_{x}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}B\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.21)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow g(J_{x},t)=-i{\cal L}^{-1}[\frac{1}{s+i\omega_{x}(J_{x})}]\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}B\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.22)

Using the inverse Laplace transform

{\cal L}^{-1}[\frac{1}{s+a}]=e^{-at}\Theta(t)

where
\Theta(t)
is the Heavyside step function.

Hence, the perturbed density distribution is

\psi_{1}(J_{x},t)=-i\frac{B}{2}\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}e^{i[\phi_{x}-\omega_{x}(J_{x})t]}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}\Theta(t)

(13.24)

which is the same as the solution from the Fourier transform, except for the factor of 2 instead of 4 in the
denominator.

Consequently, the dipole moment is

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dJ_{x}\int\frac{d\phi_{x}}{2\pi}(\psi_{0}+\epsilon\psi_{1})\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\cos\phi_{x}

(13.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-i\frac{(\epsilon\beta_{x}B)}{2}\int dJ_{x}e^{-i\omega_{x}(J_{x})t}J_{x}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}\Theta(t)

(13.26)

Taking the real part, we have

\langle x\rangle(t)=\frac{(\epsilon\beta_{x}B)}{2}\int dJ_{x}\sin[\omega_{x}(J_{x})t]J_{x}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}\Theta(t)

(13.27)

However with an exponential distribution in the action
\psi_{0}(J_{x})=\frac{1}{2\pi\epsilon_{x}}\exp[-\frac{J_{x}}{\epsilon_{x}}]

it is better to keep the complex form for now.

Compare this solution with that in Chao’s lecture notes with the specific choices of
\omega_{x})J_{x}),\psi_{0}
.

13.2
Linearized Vlasov solution following dipole kick and quadrupole kicks

With a dipole kick at
t=0
and a quadrupole kick at
t=\tau
, the Hamiltonian and the equations of motion
are of the form

\displaystyle H(J_{x},\phi_{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{0}(J_{x})-\epsilon Bx\delta(t)-\epsilon K_{Q}x^{2}\delta(t-\tau)

(13.28)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H_{0}(J_{x})-\epsilon B\delta(t)\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\cos\phi_{x}-\epsilon K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)(2\beta_{x}J_{x})\cos^{2}\phi_{x}

\displaystyle\dot{\phi_{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\partial H}{\partial J_{x}}=\omega_{x}(J_{x})-\epsilon B\delta(t)\sqrt{\beta_{x}}\frac{\cos\phi_{x}}{\sqrt{2J_{x}}}-\epsilon 2K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)\cos^{2}\phi_{x}

(13.29)

\displaystyle\dot{J_{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\partial H}{\partial\phi_{x}}=-\epsilon B\delta(t)\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\sin\phi_{x}-\epsilon K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)(2\beta_{x}J_{x})\sin 2\phi_{x}

(13.30)

Here we assumed the same order of smallness
\epsilon
for the dipole and quadrupole kicks.
The parameter
K_{Q}
is related to the quad strength parameter
q
.

The Vlasov equation after keeping terms to
O(\epsilon)
is

\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial t}+\omega_{x}(J_{x})\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial\phi_{x}}-[B\delta(t)\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\sin\phi_{x}+K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)(2\beta_{x}J_{x})\sin 2\phi_{x}]\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}=0

(13.31)

The dipole kick introduces changes to the phase
\phi_{x}
while the quadrupole kick introduces changes
to twice the phase
\phi_{x}
.

Since the distribution function is periodic in the phase
\phi_{x}
, it can be expanded as a Fourier series in
\phi_{x}
.
One possibility is to truncate the expansion at two terms and make an ansatz of the form

\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},t)=e^{i\phi_{x}}g_{1}(J_{x},t)+e^{2i\phi_{x}}g_{2}(J_{x},t)

However this does not generate a real distribution, so the complete expansion is of the form

\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},t)=e^{i\phi_{x}}g_{1}(J_{x},t)+e^{-i\phi_{x}}g_{-1}(J_{x},t)+e^{2i\phi_{x}}g_{2}(J_{x},t)+e^{-2i\phi_{x}}g_{-2}(J_{x},t)

(13.32)

with the constraints
g_{-1}=g_{1}^{*}
,
g_{-2}=g_{2}^{*}
so that
\psi_{1}
is real.

Constraint on the perturbation is

\int dJ_{x}d\phi_{x}\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},t)=0

This does not introduce constraints into the functions
g_{1},g_{2}
since the integrals over the phase vanish for
both terms.

Substituting back into the Vlasov equation,

\displaystyle e^{i\phi_{x}}\frac{\partial g_{1}}{\partial t}+e^{2i\phi_{x}}\frac{\partial g_{2}}{\partial t}+e^{-i\phi_{x}}\frac{\partial g_{1}^{*}}{\partial t}+e^{-2i\phi_{x}}\frac{\partial g_{2}^{*}}{\partial t}+i\omega_{x}(J_{x})[e^{i\phi_{x}}g_{1}-e^{-i\phi_{x}}g_{1}^{*}+2e^{2i\phi_{x}}g_{2}-2e^{-2i\phi_{x}}g_{2}^{*}]

(13.33)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[B\delta(t)\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\frac{e^{i\phi_{x}}-e^{-i\phi_{x}}}{2i}+K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)(2\beta_{x}J_{x})\frac{e^{2i\phi_{x}}-e^{-2i\phi_{x}}}{2i}]\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

We can equate coefficients of
e^{ik\phi_{x}},k=\pm 1,\pm 2
on both sides of this equation or alternatively do
the following.

Multiplying both sides by
e^{-i\phi_{x}}
and averaging over
\phi_{x}
leads to

\frac{\partial g_{1}}{\partial t}+i\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{1}=-\frac{i}{2}B\delta(t)\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.34)

which is the same as the equation for
g
in the last sub-section, leading to the same solution

g_{1}(J_{x},t)=-\frac{i}{2}B\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}e^{-i\omega_{x}(J_{x})t}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.35)

Now multiplying both sides of Eq.(
13.33
) by
e^{-2i\phi_{x}}
and averaging over
\phi_{x}
leads to

\frac{\partial g_{2}}{\partial t}+2i\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{2}=-\frac{i}{2}K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)(2\beta_{x}J_{x})\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.36)

Let
G_{2}(s)
be the Laplace transform of
g_{2}
and we use
{\cal L}[\delta(t-\tau)]=e^{-s\tau}
to obtain

G_{2}(J_{x},s)=-\frac{i}{2}K_{Q}\frac{e^{-s\tau}}{s+2i\omega_{x}(J_{x})}(2\beta_{x}J_{x})\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.37)

Using

{\cal L}^{-1}[\frac{e^{-s\tau}}{s+2i\omega_{x}(J_{x})}]=\exp[-2i\omega_{x}(J_{x})(t-\tau)]\Theta(t-\tau)

we have

g_{2}(J_{x},t)=-\frac{i}{2}K_{Q}e^{[-2i\omega_{x}(J_{x})(t-\tau)]}(2\beta_{x}J_{x})\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}\Theta(t-\tau)

(13.38)

With the ansatz we made for the perturbed density, we have

\displaystyle\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}B\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}[-ie^{i[\phi_{x}-\omega_{x}(J_{x})t]}+ie^{-i[\phi_{x}-\omega_{x}(J_{x})t]}]\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}\Theta(t)

(13.39)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}K_{Q}(2\beta_{x}J_{x})[-ie^{2i[\phi_{x}-\omega_{x}(J_{x})(t-\tau)]}+ie^{-2i[\phi_{x}-\omega_{x}(J_{x})(t-\tau)]}]\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}\Theta(t-\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\sin[\phi_{x}-\omega_{x}(J_{x})t]+K_{Q}(2\beta_{x}J_{x})\sin 2[\phi_{x}-\omega_{x}(J_{x})(t-\tau)]

Problem with this solution: The second term from the quadrupole kick is even in
\phi_{x}
and will give
a zero dipole moment. From the Eulerian method of solution for the density distribution, we know that the
