term giving a non-zero contribution to the dipole moment was linear in both the dipole and quadrupole
parameters. In other words, the non-zero contribution came from a term of
O(\epsilon^{2})
.

So, I have to solve the Vlasov equation to second order. This should not be a surprise, the theory of
the plasma wave echo had shown the echo to be a nonlinear process.

13.3
Nonlinear Vlasov solution of the echo response

Starting with the perturbative solution but setting the small parameter
\epsilon=1
[we are not doing a
perturbative order by order solution]

\psi(J_{x},\phi_{x},t)=\psi_{0}(J_{x})+\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},t)

and substituting into the complete Vlasov equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial t}+\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial\phi_{x}}[\omega_{x}(J_{x})-B\delta(t)\sqrt{\beta_{x}}\frac{\cos\phi_{x}}{\sqrt{2J_{x}}}-2K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)\cos^{2}\phi_{x}]

\displaystyle+(\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}+\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial J_{x}})[-B\delta(t)\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\sin\phi_{x}-K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)(2\beta_{x}J_{x})\sin 2\phi_{x}]=0

Introduce the notation

d_{x}=\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{x}}{2J_{x}}},\;\;\;A_{x}=\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}

(13.40)

We can rewrite the Vlasov equation as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial t}+[\omega_{x}(J_{x})-B\delta(t)d_{x}\cos\phi_{x}-2K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)\cos^{2}\phi_{x}]\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial\phi_{x}}

\displaystyle-[B\delta(t)A_{x}\sin\phi_{x}+K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)A_{x}^{2}\sin 2\phi_{x}]\frac{\partial\psi_{1}}{\partial J_{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[B\delta(t)A_{x}\sin\phi_{x}+K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)A_{x}^{2}\sin 2\phi_{x}]\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

Since the distribution function is periodic in
\phi_{x}
, we can expand it in a Fourier series in
\phi_{x}
as

\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},t)=\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}g_{k}(J_{x},t)e^{ik\phi_{x}}

(13.41)

Since we want a real distribution function it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{k}g_{k}(J_{x},t)e^{ik\phi_{x}}=\sum_{k}g_{k}^{*}(J_{x},t)e^{-ik\phi_{x}}=\sum_{k}g_{-k}^{*}(J_{x},t)e^{ik\phi_{x}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle g_{k}=g_{-k}^{*},\;\;\;{\rm or}\;\;\;g_{-k}=g_{k}^{*}

I expect that the only the components
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}
will be necessary. It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\frac{\partial g_{k}}{\partial t}e^{ik\phi_{x}}+\left[\omega_{x}(J_{x})-\frac{1}{2}B\delta(t)d_{x}(e^{i\phi_{x}}+e^{-i\phi_{x}})-K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[1+\frac{1}{2}(e^{2i\phi_{x}}+e^{-2i\phi_{x}})]\right]\sum_{k}ikg_{k}(J_{x},t)e^{ik\phi_{x}}

\displaystyle-\left[\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}[e^{i\phi_{x}}-e^{-i\phi_{x}}]+\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}(e^{2i\phi_{x}}-e^{-2i\phi_{x}})\right]\sum_{k}\frac{\partial g_{k}}{\partial J_{x}}e^{ik\phi_{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}[e^{i\phi_{x}}-e^{-i\phi_{x}}]+\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}(e^{2i\phi_{x}}-e^{-2i\phi_{x}})]\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

Writing out the LHS to isolate the
k
th harmonic

\displaystyle\sum_{k}\left\{\frac{\partial g_{k}}{\partial t}e^{ik\phi_{x}}+ikg_{k}\left[\omega_{x}(J_{x})e^{ik\phi_{x}}-\frac{1}{2}B\delta(t)d_{x}[e^{i(k+1)\phi_{x}}+e^{i(k-1)\phi_{x}}]\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.-K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)(e^{ik\phi_{x}}+\frac{1}{2}(e^{i(k+2)\phi_{x}}+e^{i(k-2)\phi_{x}})]\right]\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{\partial g_{k}}{\partial J_{x}}[\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}[e^{i(k+1)\phi_{x}}-e^{i(k-1)\phi_{x}}]+\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}(e^{i(k+2)\phi_{x}}-e^{i(k-2)\phi_{x}})]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k}e^{ik\phi_{x}}\left\{\frac{\partial g_{k}}{\partial t}+ik\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{k}-\frac{i}{2}B\delta(t)d_{x}[(k-1)g_{k-1}+(k+1)g_{k+1}]\right.

\displaystyle-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[kg_{k}+\frac{1}{2}((k-2)g_{k-2}+(k+2)g_{k+2})]

\displaystyle\left.-\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}[\frac{\partial g_{k-1}}{\partial J_{x}}-\frac{\partial g_{k+1}}{\partial J_{x}}]-\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}[\frac{\partial g_{k-2}}{\partial J_{x}}-\frac{\partial g_{k+2}}{\partial J_{x}}]\right\}

Hence the Vlasov equation in terms of the harmonics is

\displaystyle\sum_{k}e^{ik\phi_{x}}\left\{\frac{\partial g_{k}}{\partial t}+ik\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{k}-\frac{i}{2}B\delta(t)d_{x}[(k-1)g_{k-1}+(k+1)g_{k+1}]\right.

(13.42)

\displaystyle-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[kg_{k}+\frac{1}{2}((k-2)g_{k-2}+(k+2)g_{k+2})]

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{k+1}-g_{k-1}]+\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{k+2}-g_{k-2}]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}[e^{i\phi_{x}}-e^{-i\phi_{x}}]+\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}(e^{2i\phi_{x}}-e^{-2i\phi_{x}})]\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

This equation shows that the time evolution of the
k
th mode is coupled to the neighboring 4 modes:

g_{k-2},g_{k-1},g_{k+1},g_{k+2}
.

Equating the
k=0,\pm 1,\pm 2
and the
|k|>2
harmonics on the two sides, we have
first for the
k=0
harmonic

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g_{0}}{\partial t}-\frac{i}{2}B\delta(t)d_{x}[-g_{-1}+g_{1}]-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[\frac{1}{2}(-2g_{-2}+2g_{2})]

\displaystyle+\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{1}-g_{-1}]+\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{2}-g_{-2}]=0

Using and introducing notation for the real and imaginary parts

g_{-k}=g_{k}^{*},\;\;\;g_{k}+g_{k}^{*}=2{\rm Re}[g_{k}]\equiv g_{k,R},\;\;\;g_{k}-g_{k}^{*}=2i{\rm Im}[g_{k}]\equiv g_{k,I}

the above implies

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g_{0}}{\partial t}+B\delta(t)d_{x}{\rm Im}[g_{1}]+2K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau){\rm Im}[g_{2}]

\displaystyle+B\delta(t)A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}{\rm Im}[g_{1}]+K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}{\rm Im}[g_{2}]=0

WLOG we can put
g_{0}=0
since the zeroth harmonic time independent function is already in
\psi_{0}
,
Then the above equation can be written as one linking the imaginary parts

B\delta(t)[A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,I}+d_{x}g_{1,I}]+K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)[A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{2,I}+2\beta_{x}g_{2,I}]=0

(13.43)

Integrating this equation over time leads to

B[A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,I}(J_{x},0)+d_{x}g_{1,I}(J_{x},0)]+K_{Q}[A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{2,I}(J_{x},\tau)+2\beta_{x}g_{2,I}(J_{x},\tau)]=0

Using the initial condition
g_{k}(J_{x},0)=0
for all
k
, this reduces to

J_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{2,I}(J_{x},\tau)+g_{2,I}(J_{x},\tau)=0

(13.44)

This has the solution

g_{2,I}(J_{x},\tau)=c_{2,I,\tau}\frac{1}{J_{x}}

(13.45)

where
c_{2,I,\tau}
is a real constant.

Equation for the
k=1
harmonic and using
g_{0}=0,g_{-k}=g_{k}^{*}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g_{1}}{\partial t}+i\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{1}-iB\delta(t)d_{x}g_{2}-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[g_{1}+\frac{1}{2}(-g_{1}^{*}+3g_{3})]

\displaystyle+\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{2}]+\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{3}-g_{1}^{*}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

Equation for the
k=-1
harmonic and using
g_{0}=0,g_{-k}=g_{k}^{*}
:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g_{1}^{*}}{\partial t}-i\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{1}^{*}-iB\delta(t)d_{x}[-g_{2}^{*}]-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[-g_{1}^{*}+\frac{1}{2}(-3g_{3}^{*}+g_{1})]

\displaystyle+\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[-g_{2}^{*}]+\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{1}-g_{3}^{*}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

Adding the equations for
k=1
and
k=-1
, we obtain

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial g_{1,R}}{\partial t}-2\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{1,I}+2B\delta(t)d_{x}g_{2,I}+3K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[g_{1,I}+g_{3,I}]

\displaystyle+B\delta(t)A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{2,I}]+K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{1,I}+g_{3,I}]=0

(13.46)

Note that the time derivative of the real part
g_{1,R}
is determined by the imaginary parts of
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}

and it does not depend on the unperturbed density
\psi_{0}
.

Subtracting the equations for
k=-1
from that for
k=1
, we obtain after multiplying by
i
,

\displaystyle-2\frac{\partial g_{1,I}}{\partial t}-2\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{1,R}+2B\delta(t)d_{x}g_{2,R}+K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[g_{1,R}+3g_{3,R}]

(13.47)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle B\delta(t)A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{2,R}]-K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{1,R}-g_{3,R}]=B\delta(t)A_{x}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

This shows the time derivative of the imaginary part
g_{i,I}
depends on the real parts of
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3}

and also on
\psi_{0}
.

Assumption: We will assume that modes higher than 3 are relatively small so we put

g_{|k|}=0,\;\;\;|k|>3

With this assumption, we have the equation for the
k=2
mode

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g_{2}}{\partial t}+2i\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{2}-\frac{i}{2}B\delta(t)d_{x}[g_{1}+3g_{3}]-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[2g_{2}]

\displaystyle+\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{3}-g_{1}]=\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

and with
k=-2
,

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g_{2}^{*}}{\partial t}-2i\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{2}^{*}-\frac{i}{2}B\delta(t)d_{x}[-3g_{3}^{*}-g_{1}^{*}]-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[-2g_{2}^{*}]

\displaystyle-\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{3}^{*}-g_{1}^{*}]=-\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

Adding we have,

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial g_{2,R}}{\partial t}-4\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{2,I}+B\delta(t)d_{x}[g_{1,I}+3g_{3,I}]+4K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)g_{2,I}+B\delta(t)A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{3,I}-g_{1,I}]=0

and subtracting we have, after multiplying by
i

\displaystyle-2\frac{\partial g_{2,I}}{\partial t}-4\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{2,R}+B\delta(t)d_{x}[g_{1,R}+3g_{3,R}]+4K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)g_{2,R}+B\delta(t)A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{3,R}-g_{1,R}]

(13.49)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

Next, setting
k=3
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g_{3}}{\partial t}+i3\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{3}-\frac{i}{2}B\delta(t)d_{x}[2g_{2}]-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[3g_{3}+\frac{1}{2}g_{1}]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{2}]-\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{1}]=0

and
k=-3
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial g_{3}^{*}}{\partial t}-3i\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{3}^{*}-\frac{i}{2}B\delta(t)d_{x}[-2g_{2}^{*}]-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[-3g_{3}^{*}+\frac{1}{2}(-g_{1}^{*})]

\displaystyle+\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{2}^{*}]+\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{1}^{*}]=0

Adding the equations, we have

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial g_{3,R}}{\partial t}-6\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{3,I}+2B\delta(t)d_{x}g_{2,I}+K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[6g_{3,I}+g_{1,I}]

\displaystyle-B\delta(t)A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{2,I}-K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,I}=0

(13.50)

while subtracting gives

\displaystyle-2\frac{\partial g_{3,I}}{\partial t}-6\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{3,R}+2B\delta(t)d_{x}g_{2,R}+K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[6g_{3,R}+g_{1,R}]

\displaystyle-B\delta(t)A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{2,R}-K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,R}=0

(13.51)

The
k=\pm 4,\pm 5
equations contain
g_{2},g_{3}
so may have useful relations on them.
Caution: Is it consistent or self-consistent to use the equations for the higher modes
k\geq 4
which are ignored in the rest of
the analysis?

With
k=4
, we have after assuming
g_{k}=0,|k|\geq 4
,

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}B\delta(t)d_{x}(3g_{3})-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[g_{2}]

\displaystyle-\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{3}]-\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{2}]=0

while with
k=-4
, we have

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}B\delta(t)d_{x}[(-3)g_{3}^{*}]-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[-g_{2}^{*}]

\displaystyle-\frac{B\delta(t)}{2i}A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[-g_{3}^{*}]-\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[-g_{2}^{*}]=0

Adding the two equations, we have

\displaystyle 3B\delta(t)d_{x}g_{3,I}+2K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)g_{2,I}-B\delta(t)A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{3,I}]-K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{2,I}=0

(13.52)

Subtracting gives us

\displaystyle 3B\delta(t)d_{x}g_{3,R}+2K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)g_{2,R}-B\delta(t)A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{3,R}]-K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{2,R}=0

(13.53)

Setting
k=5
yields

\displaystyle-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[\frac{1}{2}(3g_{3})]-\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{3}]=0

and
k=-5
yields

\displaystyle-iK_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[\frac{1}{2}((-3)g_{3}^{*})]-\frac{K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)}{2i}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[-g_{3}^{*}]=0

The equation for
g_{3},g_{3}^{*}
can be written as (using
A_{x}^{2}=2\beta_{x}J_{x}
)

\displaystyle\delta(t-\tau)\left\{\frac{3}{2}g_{3}-J_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{3}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(13.54)

\displaystyle\delta(t-\tau)\left\{\frac{3}{2}g_{3}^{*}-J_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{3}^{*}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(13.55)

Integrating these over time yields the same equation for
g_{3},g_{3}^{*}

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2}g_{3}(J_{x},\tau)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle J_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{3}(J_{x},\tau)=0

(13.56)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow g_{3}(J_{x},\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{3,\tau}J_{x}^{3/2}

(13.57)

where
C_{3,\tau}
is a constant, possibly complex. Eq. (
13.56
) shows that
g_{3,R},g_{3,I}
obey the same equation.

Integrating Eq.(
13.52
) over time yields

\displaystyle 3Bd_{x}g_{3,I}(J_{x},0)+2K_{Q}\beta_{x}g_{2,I}(J_{x},\tau)-BA_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{3,I}(J_{x},0)]-K_{Q}A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{2,I}(J_{x},\tau)=0

With the initial condition that
\psi_{1}(t=0)=0
, we also have
g_{k}(J_{x},0)=0
. Hence the above equation
reduces to, after using
A_{x}^{2}=2\beta_{x}J_{x}
,

g_{2,I}(J_{x},\tau)-J_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{2,I}(J_{x},\tau)=0

(13.58)

Adding Equations (
13.44
) and (
13.58
), we have

g_{2,I}(J_{x},\tau)=0

(13.59)

Since we now have
g_{2,I}(J_{x},0)=0=g_{2,I}(J_{x},\tau)
, this suggests we can set

g_{2,I}(J_{x},t)=0

(13.60)

Integrating Eq.(
13.53
) over time yields the solution for
g_{2,R}(J_{x},\tau)

g_{2,R}(J_{x},\tau)=c_{2,R,\tau}J_{x}

where
c_{2,R,\tau}
is a real constant. Thus the equations at
k=\pm 4,\pm 5
yield

g_{2}(J_{x},\tau)=c_{2,R}J_{x},\;\;\;g_{3}(J_{x},\tau)=C_{3,\tau}J_{x}^{3/2}

(13.61)

Notation: Lower case constants
c_{k}
are real while upper case constants
C_{k}
are in general complex.

Setting
g_{2,I}=0
, the remaining equations from
k=\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3
are

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial g_{1,R}}{\partial t}-2\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{1,I}+3K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[g_{1,I}+g_{3,I}]+K_{Q}A_{x}^{2}\delta(t-\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{1,I}+g_{3,I}]=0

(13.62)

\displaystyle-2\frac{\partial g_{1,I}}{\partial t}-2\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{1,R}+2Bd_{x}\delta(t)g_{2,R}+K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[g_{1,R}+3g_{3,R}]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle BA_{x}\delta(t)\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{2,R}]-K_{Q}A_{x}^{2}\delta(t-\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{1,R}-g_{3,R}]=BA_{x}\delta(t)\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.63)

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial g_{2,R}}{\partial t}+Bd_{x}\delta(t)[g_{1,I}+3g_{3,I}]-BA_{x}\delta(t)\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{1,I}-g_{3,I}]=0

(13.64)

\displaystyle-4\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{2,R}+Bd_{x}\delta(t)[g_{1,R}+3g_{3,R}]+4K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)g_{2,R}-BA_{x}\delta(t)\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{1,R}-g_{3,R}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle K_{Q}A_{x}^{2}\delta(t-\tau)\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

(13.67)

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial g_{3,R}}{\partial t}-6\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{3,I}+K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[6g_{3,I}+g_{1,I}]-K_{Q}A_{x}^{2}\delta(t-\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,I}=0

\displaystyle-2\frac{\partial g_{3,I}}{\partial t}-6\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{3,R}+2Bd_{x}\delta(t)g_{2,R}+K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)[6g_{3,R}+g_{1,R}]

\displaystyle-BA_{x}\delta(t)\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{2,R}-K_{Q}A_{x}^{2}\delta(t-\tau)\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,R}=0

We take the Laplace transform of these equations w.r.t time to solve them. We define

G_{k,R}(J_{x},s)={\cal L}[g_{k,R}(J_{x},t)],\;\;\;G_{k,I}(J_{x},s)={\cal L}[g_{k,I}(J_{x},t)]

and using

\int dte^{-st}\delta(t-\tau)g(t)=e^{-s\tau}g(\tau),\;\;\;{\cal L}\frac{\partial g_{k}}{\partial t}=sG_{k}(s),\;\;\;g_{k}(J_{x},t=0)=0

The Laplace transform of Eq.(
13.64
) yields

sG_{2,R}(J_{x},s)=0

(13.68)

which yields identically
G_{2,R}=0
which in turn implies
g_{2,R}(J_{x},t)=0
. Hence we have no contribution at the
2nd harmonic,
g_{2}=0
. With this the remaining equations for
g_{1},g_{3}
simplify to

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial g_{1,R}}{\partial t}-2\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{1,I}+K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)\left[(3\beta_{x}g_{1,I}+A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,I})+(3\beta_{x}g_{3,I}+A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{3,I})\right]=0

(13.73)

\displaystyle-2\frac{\partial g_{1,I}}{\partial t}-2\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{1,R}+K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)\left[(\beta_{x}g_{1,R}-A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,R})+(3\beta_{x}g_{3,R}+A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{3,R})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle B\delta(t)A_{x}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

\displaystyle B\delta(t)\left\{d_{x}[g_{1,R}+3g_{3,R}]-A_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}[g_{1,R}-g_{3,R}]\right\}=K_{Q}A_{x}^{2}\delta(t-\tau)\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

\displaystyle 2\frac{\partial g_{3,R}}{\partial t}-6\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{3,I}+K_{Q}\delta(t-\tau)\left[6\beta_{x}g_{3,I}+\beta_{x}g_{1,I}-A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,I}\right]=0

\displaystyle-2\frac{\partial g_{3,I}}{\partial t}-6\omega_{x}(J_{x})g_{3,R}+K_{Q}\beta_{x}\delta(t-\tau)\left[6\beta_{x}g_{3,R}+\beta_{x}g_{1,R}-A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,R}\right]=0

Laplace transforms of the first of the above two equations yield

\displaystyle 2sG_{1,R}-2\omega_{x}(J_{x})G_{1,I}+K_{Q}e^{-s\tau}[3\beta_{x}g_{1,I}(J_{x},\tau)+A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,I}(J_{x},\tau)]

\displaystyle+K_{Q}e^{-s\tau}[3\beta_{x}g_{3,I}(J_{x},\tau)+A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{3,I}(J_{x},\tau)]=0

\displaystyle-2sG_{1,I}-2\omega_{x}(J_{x})G_{1,R}+K_{Q}e^{-s\tau}[\beta_{x}g_{1,R}(J_{x},\tau)-A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,R}(J_{x},\tau)]

\displaystyle-K_{Q}e^{-s\tau}[3\beta_{x}g_{3,R}(J_{x},\tau)+A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{3,R}(J_{x},\tau)]=BA_{x}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

We have using Eq. (
13.56
), that

A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}g_{3,I}(J_{x},\tau)\\
g_{3,I}(J_{x},\tau)\end{array}\right.=3\beta_{x}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}g_{3,I}(J_{x},\tau)\\
g_{3,I}(J_{x},\tau)\end{array}\right.

Hence

\displaystyle 2sG_{1,R}-2\omega_{x}(J_{x})G_{1,I}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-K_{Q}e^{-s\tau}[3\beta_{x}(g_{1,I}(J_{x},\tau)+2g_{3,I}(J_{x},\tau))-A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,I}(J_{x},\tau)]

\displaystyle-2sG_{1,I}-2\omega_{x}(J_{x})G_{1,R}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-K_{Q}e^{-s\tau}[\beta_{x}g_{1,R}(J_{x},\tau)-A_{x}^{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial J_{x}}g_{1,R}(J_{x},\tau)]+BA_{x}\frac{\partial\psi_{0}}{\partial J_{x}}

14
Theory of Nonlinear Dipole Kick from a different approach

•

Extend the present approach to nonlinear dipole kicks

•

Use the Vlasov equation to find the solution with nonlinear dipole kicks if the approach above
does not work

14.1
Integrating the equations of motion

We assume that the Hamiltonian in the absence of the dipole and quadrupole kicks leads to motion
where the linear action
J
is conserved but the betatron frequency depends on the action. In terms
of the usual canonical coordinates
(x,x^{\prime})
, this Hamiltonian is
\bar{H}(x,x^{\prime})
.

\frac{dx}{ds}=\frac{\partial\bar{H}}{\partial x^{\prime}},\;\;\;\;\frac{dx^{\prime}}{ds}=-\frac{\partial\bar{H}}{\partial x^{\prime}}

Now consider the Hamiltonian
H_{d}
which describes the impulsive dipole kick with angle
\theta
at
t=0
.

\theta=\Delta x^{\prime}\equiv\int\frac{dx^{\prime}}{dt}dt=(\beta_{kin}c)\int\frac{dx^{\prime}}{ds}dt=-(\beta_{kin}c)\int\frac{\partial H_{d}}{\partial x}dt

where we used the longitudinal distance variable
s=\beta_{kin}ct
where
\beta_{kin}
is the kinematic

\beta
. This implies that

H_{d}(x,x^{\prime})=-\theta x\frac{\delta(t)}{\beta_{kin}c}

(14.1)

Next, the quadrupole kick at
t=\tau
leads to a kick
\Delta x^{\prime}=-x/f
and is given by a Hamiltonian

H_{q}
which obeys at time
t=\tau
,

-\frac{x}{f}=\Delta x^{\prime}=-(\beta_{kin}c)\int\frac{\partial H_{q}}{\partial x}dt

This leads to

H_{q}(x,x^{\prime})=\frac{1}{2}\frac{x^{2}}{f}\frac{\delta(t-\tau)}{\beta_{kin}c}

(14.2)

The complete Hamiltonian is
\bar{H}=\bar{H}+H_{d}+H_{q}
. Note that each of these Hamiltonians is
dimensionless.

We now make a canonical transformation from
(x,x^{\prime})
to action angle variables
(J,\phi)
where

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta J}\cos\phi,\;\;\;x^{\prime}=-\sqrt{\frac{2J}{\beta}}[\sin\phi+\alpha\cos\phi]

(14.3)

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\beta}[x^{2}+(\beta x^{\prime}+\alpha x)^{2}],\;\;\;\tan\phi=-\frac{\beta x^{\prime}+\alpha x}{x}

(14.4)

This can be done by a generating function of the type, say
F_{1}
. Then the transformed Hamiltonian
is

\bar{H}\rightarrow H=\bar{H}+\frac{\partial F_{1}}{\partial s}=H_{0}(J)+H_{1}(J,\phi)+H_{d}+H_{q}

The term
H_{1}(J,\phi)
drives resonances but we assume in the following discussion that we are
sufficiently far from resonances that this term has a negligible impact and can be dropped.
The Hamiltonian we consider in the following has the form

H(J,\phi)=H_{0}(J)-\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J}\cos\phi\frac{\delta(t)}{\beta_{kin}c}+\frac{\beta_{Q}}{f}J\cos^{2}\phi\frac{\delta(t-\tau)}{\beta_{kin}c}

(14.5)

I have used
\beta_{K}
(beta function at dipole kicker) with the
\theta
term and defined
q=\beta_{Q}/f
.
The equations of motion are

\frac{d\phi}{ds}=\frac{\partial H}{\partial J},\;\;\;\frac{dJ}{ds}=-\frac{\partial H}{\partial\phi}

Define

\omega(J)=\beta_{kin}c\frac{\partial H_{0}}{\partial J}

Note that since
H_{0}
is dimensionless, the RHS has the dimensions of frequency.
We have

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(J)-\theta\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{K}}{2J}}\cos\phi\delta(t)+q\cos^{2}\phi\delta(t-\tau)

(14.6)

\displaystyle\frac{dJ}{dt}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J}\sin\phi\delta(t)+qJ\sin 2\phi\delta(t-\tau)

(14.7)

where we set
q=\beta_{Q}/f
.

Integrating the equations of motion from
0<t<\tau
,

\displaystyle J(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J(0)+\int_{0}^{t}dt[-\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J}\sin\phi\delta(t)+qJ\sin 2\phi\delta(t-\tau)]

\displaystyle J(t)

\displaystyle\equiv J_{0+}

\displaystyle=J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta},\;\;\;\;0<t<\tau

(14.8)

\displaystyle\Delta J_{\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J_{i}}\sin\phi_{i}

(14.9)

where we set the initial values
J_{i}=J(0),\phi_{i}=\phi(0)
and we used the fact that

J(0<t<\tau)=J_{0+}
stays constant over this time interval. Note that in the 1st quadrant where
(x,x^{\prime})>0

and provided that
\beta x^{\prime}+\alpha x>0
, we have
\phi<0
and hence
\Delta J_{\theta}<0
, so that
J_{0+}>J_{i}
.

Now integrating the equation for
\phi
, we have over this same time interval

\displaystyle\phi(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{0+})t-\Delta\phi_{\theta},\;\;\;\;0<t<\tau

(14.10)

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{K}}{2J_{i}}}\cos\phi_{i}

(14.11)

Integrating over times
t>\tau
, we have

\displaystyle J(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J(\tau)+qJ(\tau)\sin 2\phi(\tau)=J_{0+}+\Delta J_{q},\;\;\;t>\tau

(14.12)

\displaystyle\Delta J_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle qJ_{0+}\sin 2\phi(\tau)

(14.13)

Again, this is time independent for
t>\tau
, so we set
J_{\tau+}=J(t>\tau)
. For the
\phi
variable
we obtain

\displaystyle\phi(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\tau)+\int_{\tau}^{t}dt[\omega(J)+q\cos^{2}\phi\delta(t-\tau)]=\phi(\tau)+\omega(J_{\tau+})(t-\tau)+\Delta\phi_{q}

(14.14)

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{q}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q\cos^{2}\phi(\tau)

(14.15)

In order to do the phase space integration to find the dipole moment, I need to express

J_{\tau+},\phi(t)
in terms of the initial values
J_{i},\phi_{i}
.
We have

\displaystyle J_{\tau+}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta}+q(J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta})\sin 2\phi(\tau)

(14.16)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta}+q(J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta})\sin 2\left[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{0+})\tau-\Delta\phi_{\theta}\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta}+q(J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta})\sin 2\left[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{0+})\tau\right]

where we dropped the phase correction
\Delta\phi_{\theta}
that does not grow with either
t
or
\tau
.
We now do a Taylor expansion of
\omega(J_{0+})
about
J_{i}
. Keeping terms to first order in
\theta
, we
have

\omega(J_{0+})=\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})

Similarly we do a Taylor expansion to 1st order in
q
,

\displaystyle\omega(J_{\tau+})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(J_{0+})+qJ_{0+}\sin 2\phi(\tau)\omega^{\prime}(J_{0+})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})+q(J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta})\sin 2\left[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{0+})\tau-\Delta\phi_{\theta}\right]

\displaystyle\times\left(\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime\prime}(J_{i})\right)

We drop the phase correction
\Delta\phi_{\theta}
as before and also the correction

\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime\prime}(J_{i})
for reasons of smallness. Later we will explicitly assume that
\omega^{\prime\prime}=0
.

\displaystyle\omega(J_{\tau+})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})+q(J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta})\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\sin 2\left[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{0+})\tau\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})+q(J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta})\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\sin 2\left[\phi_{i}+\left(\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\right)\tau\right]

Using this form, we have

\displaystyle\phi(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(\tau)+\Delta\phi_{q}+(t-\tau)\left\{\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+q(J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta})\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\sin 2\left[\phi_{i}+\left(\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\right)\tau\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{0+})\tau-\Delta\phi_{\theta}+\Delta\phi_{q}+(t-\tau)\left\{\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+q(J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta})\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\sin 2\left[\phi_{i}+\left(\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\right)\tau\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{i}+[\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})]\tau-\Delta\phi_{\theta}+\Delta\phi_{q}+(t-\tau)\left\{\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\right.

\displaystyle\left.+q(J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta})\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\sin 2\left[\phi_{i}+\left(\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\right)\tau\right]\right\}

Both
\Delta\phi_{\theta}
and
\Delta\phi_{q}
are small terms that do not grow with time and will be dropped from here on.
With this approximation,

\displaystyle\phi(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{i}+[\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})]t

(14.18)

\displaystyle+(t-\tau)q(J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta})\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\sin 2\left[\phi_{i}+\left(\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\right)\tau\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{i}+t(\omega(J_{i})-a_{\theta}\sin\phi_{i})+a_{q}(t-\tau)(1-\frac{\Delta J_{\theta}}{J_{i}})\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]

\displaystyle a_{\theta}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J_{i}}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i}),\;\;\;a_{q}=qJ_{i}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})

(14.19)

Note that both
a_{\theta}
and
a_{q}
depend on
J_{i}
and have dimension of
[1/T]
.
In this form, Eq.(
14.18
) shows the dependence of
\phi(t)
on
(J_{i},\phi_{i},t)
.

The time dependent dipole moment is

\langle x(t)\rangle=\int dJd\phi\sqrt{2\beta J(t)}\cos\phi(t)\psi(J,\phi)

(14.20)

By the conservation of phase space, we have

dJd\phi\psi(J,\phi)=dJ_{i}d\phi_{i}\psi_{i}(J_{i})

(14.21)

where the initial distribution function depends only on
J_{i}
. Thus

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta}\int dJ_{i}d\phi\psi_{i}(J_{i})\sqrt{J_{\tau+}(J_{i},\phi_{i})}\cos\phi(J_{i},\phi_{i},t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta}\int dJ_{i}d\phi\psi_{i}(J_{i})\sqrt{J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta}+q(J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta})\sin 2\left(\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{0+})\tau\right)}

\displaystyle\times\cos[\phi_{i}+t(\omega(J_{i})-a_{\theta}\sin\phi_{i})+a_{q}(t-\tau)(1-\frac{\Delta J_{\theta}}{J_{i}})\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]

In arriving at this form, we have dropped the small phase corrections
\Delta\phi_{\theta},\Delta\phi_{q}
but not made
assumptions on the smallness of
\Delta J_{\theta}/J_{i}
. This Eq.(
14.1
) could therefore be useful for

numerical integration
to arrive at a relatively exact result.

To make analytical progress, we have to make further approximations.

Approximation of small
\Delta J_{\theta}/J_{i}
: We now approximate

\displaystyle\sqrt{J_{\tau+}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta}+q(J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta})\sin 2\left(\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{0+})\tau\right)\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{J_{i}}\left[1-\frac{\Delta J_{\theta}}{J_{i}}+q(1-\frac{\Delta J_{\theta}}{J_{i}})\sin 2\left(\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{0+})\tau\right)\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\left[\sqrt{J_{i}}-\frac{1}{2}\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}}\sin\phi_{i}+\frac{1}{2}q\sqrt{J_{i}}(1-\frac{\Delta J_{\theta}}{J_{i}})\sin 2\left(\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{0+})\tau\right)\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\sqrt{J_{i}}-\frac{1}{2}\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}}\sin\phi_{i}+\frac{1}{2}q\sqrt{J_{i}}(1-\frac{\Delta J_{\theta}}{J_{i}})\sin 2\left[\phi_{i}+\left(\omega(J_{i})-\Delta J_{\theta}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\right)\tau\right]

Here we assumed that
\Delta J_{\theta}/J_{i}=\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}/J_{i}}\sin\theta_{i}\ll 1
.

If the above approximation is not a good one, then an alternative could be to instead

\displaystyle\sqrt{J_{\tau+}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta}}\left[1+q\sin 2\left(\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{0+})\tau\right)\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\sqrt{J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta}}\left[1+\frac{1}{2}q\sin 2\left(\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{0+})\tau\right)\right]

The problems is that
\sqrt{J_{i}-\Delta J_{\theta}}=\sqrt{J_{i}-\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J_{i}}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\sin\phi_{i}}
and it’s not clear
how to do the
\phi_{i}
integration.

Returning to the first approximation above, the integration over phase space can be written as

\langle x(t)\rangle=\sqrt{2\beta}\int dJ_{i}\psi_{i}(J_{i})\left[\sqrt{J_{i}}T_{1}-\frac{1}{2}\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}}T_{2}+\frac{1}{2}q\sqrt{J_{i}}T_{3}\right]

(14.23)

where

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi_{i}\cos\phi(t)={\rm Re}\left[\int d\phi_{i}\exp[i\phi(t)]\right]

(14.24)

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \int d\phi_{i}\cos\phi(t)\sin\phi_{i}=\frac{1}{2}\int d\phi_{i}[\sin(\phi(t)+\phi_{i})-\sin(\phi(t)-\phi_{i})]

(14.25)

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi_{i}(1-\frac{\Delta J_{\theta}}{J_{i}})\cos\phi(t)\sin 2\left[\phi_{i}+\left(\omega(J_{i})-\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J_{i}}\sin\phi_{i}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\right)\tau\right]

(14.26)

Hence

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left[e^{i\omega(J_{i})t}\int d\phi_{i}\exp\left(i\{\phi_{i}-a_{\theta}t\sin\phi_{i}\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.+a_{q}(t-\tau)(1-\frac{\Delta J_{\theta}}{J_{i}})\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]\}\right)\right]

We decompose the product

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta J_{\theta}}{J_{i}}\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta\sqrt{\frac{2\beta_{K}}{J_{i}}}\sin\phi_{i}\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2\beta_{K}}{J_{i}}}\left\{\cos[\phi_{i}+2\omega(J_{i})\tau-2a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\cos[3\phi_{i}+2\omega(J_{i})\tau-2a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]\right\}

Define

a_{\theta q}=a_{q}\theta\sqrt{\frac{2\beta_{K}}{J_{i}}}=q\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J_{i}}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})=qa_{\theta}\ll a_{\theta}

(14.27)

Like
a_{\theta},a_{q}
,
a_{\theta q}
also has dimension [1/T]. Hence

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left[e^{i\omega(J_{i})t}\int d\phi_{i}\exp\left(i\left\{\phi_{i}-a_{\theta}t\sin\phi_{i}+a_{q}(t-\tau)\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]\}\right.\right.\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.\left.-\frac{1}{2}a_{\theta q}(t-\tau)\cos[\phi_{i}+2\omega(J_{i})\tau-2a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]+\frac{1}{2}a_{\theta q}(t-\tau)\cos[3\phi_{i}+2\omega(J_{i})\tau-2a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]\right\}\right)\right]

The parameter
a_{\theta q}
is an order of magnitude smaller than the leading parameter

a_{\theta}
, so I drop it in the following. This also implies that the factor

q(1-\Delta J_{\theta}/J_{i})
will be approximated as
q
in the following.

Approximation of dropping
a_{\theta q}

With this approximation, we have

\displaystyle\phi(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{i}+t(\omega(J_{i})-a_{\theta}\sin\phi_{i})+a_{q}(t-\tau)\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]

(14.28)

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left[e^{i\omega(J_{i})t}\int d\phi_{i}\exp\left(i\left\{\phi_{i}-a_{\theta}t\sin\phi_{i}+a_{q}(t-\tau)\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]\}\right\}\right)\right]

(14.29)

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int d\phi_{i}\cos\phi(t)\sin 2\left[\phi_{i}+\left(\omega(J_{i})-\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J_{i}}\sin\phi_{i}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\right)\tau\right]

(14.30)

Now use the expansion

\exp[iz\sin\theta]=\sum_{l=-\infty}^{\infty}J_{l}(z)e^{il\theta}

where
J_{l}
is the Bessel function of order
l
. Hence

\displaystyle e^{-ia_{\theta}t\sin\phi_{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l_{1}}J_{l_{1}}(a_{\theta}t)\exp[-il_{1}\phi_{i}]

\displaystyle e^{ia_{q}(t-\tau)\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l_{2}}J_{l_{2}}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\exp[2il_{2}(\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{l_{2}}\sum_{l_{3}}J_{l_{2}}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{l_{3}}(2l_{2}a_{\theta}\tau)\exp[2il_{2}(\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau]\exp[-il_{3}\phi_{i}]

Combining all terms,

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Re}\left[e^{i\omega(J)t}\sum_{l_{1}}\sum_{l_{2}}\sum_{l_{3}}\exp[2il_{2}\omega(J_{i})\tau]J_{l_{1}}(a_{\theta}t)J_{l_{2}}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{l_{3}}(2l_{2}a_{\theta}\tau)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times\int d\phi_{i}\exp[i\{1-l_{1}+2l_{2}-l_{3}\}\phi_{i}]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi{\rm Re}\left[e^{i\omega(J)t}\sum_{l_{1}}\sum_{l_{2}}\sum_{l_{3}}\exp[2il_{2}\omega(J_{i})\tau]J_{l_{1}}(a_{\theta}t)J_{l_{2}}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{l_{3}}(2l_{2}a_{\theta}\tau)\delta_{1-l_{1}+2l_{2}-l_{3}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi{\rm Re}\left[e^{i\omega(J)t}\sum_{l_{1}}\sum_{l_{2}}\exp[2il_{2}\omega(J_{i})\tau]J_{l_{1}}(a_{\theta}t)J_{l_{2}}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{2l_{2}+1-l_{1}}(2l_{2}a_{\theta}\tau)\right]

Now we use the identity
[
17
]

\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}J_{p\mp k}(u)J_{k}(v)=J_{p}(u\pm v)

Hence

\sum_{l_{1}}J_{l_{1}}(a_{\theta}t)J_{2l_{2}+1-l_{1}}(2l_{2}a_{\theta}\tau)=J_{2l_{2}+1}(a_{\theta}(t+2l_{2}\tau))

which leads to

T_{1}=2\pi{\rm Re}\left[\sum_{l}\exp[i\omega(J)(t+2l\tau)]J_{l}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{2l+1}(a_{\theta}(t+2l\tau))\right]

(14.31)

CHECK
: Consider the expression for
T_{1}
with
a_{\theta}=0=a_{q}
. From the definition of
\phi(t)

it follows that in this case
\phi(t)=\phi_{i}+\omega(I)t
, then the original expression for
T_{1}
yields

T_{1}=\int d\phi_{i}\cos\phi(t)=\int d\phi_{i}\cos[\phi_{i}+\omega(J)t]=0

while if I substitute
a_{\theta}=0=a_{q}
into Eq. (
14.31
), then using that only
J_{l}(0)=0
for all
l\neq 0

and
J_{0}(0)=1
,

T_{1}=2\pi{\rm Re}\left[\sum_{l}\exp[i\omega(J)(t+2l\tau)]J_{l}(0)J_{2l+1}(0)\right]

In order for both Bessel functions not to vanish, we must have
l=0=2l+1
which has no solution. Hence
the integrated result vanishes as it should. This shows that the integrated result in Eq.(
14.31
) is correct in this
simple limiting case.

Consider the three lowest order terms:
l=0,\pm 1

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi{\rm Re}\left[e^{i\omega(J)t}J_{0}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{1}(a_{\theta}t)+e^{i\omega(J)(t-2\tau)}J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{1}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right.

(14.32)

\displaystyle\left.+e^{i\omega(J)(t+2\tau)}J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{3}(a_{\theta}(t+2\tau))+...\right]

where we used the relation
J_{-n}(z)=(-1)^{n}J_{n}(z)
to reverse the index on the
l=-1
factors.

The complete contribution to the dipole moment from this term is

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle_{T_{1}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta}\int dJ_{i}\psi_{i}(J_{i})\sqrt{J_{i}}T_{1}

(14.34)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2\beta}}{J_{0}}{\rm Re}\left[\int dJ_{i}e^{-J_{i}/J_{0}}\sqrt{J_{i}}\right.

\displaystyle\times\left\{e^{i\omega(J)t}J_{0}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{1}(a_{\theta}t)+e^{i\omega(J)(t-2\tau)}J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{1}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left.+e^{i\omega(J)(t+2\tau)}J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{3}(a_{\theta}(t+2\tau))+...\right\}\right]

The exponents in the first and third terms never vanish since we have
t>\tau
, while the second
term has a vanishing exponent at
t=2\tau
, the time of the echo. At exactly
t=2\tau
, the second term
vanishes because of the factor
J_{1}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))
. The dominant contribution to the echo at
t
close to
2\tau
is

\langle x(t)\rangle_{T_{1}}(t\approx 2\tau)=\frac{\sqrt{2\beta}}{J_{0}}{\rm Re}\left[\int dJ_{i}e^{-J_{i}/J_{0}}\sqrt{J_{i}}e^{i\omega(J)(t-2\tau)}J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{1}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right]

(14.35)

Linearized approximation for
T_{1}

For small arguments

J_{n}(z)\simeq\frac{(z/2)^{n}}{\Gamma(n+1)}\Rightarrow\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}J_{0}(z)=1,\;\;\;\lim_{z\rightarrow 0}J_{1}(z)=\frac{z}{2},\;\;\;J_{2}(z)=\frac{z^{2}}{8}

Considering only the second term and taking small arguments for both Bessel functions, we have

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle_{T_{1}}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{2\beta}}{J_{0}}{\rm Re}\left\{\int dJ_{i}e^{-J_{i}/J_{0}}\sqrt{J_{i}}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times e^{i\omega(J_{i})(t-2\tau)}\left[\frac{1}{2}\theta\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J_{i}}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})(t-2\tau)\right]\left[\frac{1}{2}qJ_{i}\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})(t-\tau)\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2J_{0}}\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta q(t-2\tau)(t-\tau){\rm Re}\left[\int dJ_{i}e^{-J_{i}/J_{0}}J_{i}^{2}(\omega^{\prime}(J_{i}))^{2}e^{i\omega(J_{i})(t-2\tau)}\right]

Assuming now that

\omega(J)=\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J_{i}

Let as before

\Phi=\omega_{\beta}(t-2\tau),\;\;\;\xi=\omega^{\prime}J_{0}(t-2\tau)

Then using the result

\int dz\;z^{2}\exp[-az]=\frac{2}{a^{3}}

we have with
a=1-i\xi

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle_{T_{1}}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta q(t-2\tau)(t-\tau)(\omega^{\prime}J_{0})^{2}{\rm Re}\left[\frac{e^{i\Phi}}{(1-i\xi)^{3}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta q\xi(\omega^{\prime}J_{0})(t-\tau){\rm Re}\left[\frac{e^{i\Phi}}{(1-i\xi)^{3}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta q\xi(\omega^{\prime}J_{0})(t-\tau)\frac{(1-3\xi^{2})\cos\Phi-\xi(3-\xi^{2})\sin\Phi}{(1+\xi^{2})^{3}}

Letting

\chi=\tan^{-1}[\frac{\xi(3-\xi^{2})}{1-3\xi^{2}}]=3\tan^{--1}\xi

we have in the completely linearized approximation

\langle x(t)\rangle_{T_{1}}=\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta q\frac{\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\xi(t-\tau)}{(1+\xi^{2})^{3/2}}\cos[\Phi+\chi]

(14.36)

This has the features that the moment amplitude vanishes at
t=\tau
and also at
t=2\tau
.

Contribution of
T_{2}

Evaluating the contribution from
T_{2}
. We have

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\omega(J_{i})t}\int d\phi_{i}\left(\exp[i(\phi(t)+\phi_{i})]-\exp[i(\phi(t)-\phi_{i})]\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\omega(J_{i})t}\int d\phi_{i}\exp\left(i\{2\phi_{i}-a_{\theta}t\sin\phi_{i}+a_{q}(t-\tau)\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]\}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-e^{i\omega(J_{i})t}\int d\phi_{i}\exp\left(i\{-a_{\theta}t\sin\phi_{i}+a_{q}(t-\tau)\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]\}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\omega(J)t}\sum_{l_{1}}\sum_{l_{2}}\sum_{l_{3}}\exp[2il_{2}\omega(J_{i})\tau]J_{l_{1}}(a_{\theta}t)J_{l_{2}}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{l_{3}}(2l_{2}a_{\theta}\tau)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times\left(\delta_{2-l_{1}+2l_{2}-l_{3}}-\delta_{-l_{1}+2l_{2}-l_{3}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\omega(J)t}\sum_{l_{1}}\sum_{l_{2}}\exp[2il_{2}\omega(J_{i})\tau]J_{l_{1}}(a_{\theta}t)J_{l_{2}}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left(J_{2l_{2}+2-l_{1}}(2l_{2}a_{\theta}\tau)-J_{2l_{2}-l_{1}}(2l_{2}a_{\theta}\tau)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\omega(J)t}\sum_{l}\exp[2il\omega(J_{i})\tau]J_{l}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left(J_{2l+2}(a_{\theta}(t+2l\tau))-J_{2l}(a_{\theta}(t+2l\tau))\right)\right]

CHECK
: Case when
a_{\theta}=0=a_{q}
. Using the original expression for
T_{2}
with
\phi(t)=\phi_{i}+\omega(J)t
,

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int d\phi_{i}[\sin(\phi(t)+\phi_{i})-\sin(\phi(t)-\phi_{i})]=\frac{1}{2}\int d\phi_{i}[\sin(2\phi_{i}+\omega(J)t)-\sin(\omega(J)t)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\pi\sin(\omega(J)t)

Substituting
a_{\theta}=0=a_{q}
in the sum over Bessel functions above, we have

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\omega(J)t}\sum_{l}\exp[2il\omega(J_{i})\tau]J_{l}(0)\left\{J_{2l+2}(0)-J_{2l}(0)\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\pi{\rm Im}[e^{i\omega(J)t}=-\pi\sin(\omega(J)t)]

where in the second line we used the result that only the second term within the curly braces
\{\}
contributes with
l=0
.
This agrees with the exact integrated result.

Keeping the lowest order terms with
l=0,\pm 1
, the contribution of this term to the dipole moment is

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle_{T2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta\int dJ_{i}\;\psi_{i}(J_{i})T_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\pi\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta{\rm Im}\left[\int dJ_{i}\;\psi_{i}(J_{i})e^{i\omega(J)t}\right.

\displaystyle\times\left(e^{-2i\omega(J_{i})\tau}J_{-1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left\{J_{0}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))-J_{-2}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right\}\right.

\displaystyle+J_{0}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left\{J_{2}(a_{\theta}t)-J_{0}(a_{\theta}t)\right\}

\displaystyle\left.\left.e^{2i\omega(J_{i})\tau}J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left\{J_{4}(a_{\theta}(t+2\tau))-J_{2}(a_{\theta}(t+2\tau))\right\}+\ldots\right)\right]

Considering the terms with phase factor close to zero around
t=2\tau
, we have as an approximation

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle_{T2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\pi\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta{\rm Im}\left[\int dJ_{i}\;\psi_{i}(J_{i})e^{i\omega(J)(t-2\tau)}\right.

(14.37)

\displaystyle\left.\times J_{-1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left\{J_{0}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))-J_{-2}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta{\rm Im}\left[\int dJ_{i}\;\psi_{i}(J_{i})e^{i\omega(J)(t-2\tau)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left\{J_{0}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))-J_{2}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right\}\right]

where in the last line we used the relation
J_{-n}(z)=(-1)^{n}J_{n}(z)
for integer
n
.
Now substitute
z=J_{i}/J_{0}
and
\psi(J_{i})=(1/(2\pi J_{0}))\exp[-z]
, we have

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle_{T2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\Phi}\int dz\;e^{-a_{1}z}\right.

(14.38)

\displaystyle\left.\times J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left\{J_{0}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))-J_{2}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right\}\right]

Consider the approximation of this equation above, replacing

J_{0}(z)\simeq 1-\frac{1}{2}z^{2}\;\;\;J_{1}(z)=\frac{z}{2},\;\;\;J_{z}(z)=\frac{z^{2}}{8}

Then

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle_{T2}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\pi\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta{\rm Im}\left[\int dJ_{i}\;\psi_{i}(J_{i})e^{i\omega(J)(t-2\tau)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times\frac{1}{2}qJ_{i}\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau)\{1-\frac{3}{8}(\theta\omega^{\prime}(t-2\tau))^{2}(2\beta_{K}J_{i})\}\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta q\omega^{\prime}J_{0}(t-\tau){\rm Im}\left[e^{i\Phi}\int dze^{-a_{1}z}z\{1-\frac{3}{4}\frac{\beta_{K}}{J_{0}}(\theta\xi)^{2}z\}\right]

Using the result

\int dzz\exp[-a_{1}z]=\frac{1}{a^{2}}

we have

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle_{T2}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta q\omega^{\prime}J_{0}(t-\tau){\rm Im}\left[e^{i\Phi}\left\{\frac{1}{a_{1}^{2}}-\frac{3}{2}\frac{\beta_{K}}{J_{0}}(\theta\xi)^{2}\frac{2}{a_{1}^{3}}\right\}\right]

(14.39)

Keeping only the leading term,

\langle x(t)\rangle_{T2}\approx\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta q\omega^{\prime}J_{0}(t-\tau){\rm Im}\left[\frac{e^{i\Phi}}{(1-i\xi)^{2}}\right]

(14.40)

This is larger than the approximate
\langle x(t)\rangle_{T1}
term because it does not have the
multiplying factor
\xi
. It also does not agree with the linear result from Chao because the
denominator has
(1-i\xi)^{2}
instead of
(1-i\xi)^{3}
as in Chao.

Consider the
T_{3}
term

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int d\phi_{i}\left[\sin\left(\phi(t)+2\phi_{i}+2\tau(\omega(J_{i})-a_{\theta}\sin\phi_{i})\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.-\sin\left(\phi(t)-2\phi_{i}-2\tau(\omega(J_{i})-a_{\theta}\sin\phi_{i})\right)\right]

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int d\phi_{i}\left[\sin\left(\phi(t)+2\phi_{i}+2\tau(\omega(J_{i})-a_{\theta}\sin\phi_{i})\right)-\sin\left(\phi(t)-2\phi_{i}-2\tau(\omega(J_{i})-a_{\theta}\sin\phi_{i})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\omega(J_{i})(t+2\tau)}\int d\phi_{i}\right.

\displaystyle\times\exp\left(i\left\{3\phi_{i}-a_{\theta}(t+2\tau)\sin\phi_{i}+a_{q}(t-\tau)\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]\right\}\right)

\displaystyle-e^{i\omega(J_{i})(t-2\tau)}\int d\phi_{i}

\displaystyle\left.\times\exp\left(i\left\{-\phi_{i}-a_{\theta}(t-2\tau)\sin\phi_{i}+a_{q}(t-\tau)\sin 2[\phi_{i}+\omega(J_{i})\tau-a_{\theta}\tau\sin\phi_{i}]\right\}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\omega(J_{i})t}\sum_{l_{1}}\sum_{l_{2}}\sum_{l_{3}}\exp[2il_{2}\omega(J_{i})\tau]J_{l_{2}}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{l_{3}}(2l_{2}a_{\theta}\tau)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times\left(e^{i\omega(J_{i})2\tau}J_{l_{1}}(a_{\theta}(t+2\tau))\delta_{3-l_{1}+2l_{2}-l_{3}}-(e^{-i\omega(J_{i})2\tau}J_{l_{1}}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\delta_{-1-l_{1}+2l_{2}-l_{3}})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\omega(J_{i})t}\sum_{l_{1}}\sum_{l_{2}}\exp[2il_{2}\omega(J_{i})\tau]J_{l_{2}}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times\left(e^{i\omega(J_{i})2\tau}J_{l_{1}}(a_{\theta}(t+2\tau))J_{2l_{2}+3-l_{1}}(2l_{2}a_{\theta}\tau)-e^{-i\omega(J_{i})2\tau}J_{l_{1}}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))J_{2l_{2}-1-l_{1}}(2l_{2}a_{\theta}\tau)\right)\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pi{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\omega(J_{i})t}\sum_{l}\exp[2il\omega(J_{i})\tau]J_{l}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times\left(e^{i2\omega(J)\tau}J_{2l+3}(a_{\theta}(t+2(l+1)\tau))-e^{-i2\omega(J)\tau}J_{2l-1}(a_{\theta}(t+2(l-1)\tau))\right)\right]

Now considering the three lowest order terms
l=0,\pm 1
, the contribution to the dipole moment from
T_{3}(1)
is

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle_{T_{3}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}q\sqrt{2\beta}\int dJ_{i}\;\psi_{i}(J_{i})\sqrt{J_{i}}T_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}q\sqrt{2\beta}\int dJ_{i}\;\psi_{i}(J_{i})\sqrt{J_{i}}{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\omega(J_{i})t}\left(\right.\right.

\displaystyle\times e^{-2i\omega(J_{i})\tau}J_{-1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left\{e^{2i\omega(J_{i})\tau}J_{1}(a_{\theta}t)-e^{-2i\omega(J_{i})\tau}J_{-3}(a_{\theta}(t-4\tau))\right\}

\displaystyle+J_{0}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left\{e^{2i\omega(J_{i})\tau}J_{3}(a_{\theta}(t+2\tau))-e^{-2i\omega(J_{i})\tau}J_{-1}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right\}

\displaystyle\left.\left.+e^{2i\omega(J_{i})\tau}J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left\{e^{2i\omega(J_{i})\tau}J_{5}(a_{\theta}(t+4\tau))-e^{-2i\omega(J_{i})\tau}J_{1}(a_{\theta}t)\right\}+\ldots\right)\right]

If we further approximate this by keeping only those phase factors which will be small around
t=2\tau
,
we have the approximate result

\langle x(t)\rangle_{T_{3}}=\frac{\pi}{2}q\sqrt{2\beta}{\rm Im}\left[\int dJ_{i}\;\psi_{i}(J_{i})\sqrt{J_{i}}e^{i\omega(J_{i})(t-2\tau)}J_{0}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{1}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right]

(14.41)

Consider the small amplitude approximation with
J_{0}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\approx 1
,

J_{1}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))=a_{\theta}(t-2\tau)/2
, we have

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle_{T_{3}}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{\pi}{2}q\sqrt{2\beta}\theta(\frac{1}{2}\omega^{\prime}\sqrt{2\beta_{K}}(t-2\tau)){\rm Im}\left[e^{i\Phi}\int dJ_{i}(\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}e^{-J_{i}/J_{0}})e^{i\xi J_{i}/J_{0}}(\sqrt{J_{i}})^{2}\right]

(14.42)

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}J_{0}\theta q\omega^{\prime}(t-2\tau){\rm Im}\left[e^{i\Phi}\int dz\;ze^{-a_{1}z}\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}J_{0}\theta q\omega^{\prime}(t-2\tau){\rm Im}\left[\frac{e^{i\Phi}}{(1-i\xi)^{2}}\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}J_{0}\theta q\omega^{\prime}(t-2\tau)\frac{2\xi\cos\Phi+(1-\xi^{2})\sin\Phi}{(1+\xi^{2})^{2}}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}J_{0}\theta q\omega^{\prime}(t-2\tau)\frac{1}{(1+\xi^{2})}\sin(\Phi+2\tan^{-1}\xi)

In the completely linearized approximation, we have the contributions from
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
as the dipole moment

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle_{lin}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta q\frac{\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\xi(t-\tau)}{(1+\xi^{2})^{3/2}}\cos[\Phi+\chi]

(14.43)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta q\omega^{\prime}J_{0}(t-\tau)\frac{1}{(1+\xi^{2})}\sin(\Phi+2\tan^{-1}\xi)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4}\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta q\omega^{\prime}J_{0}(t-2\tau)\frac{1}{(1+\xi^{2})}\sin(\Phi+2\tan^{-1}\xi)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta q\omega^{\prime}J_{0}\left[\frac{\xi(t-\tau)}{(1+\xi^{2})^{3/2}}\cos[\Phi+\chi]\right.

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{1}{4}(2t-3\tau)\frac{1}{(1+\xi^{2})}\sin(\Phi+2\tan^{-1}\xi)\right]

This is WRONG.

Combining the contributions from
T_{1},T_{2},T3
for the echo around
t=2\tau
, we have

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\sqrt{2\beta}{\rm Re}\left[\int dJ_{i}\psi_{i}(J_{i})\sqrt{J_{i}}e^{i\omega(J_{i})(t-2\tau)}J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{1}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right]

(14.44)

\displaystyle+\pi\sqrt{\beta\beta_{K}}\theta{\rm Im}\left[\int dJ_{i}\;\psi_{i}(J_{i})e^{i\omega(J)(t-2\tau)}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\times J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left\{J_{0}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))-J_{2}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right\}\right]

\displaystyle+\frac{\pi}{2}q\sqrt{2\beta}{\rm Im}\left[\int dJ_{i}\;\psi_{i}(J_{i})\sqrt{J_{i}}e^{i\omega(J_{i})(t-2\tau)}J_{0}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{1}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\pi\sqrt{2\beta}\left\{{\rm Re}\left[\int dJ_{i}\psi_{i}(J_{i})\sqrt{J_{i}}e^{i\omega(J_{i})(t-2\tau)}J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{1}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right]\right.

\displaystyle+\frac{\sqrt{2\beta_{K}}\;\theta}{4}{\rm Im}\left[\int dJ_{i}\;\psi_{i}(J_{i})e^{i\omega(J)(t-2\tau)}J_{1}(a_{q}(t-\tau))\left\{J_{0}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))-J_{2}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right\}\right]

\displaystyle+\left.\frac{q}{2}{\rm Im}\left[\int dJ_{i}\;\psi_{i}(J_{i})\sqrt{J_{i}}e^{i\omega(J_{i})(t-2\tau)}J_{0}(a_{q}(t-\tau))J_{1}(a_{\theta}(t-2\tau))\right]\right\}

With the usual assumption that
\omega(J)=\omega_{\beta}+\omega^{\prime}J
, and using
z=J_{i}/J_{0}
as the independent
variable, we have

\displaystyle\omega(J_{i})(t-2\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{\beta}(t-2\tau)+\omega^{\prime}(t-2\tau)J_{i}=\Phi+\omega^{\prime}(t-2\tau)J_{0}z=\Phi+\xi z

\displaystyle a_{\theta}(t-2\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\theta\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})\sqrt{2\beta_{K}J_{i}}(t-2\tau)=\sqrt{2\beta_{K}}\theta(\xi/J_{0})\sqrt{J_{i}}\equiv a_{2}\sqrt{z},\;\;\;a_{2}=\sqrt{\frac{2\beta_{K}}{J_{0}}}\theta\xi

\displaystyle a_{q}(t-\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle q\omega^{\prime}(J_{i})(t-\tau)J_{i}=q\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau)J_{0}z\equiv a_{3}z,\;\;\;\;a_{3}=q\xi_{1},\;\;\;\xi_{1}=\omega^{\prime}(t-\tau)J_{0}

\displaystyle\psi_{i}(J_{i})e^{i\omega(J_{i})(t-2\tau)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi J_{0}}\exp[i\Phi]\exp[-a_{1}z],\;\;\;a_{1}=1-i\xi

The parameters
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}
are all dimensionless.

We can now rewrite Eq. (
14.44
) in the form, after changing the integration variable to

z=J_{i}/J_{0}
,

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta}\left\{\sqrt{J_{0}}{\rm Re}[e^{i\Phi}I_{1}]+\frac{\sqrt{2\beta_{K}}\;\theta}{4}{\rm Im}[e^{i\Phi}I_{2}]+\frac{q}{2}\sqrt{J_{0}}{\rm Im}[e^{i\Phi}I_{3}]\right\}

(14.45)

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dz\exp(-a_{1}z)\sqrt{z}J_{1}(a_{2}\sqrt{z})J_{1}(a_{3}z)

(14.46)

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dz\exp[-a_{1}z]J_{1}(a_{3}z)[J_{0}(a_{2}\sqrt{z})-J_{2}(a_{2}\sqrt{z})]

(14.47)

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dz\exp(-a_{1}z)\sqrt{z}J_{1}(a_{2}\sqrt{z})J_{0}(a_{3}z)

(14.48)

The approximations made in deriving this result have been in dropping the phase corrections

\Delta\phi_{\theta},\Delta\phi_{q}
and approximating
q(1-\Delta J{\theta}/J_{i})
by
q
.
None of the integrals
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
can be done by Mathematica.
These integrals can of course be evaluated numerically.

One analytic possibility is to expand the Bessel function with
\theta
in
its argument in powers of the argument and integrate term by term. Using

J_{n}(w)=\frac{w^{n}}{2^{n}}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{p}\frac{1}{2^{2p}p!\Gamma(n+p+1)}w^{2p}\equiv\frac{w^{n}}{2^{n}}\sum_{p=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{p}\frac{c_{p}}{\Gamma(n+p+1)}w^{2p}

Then we have

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a_{2}}{2}\int dz\exp(-a_{1}z)J_{1}(a_{3}z)z\sum_{p=0}(-1)^{p}\frac{1}{2^{2p}p!\Gamma(p+2)}a_{2}^{2p}z^{p}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a_{2}}{2}\int dz\exp(-a_{1}z)J_{1}(a_{3}z)z\left[1-\frac{1}{2^{3}}a_{2}^{2}z+\frac{1}{2^{4}\times 2\times 6}a_{2}^{4}z^{2}+\ldots\right]

This can be integrated term by term using

\int dz\exp(-a_{1}z)z^{m}J_{1}(a_{3}z)=\frac{1}{2}{a_{3}}{a_{1}^{m+2}}\Gamma(m+2)\mbox{}_{2}F_{1}\left[\frac{m+2}{2},\frac{m+3}{2},2,-(\frac{a_{3}}{a_{1}})^{2}\right]

where
\mbox{}_{2}F_{1}
is the hypergeometric function. For
m=0,1,2
we have explicitly,

\displaystyle\int dz\exp(-a_{1}z)J_{1}(a_{3}z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a_{3}}[1-\frac{a_{1}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{1/2}}]

\displaystyle\int dz\exp(-a_{1}z)zJ_{1}(a_{3}z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a_{3}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\int dz\exp(-a_{1}z)z^{2}J_{1}(a_{3}z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{3a_{1}a_{3}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{5/2}}

Writing out the 1st two terms of
I_{1}
explicitly we have

I_{1}=\frac{a_{2}}{2}\left[\frac{a_{3}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{3/2}}-\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{8}\frac{3a_{1}a_{3}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{5/2}}+\ldots\right]

(14.49)

I_{2}
also has to be integrated term by term by expanding
J_{0}(a_{2}\sqrt{z}),J_{2}(a_{2}\sqrt{z})
. First we note
that using the series expansion above, we can write

J_{0}(a_{2}\sqrt{z})-J_{2}(a_{2}\sqrt{z})=1-(c_{1}+\frac{1}{2^{2}\Gamma(3)})a_{2}^{2}z+(\frac{1}{\Gamma(3)}c_{2}+\frac{1}{2^{2}\Gamma(5)}c_{1})a_{2}^{4}z^{2}+\ldots

where as defined above,
c_{1}=1/2^{2}
,
c_{2}=1/2^{5}
etc.
Again, writing out the first two terms in the term by term integration, we have

I_{2}=\frac{1}{a_{3}}(1-\frac{a_{1}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{1/2}})-\frac{3}{8}a_{2}^{2}\frac{a_{3}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{3/2}}+\ldots

(14.50)

In the limit of small
a_{3}
, the first term behaves as

\displaystyle\lim_{a_{3}\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{a_{3}}(1-\frac{a_{1}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{1/2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{a_{3}\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{a_{3}}(1-[1+(\frac{a_{3}}{a_{1}})^{2}]^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a_{3}}[\frac{1}{2}(\frac{a_{3}}{a_{1}})^{2}+{\rm HOTs}]=\frac{a_{3}}{2a_{1}^{2}}+\dots

so it vanishes linearly as
a_{3}\rightarrow 0
.

Finally for
I_{3}
we have

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dz\exp(-a_{1}z)\sqrt{z}J_{0}(a_{3}z)[\frac{a_{2}\sqrt{z}}{2}-\frac{1}{16}a_{2}^{3}z^{3/2}+\ldots]

(14.51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a_{2}}{2}\frac{a_{1}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{3/2}}-\frac{a_{2}^{3}}{16}\frac{2a_{1}^{2}-a_{3}^{2}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{5/2}}

Hence,

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta}\left\{\sqrt{J_{0}}{\rm Re}\left[e^{i\Phi}\frac{a_{2}}{2}\left(\frac{a_{3}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{3/2}}-\frac{a_{2}^{2}}{8}\frac{3a_{1}a_{3}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{5/2}}+\ldots\right)\right]\right.

(14.52)

\displaystyle+\frac{\sqrt{2\beta_{K}}\;\theta}{2}{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\Phi}\left(\frac{1}{a_{3}}(1-\frac{a_{1}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{1/2}})-\frac{3}{8}a_{2}^{2}\frac{a_{3}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{3/2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{q}{2}\sqrt{J_{0}}{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\Phi}\left(\frac{a_{2}}{2}\frac{a_{1}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{3/2}}-\frac{a_{2}^{3}}{16}\frac{2a_{1}^{2}-a_{3}^{2}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{5/2}}\right)\right]\right\}

Keeping only the 1st term in the series from each contribution, we have from the
I_{1}
term,

\displaystyle{\rm Re}[e^{i\Phi}\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{3/2}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a_{2}a_{3}{\rm Re}[e^{i\Phi}\frac{1}{[(1-i\xi)^{2}+a_{3}^{2}]^{3/2}}]

\displaystyle a_{2}a_{3}{\rm Re}[e^{i\Phi}\frac{1}{[1-\xi^{2}-2i\xi+a_{3}^{2}]^{3/2}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{[(1-\xi^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{2}+4\xi^{2}]^{3/2}}{\rm Re}[e^{i\Phi}(1-\xi^{2}+2i\xi+a_{3}^{2})^{3/2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{[(1-\xi^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{2}+4\xi^{2}]^{3/4}}{\rm Re}[e^{i\Phi}(\exp[i\Theta])^{3/2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{[(1-\xi^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{2}+4\xi^{2}]^{3/4}}\cos[\Phi+\frac{3}{2}\Theta]

\displaystyle\Theta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tan^{-1}\left[\frac{2\xi}{1-\xi^{2}+a_{3}^{2}}\right]

Here we used

1-\xi^{2}+2i\xi+a_{3}^{2}=[(1-\xi^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{2}+4\xi^{2}]^{1/2}\exp[i\Theta]

The contribution from the
I_{2}
term is

\displaystyle{\rm Im}[e^{i\Phi}\left(\frac{1}{a_{3}}(1-\frac{a_{1}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{1/2}})\right)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a_{3}}{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\Phi}-e^{i\Phi}\frac{1-i\xi}{[(1-i\xi)^{2}+a_{3}^{2}]^{1/2}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a_{3}}{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\Phi}-\frac{e^{i\Phi}(1-i\xi)e^{i\Theta/2}}{[(1-\xi^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{2}+4\xi^{2}]^{1/4}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{a_{3}}\left[\sin\Phi-\frac{\left\{\sin(\Phi+\Theta/2)-\xi\cos(\Phi+\Theta/2)\right\}}{[(1-\xi^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{2}+4\xi^{2}]^{1/4}}\right]

Similarly the contribution from the 1st term in
I_{3}
is

\displaystyle{\rm Im}\left[e^{i\Phi}\frac{a_{2}}{2}\frac{a_{1}}{(a_{1}^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{3/2}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{a_{2}}{2}\frac{\left\{\sin(\Phi+\Theta/2)-\xi\cos(\Phi+\Theta/2)\right\}}{[(1-\xi^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{2}+4\xi^{2}]^{1/4}}

Combining the leading terms from
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
, we have

\displaystyle\langle x(t\geq\tau)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta}\left\{\sqrt{J_{0}}\left(\frac{a_{2}a_{3}}{[(1-\xi^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{2}+4\xi^{2}]^{3/4}}\cos(\Phi+\frac{3}{2}\Theta)\right)\right.

(14.53)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2\beta_{K}}\theta\left(\frac{1}{a_{3}}\left[\sin\Phi-\frac{\left\{\sin(\Phi+\Theta/2)-\xi\cos(\Phi+\Theta/2)\right\}}{[(1-\xi^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{2}+4\xi^{2}]^{1/4}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle\left.+\frac{1}{2}q\sqrt{J_{0}}\left(\frac{a_{2}}{2}\frac{\left\{\sin(\Phi+\Theta/2)-\xi\cos(\Phi+\Theta/2)\right\}}{[(1-\xi^{2}+a_{3}^{2})^{2}+4\xi^{2}]^{1/4}}\right)\right\}

where
a_{2}=\sqrt{\frac{2\beta_{K}}{J_{0}}}\theta\xi
,
a_{3}=q\xi_{1}
,
\xi=\omega^{\prime}J_{0}(t-2\tau)
,

\xi_{1}=\omega^{\prime}J_{0}(t-\tau)
,
\Phi=\omega_{\beta}(t-2\tau)
,

\Theta=\tan^{-1}[2\xi/(1-\xi^{2}+a_{3}^{2})]
.

Questions

•

At
t=2\tau
, the dipole moment vanishes because
\xi=a_{2}=\Phi=\Theta=0
.
At what time does this moment have its largest value? It is expected to be at
t=2\tau\pm T_{rev}

Evaluate the above perturbative result numerically to find the times at which amplitude is
largest. Compare with Chao’s linearized theory for the time evolution of the echo.

•

At what value of
q
does the amplitude have its largest value?

•

Is there a value of
\theta
at which the amplitude have its largest value or does this
amplitude increase monotonically with
\theta
?

•

Can the integral forms for
I_{1},I_{2},I_{3}
in Eqs. (
14.46
), (
14.47
), (
14.48
) respectively be used to obtain the values of
q,\theta
where the amplitude
reaches maxima?

Problems with the above calculation

•

The expansion for
\sqrt{J_{\tau,+}}
in Eq.() is only valid for small
\theta
such that

\frac{\Delta J_{\theta}}{J_{i}}=\theta\sqrt{\frac{2\beta_{K}}{J_{i}}}\sin\phi_{i}\ll 1

If we write the initial action as
J_{i}=(a_{i}^{2}/2)\epsilon_{0}
where
a_{i}
is an amplitude factor as
in
x_{i}=a_{i}\sigma_{0}
where
\sigma_{0}=\sqrt{\beta\epsilon}
is the initial beam size at the BPM, then

\displaystyle\frac{\Delta J_{\theta}}{J_{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\beta\theta)\sqrt{\frac{4\beta_{K}}{a_{i}^{2}\beta\sigma_{0}^{2}}}\sin\phi_{i}=2\frac{x_{\theta}}{a_{i}\sigma_{o}}\sqrt{\frac{\beta_{K}}{\beta}}\sin\phi_{i}

where
x_{\theta}=\beta\theta
is the amplitude of the centroid at the BPM after the dipole kick.
Assuming as is usually the case
that the two beta functions are comparable, i.e.
\beta_{K}/\beta\simeq O(1)
, we have the requirement
for the expansion to be valid for all
\phi_{i}
is

x_{\theta}\ll\frac{1}{2}a_{i}\sigma_{o}

(14.54)

If we pick
a_{i}=1
for a particle initially at an amplitude
\sigma_{0}
, then the expansion is only
for small enough kick angles
\theta
such that
x_{\theta}\ll\sigma_{0}/2
.

The amplitude of these echoes at later times can be found by identifying the dominant terms with
phase factors vanishing at these times.

15
Echoes in 2D transverse motion

Calculations to be done

•

Nonlinear quad theory (QT) and the moments
\langle x\rangle,\langle y\rangle

•

QT and the 2nd order moments
\langle x^{2}\rangle,\epsilon_{x}
and the quadrupole moment
Q_{2}=\langle x^{2}-y^{2}\rangle
.
Find the decoherence (or damping) time and the initial frequency of ringing oscillations of
Q_{2}

•

Nonlinear dipole and quad theory (DQT) and similar calculations as above.

•

Decoherence time in 2D after the initial dipole kick

•

Can this be used to measure the strength of the coupling? Diffusion rates in
the transverse planes separately, or a mix of the diffusion coefficients

D_{xx},D_{xy},D_{yy}
.

•

Options with a quad kick: kick in the same plane as the dipole, i.e follow with

p_{x}\rightarrow p_{x}+k_{q}x
or in the other plane as
p_{y}\rightarrow p_{y}+k_{q}y
.
In the 1st option, is the echo dynamics in the
x
plane the same as without coupling?
In the 2nd option, does the coupling lead to an echo in
y
and/or in
x
?

If the quad kick is in the complementary plane, the echo may be quite weak.

15.1
RMS Tune Width

Notation needs changing

Initial action

\rho(J_{x},J_{y})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}\epsilon_{x}\epsilon_{y}}\exp[-\frac{J_{x}}{\epsilon_{x}}-\frac{J_{y}}{\epsilon_{y}}]

(15.1)

which is normalized to 1 as

\int dJ_{x}dJ_{y}d\phi_{x}d\phi_{y}\rho(J_{x},J_{y})=1

Tunes as a function of
J_{x},J_{y}

\displaystyle\Delta\nu_{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{xx}J_{x}+\alpha_{xy}J_{y}

\displaystyle\Delta\nu_{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{xy}J_{x}+\alpha_{yy}J_{y}

(15.2)

Mean tune shift

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\nu_{x}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dJ_{x}dJ_{y}d\phi_{x}d\phi_{y}\rho(J_{x},J_{y})\Delta\nu_{x}

(15.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon_{x}\epsilon_{y}}\left(\alpha_{xx}\int dJ_{x}J_{x}\exp[-\frac{J_{x}}{\epsilon_{x}}]\int dJ_{y}\exp[-\frac{J_{y}}{\epsilon_{y}}]+\alpha_{xy}\int dJ_{x}\exp[-\frac{J_{x}}{\epsilon_{x}}]\int J_{y}dJ_{y}\exp[-\frac{J_{y}}{\epsilon_{y}}]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{xx}\epsilon_{x}+\alpha_{xy}\epsilon_{y}

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\nu_{y}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\alpha_{xy}\epsilon_{x}+\alpha_{yy}\epsilon_{y}

(15.4)

And for the second moment

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\nu_{x}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dJ_{x}dJ_{y}d\phi_{x}d\phi_{y}\rho(J_{x},J_{y})(\Delta\nu_{x})^{2}

(15.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\epsilon_{x}\epsilon_{y}}\left(\alpha_{xx}^{2}\int dJ_{x}J_{x}^{2}\exp[-\frac{J_{x}}{\epsilon_{x}}]\int dJ_{y}\exp[-\frac{J_{y}}{\epsilon_{y}}]+\alpha_{xy}^{2}\int dJ_{x}\exp[-\frac{J_{x}}{\epsilon_{x}}]\int J_{y}^{2}dJ_{y}\exp[-\frac{J_{y}}{\epsilon_{y}}]\right.

\displaystyle\left.+2\alpha_{xx}\alpha_{xy}\int dJ_{x}J_{x}\exp[-\frac{J_{x}}{\epsilon_{x}}]\int J_{y}dJ_{y}\exp[-\frac{J_{y}}{\epsilon_{y}}]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\alpha_{xx}^{2}\epsilon_{x}^{2}+2\alpha_{xy}^{2}\epsilon_{y}^{2}+2\alpha_{xx}\alpha_{xy}\epsilon_{x}\epsilon_{y}

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\nu_{y}^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\alpha_{xy}^{2}\epsilon_{x}^{2}+2\alpha_{yy}^{2}\epsilon_{y}^{2}+2\alpha_{xy}\alpha_{yy}\epsilon_{x}\epsilon_{y}

(15.6)

where we used

\int_{0}^{\infty}z\exp[-z]=1,\int_{0}^{\infty}z^{2}\exp[-z]=2

Hence the rms widths of the tune distributions are

\displaystyle\sigma_{\nu_{x}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle[\langle\Delta\nu_{x}^{2}\rangle-\langle\Delta\nu_{x}\rangle^{2}]^{1/2}=[2\alpha_{xx}^{2}\epsilon_{x}^{2}+2\alpha_{xy}^{2}\epsilon_{y}^{2}+2\alpha_{xx}\alpha_{xy}\epsilon_{x}\epsilon_{y}-(\alpha_{xx}\epsilon_{x}+\alpha_{xy}\epsilon_{y})^{2}]^{1/2}

(15.7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\alpha_{xx}^{2}\epsilon_{x}^{2}+\alpha_{xy}^{2}\epsilon_{y}^{2}]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\sigma_{\nu_{y}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\alpha_{xy}^{2}\epsilon_{x}^{2}+\alpha_{yy}^{2}\epsilon_{y}^{2}]^{1/2}

(15.8)

Special case

1.
\epsilon_{x}=\epsilon_{y}=\epsilon
, then

\sigma_{\nu_{x}}=[\alpha_{xx}^{2}+\alpha_{xy}^{2}]^{1/2}\epsilon,\;\;\;\;\sigma_{\nu_{y}}=[\alpha_{xy}^{2}+\alpha_{yy}^{2}]^{1/2}\epsilon

(15.9)

The coupling may be wither due to linear optics or may only arise due to the nonlinear fields. In the latter case
the coupling may only manifest in the dependence of the dependence of the tunes on both the actions.
We will consider the two cases separately, the latter case first.

15.2
Only nonlinear coupling

Follow similar line of argument as in the 1D case

15.2.1
1st moment

The phase space coordinates are

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta_{y}J_{x}}\cos\phi_{x},\;\;\;p_{x}=\beta_{x}x^{\prime}+\alpha_{x}x=-\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\sin\phi_{x}

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{2\beta_{y}J_{y}}\cos\phi_{y},\;\;\;p_{y}=\beta_{y}y^{\prime}+\alpha_{y}y=-\sqrt{2\beta_{y}J_{y}}\sin\phi_{y}

(15.10)

The initial density is uncoupled, so it is legitimate to take the initial density as

\psi_{0}(J_{x},J_{y})=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}\epsilon_{x}\epsilon_{y}}\exp[-\frac{J_{x}}{\epsilon_{x}}-\frac{J_{y}}{\epsilon_{y}}]

(15.11)

which is normalized to 1 as

\int dJ_{x}dJ_{y}d\phi_{x}d\phi_{y}\psi_{0}(J_{x},J_{y})=1

The motion is coupled via the magnetic forces acting on the beam. Now the angular frequencies are
assumed to depend linearly on the actions

\omega_{x}(J_{x},J_{y})=\omega_{x0}+w_{xx}J_{x}+w_{xy}J_{y},\;\;\;\;\omega_{y}(J_{x},J_{y})=\omega_{y0}+w_{xy}J_{x}+w_{yy}J_{y}

(15.12)

Here
\omega_{x0},\omega_{y0}
are the zero amplitude frequencies corresponding to the nominal tunes.

Under the action of a horizontal dipole kick
p_{x}\rightarrow p_{x}-\beta_{K}\theta_{x}
, the DF evolves to 1st order in the kick as

\psi_{1}(J_{x},\phi_{x},J_{y})=\psi_{0}(J_{x},J_{y})+\beta_{K}\theta_{x}\psi_{0,J_{x}}\frac{\partial J_{x}}{\partial p_{x}}=\psi_{0}(J_{x},J_{y})+\beta_{K}\theta_{x}\psi_{0,J_{x}}\sqrt{\frac{2J_{x}}{\beta_{x}}}\sin\phi_{x}

(15.13)

where
\psi_{0,J_{x}}\equiv\partial\psi_{0}/\partial J_{x}
.
This depends only the horizontal phase
\phi_{x}
but not on the vertical phase
\phi_{y}
. At time
t
after the
dipole kick, the DF is

\psi_{2}(J_{x},\phi_{x},J_{y},t)=\psi_{0}(J_{x},J_{y})+\beta_{K}\theta_{x}\psi_{0,J_{x}}\sqrt{\frac{2J_{x}}{\beta_{x}}}\sin(\phi_{x}-\omega_{x}(J_{x},J_{y})t)

(15.14)

At the time
\tau
of the quadrupole kick, the DF is
\psi_{3}(J_{x},\phi_{x},J_{y},\tau)=\psi_{2}(J_{x},\phi_{x},J_{y},t=\tau))
.
A quadrupole kicker of focal length
f
applies kicks to both planes, but with opposite signs, as

\Delta p_{x}=-q_{x}x,\;\;\;\Delta p_{y}=q_{y}y,\;\;\;q_{x}=\frac{\beta_{Q,x}}{f},\;\;\;q_{y}=\frac{\beta_{Q,y}}{f}

(15.15)

The DF following the quad kick is

\psi_{4}(x,p_{x},y,p_{y},\tau)=\psi_{3}(x,p_{x}-\Delta p_{x},y,p_{y}-\Delta p_{y},\tau)=\psi_{3}(x,p_{x}+q_{x}x,y,p_{y}-q_{y}y,\tau)

(15.16)

Following the quad kick, the transformed DF depends on the action and angle variables in both transverse planes.

The action and angle variables in the argument of the DF transform to

\displaystyle J_{x}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\bar{J}_{x}=\frac{1}{2\beta_{x}}[x^{2}+(p_{x}+q_{x}x)^{2}]=J_{x}(1+A_{x}(q_{x},\phi_{x})),\;\;\;\phi_{x}\rightarrow\bar{\phi}_{x}={\rm Arctan}[-\frac{p_{x}+q_{x}x}{x}]={\rm Arctan}[\tan\phi_{x}-q_{x}]

\displaystyle J_{y}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\bar{J}_{y}=\frac{1}{2\beta_{y}}[y^{2}+(p_{y}-q_{y}y)^{2}]=J_{y}(1+A_{y}(q_{y},\phi_{y})),\;\;\;\phi_{y}\rightarrow\bar{\phi}_{y}={\rm Arctan}[-\frac{p_{y}-q_{y}y}{y}]={\rm Arctan}[\tan\phi_{y}+q_{y}]

(15.17)

\displaystyle A_{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x}+q_{x}^{2}\cos^{2}\phi_{x},\;\;\;A_{y}=q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y}+q_{y}^{2}\cos^{2}\phi_{y}

Using Taylor expansions to expand the angle variables in powers of the quad kick as

\displaystyle{\rm Arctan}[\tan\phi_{x}-q_{x}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{x}-q_{x}\cos^{2}\phi_{x}-\frac{1}{4}q_{x}^{2}(\sin 2\phi_{x}+\frac{1}{2}\sin 2\phi_{x})+O(q_{x}^{3})

\displaystyle{\rm Arctan}[\tan\phi_{y}+q_{y}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{y}+q_{y}\cos^{2}\phi_{y}-\frac{1}{4}q_{y}^{2}(\sin 2\phi_{y}+\frac{1}{2}\sin 2\phi_{y})+O(q_{y}^{3})

Keeping terms to 1st order in
q_{x},q_{y}
, we approximate

\displaystyle A_{x}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x},\;\;\;A_{y}\approx q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y}

\displaystyle{\rm Arctan}[\tan\phi_{x}-q_{x}]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\phi_{x}-q_{x}\cos^{2}\phi_{x},\;\;\;{\rm Arctan}[\tan\phi_{y}+q_{y}]\approx\phi_{y}+q_{y}\cos^{2}\phi_{y}

Dropping the term
\psi_{0}
from the DF, the transformed DF is

\displaystyle\psi_{4}(J_{x},\phi_{x},J_{y},\phi_{y},\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta_{x}\psi_{0,J_{x}}\left(J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x}],J_{y}[1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y}]\right)\sqrt{\frac{2J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x}]}{\beta_{x}}}

(15.18)

\displaystyle\times\sin(\phi_{x,-\tau}-q_{x}\cos^{2}\phi_{x})

\displaystyle\phi_{x,-\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{x}-\omega_{x}\left(J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x}],J_{y}[1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y}]\right)

(15.19)

After the quad kick, the DF at time
t
(from the dipole kick) is

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(J_{x},\phi_{x},J_{y},\phi_{y},t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\psi_{4}(J_{x},\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}},J_{y},\phi_{y,-\Delta\phi_{y}})

(15.20)

\displaystyle\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{x}-\Delta\phi_{x},\;\;\;\phi_{y,-\Delta\phi_{y}}=\phi_{y}-\Delta\phi_{y}

(15.21)

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{x}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{x}(J_{x},J_{y})(t-\tau),\;\;\;\Delta\phi_{y}=\omega_{y}(J_{x},J_{y})(t-\tau)

(15.22)

15.2.2
Decoherence time in 2D

We also need to expand the angular betatron frequencies to 1st order in the actions

\displaystyle\omega_{x}\left(J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x}],J_{y}[1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y}]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{x0}+(1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x})\omega_{x,J_{x}}J_{x}+(1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y})\omega_{x,J_{y}}J_{y}

(15.23)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\omega_{x0}+(1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x})w_{xx}J_{x}+(1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y})w_{xy}J_{y}

Here the expansion is exact because of the assumed dependence of the betatron frequencies on the actions.

Under the change
\phi_{x}\rightarrow\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}

\phi_{x,-\tau}\rightarrow\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x},-\tau}\equiv\phi_{x}-\Delta\phi_{x}-[\omega_{x0}+w_{xx}(1-q_{x}\sin 2(\phi_{x}-\Delta\phi_{x}))J_{x}+w_{xy}(1+q_{y}\sin 2(\phi_{y}-\Delta\phi_{y}))J_{y}]\tau

Written out explicitly,

\displaystyle\psi_{0,J_{x}}\left(J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x}],J_{y}[1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y}]\right)

(15.25)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{2}\epsilon_{x}\epsilon_{y}}\frac{[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x}]}{\epsilon_{x}}\exp[-\frac{J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x}]}{\epsilon_{x}}-\frac{J_{y}[1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y}]}{\epsilon_{y}}]

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(J_{x},\phi_{x},J_{y},\phi_{y},t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta_{K}\theta_{x}\psi_{0,J_{x}}\left(J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}],J_{y}[1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y,-\Delta\phi_{y}}]\right)

\displaystyle\times\sqrt{\frac{2J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}]}{\beta_{x}}}\sin(\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x},-\tau}-q_{x}\cos^{2}\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta_{x}}{(2\pi)^{2}\epsilon_{x}^{2}\epsilon_{y}}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}]

\displaystyle\times\exp[-\frac{J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}]}{\epsilon_{x}}-\frac{J_{y}[1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y,-\Delta\phi_{y}}]}{\epsilon_{y}}]

\displaystyle\times\sqrt{\frac{2J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}]}{\beta_{x}}}\sin(\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x},-\tau}-q_{x}\cos^{2}\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}})

Using

\cos^{2}\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}=\frac{1}{2}(1+\cos 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}})

the argument of the last sine function can be written as

\displaystyle\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x},-\tau}-q_{x}\cos^{2}\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{x}-\Delta\phi_{x}-[\omega_{x0}+w_{xx}(1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}})J_{x}+w_{xy}(1+q_{y}\sin 2(\phi_{y,-\Delta\phi_{y}})J_{y}]\tau

\displaystyle-\frac{q_{x}}{2}(1+\cos 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi_{x}-\Delta\phi_{x}-\left[\omega_{x0}+w_{xx}J_{x}+w_{xy}(1+q_{y}\sin 2(\phi_{y,-\Delta\phi_{y}})J_{y}\right]\tau

\displaystyle+q_{x}(w_{xx}\sin 2(\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}J_{x}\tau-\frac{1}{2}\cos 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}-\frac{1}{2})

We can approximate

\displaystyle\sin 2(\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}w_{xx}J_{x}\tau-\frac{1}{2}\cos 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sqrt{(w_{xx}J_{x}\tau)^{2}+\frac{1}{4}}\sin[2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}-{\rm Arctan}(\frac{1}{2w_{xx}J_{x}\tau})]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle w_{xx}J_{x}\tau\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}

where we used
w_{xx}J_{x}\tau\simeq\tau/\tau_{D}\gg 1
.

\displaystyle\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x},-\tau}-q_{x}\cos^{2}\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}})

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\phi_{x}-\Delta\phi_{x}-\left[\omega_{x0}+w_{xx}J_{x}(1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}})+w_{xy}(1+q_{y}\sin 2(\phi_{y,-\Delta\phi_{y}})J_{y}\right]\tau-\frac{1}{2}q_{x}

We expand

\sqrt{[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}]}\approx 1-\frac{1}{2}q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}

\displaystyle\psi_{5}(J_{x},\phi_{x},J_{y},\phi_{y},t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\beta_{K}\theta_{x}}{(2\pi)^{2}\epsilon_{x}^{2}\epsilon_{y}}\sqrt{\frac{2J_{x}}{\beta_{x}}}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}]\exp[-\frac{J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}]}{\epsilon_{x}}-\frac{J_{y}[1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y,-\Delta\phi_{y}}]}{\epsilon_{y}}](1-\frac{1}{2}q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}})

\displaystyle\times\sin\left(\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}-\left[\omega_{x0}+w_{xx}J_{x}(1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}})+w_{xy}(1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y,-\Delta\phi_{y}})J_{y}\right]\tau-\frac{1}{2}q_{x}\right)

The dipole moments are

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int dJ_{x}dJ_{y}d\phi_{x}d\phi_{y}\sqrt{2\beta_{x}J_{x}}\cos\phi_{x}\psi_{5}(J_{x},\phi_{x},J_{y},\phi_{y},t)

(15.27)

\displaystyle\langle y(t)\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\int dJ_{x}dJ_{y}d\phi_{x}d\phi_{y}\sqrt{2\beta_{y}J_{y}}\cos\phi_{y}\psi_{5}(J_{x},\phi_{x},J_{y},\phi_{y},t)

(15.28)

Using the expression for the DF in Eq.(
), we have for the horizontal dipole moment

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2\beta_{K}\theta_{x}}{(2\pi)^{2}\epsilon_{x}^{2}\epsilon_{y}}\int dJ_{x}dJ_{y}d\phi_{x}d\phi_{y}\;J_{x}\cos\phi_{x}

\displaystyle\times[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}]\exp[-\frac{J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}]}{\epsilon_{x}}-\frac{J_{y}[1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y,-\Delta\phi_{y}}]}{\epsilon_{y}}](1-\frac{1}{2}q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}})

\displaystyle\times\sin\left(\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}-\left[\omega_{x0}+w_{xx}J_{x}(1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}})+w_{xy}(1+q_{y}\sin 2\phi_{y,-\Delta\phi_{y}})J_{y}\right]\tau-\frac{1}{2}q_{x}\right)

CHECK: Setting
q_{y}=0=w_{xy}=0=w_{yy}
should reduce this to the 1D expressions found earlier (in PR-AB paper)
In this case

\Delta\phi_{x}=[\omega_{x,0}+w_{xx}J_{x}](t-\tau),\;\;\;\Delta\phi_{y}=\omega_{y,0}(t-\tau)

These variables do not depend on
J_{y}
. The dipole moment simplifies to

\displaystyle\langle x(t)\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2\beta_{K}\theta_{x}}{(2\pi)^{2}\epsilon_{x}^{2}\epsilon_{y}}\int dJ_{x}dJ_{y}d\phi_{x}d\phi_{y}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle J_{x}\cos\phi_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2(\phi_{x}-\Delta\phi_{x})]\exp[-\frac{J_{x}[1-q_{x}\sin 2(\phi_{x}-\Delta\phi_{x})]}{\epsilon_{x}}-\frac{J_{y}}{\epsilon_{y}}](1-\frac{1}{2}q_{x}\sin 2(\phi_{x}-\Delta\phi_{x}))

\displaystyle\times\sin\left(\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}}-\left[\omega_{x0}+w_{xx}J_{x}(1-q_{x}\sin 2\phi_{x,-\Delta\phi_{x}})\right]\tau-\frac{1}{2}q_{x}\right)

This does not depend on
